\title{Finite Excess in Transfinite Nim Multiplication\\
\large Exact reductions, order bounds, and the \(0/1/4\) frontier}
\author{a9lim}
\date{August 2026}

\begin{document}
\maketitle

\begin{abstract}
Conway's field of ordinal nimbers below
$\omega^{\omega^{\omega}}$ is organized by finite Kummer carries
$\alpha_p=\kappa_{f(p)}+m_p$, where $f(p)=\operatorname{ord}_p(2)$ and
$m_p$ is Lenstra's finite excess.  The observed rule is
$m_p\in\{0,1,4\}$.  We prove that this rule is equivalent to four
distinguished finite-field order statements: a primitive-support norm
condition, maximal projective order on the ordinary odd Kummer spines,
primitivity of a Gaussian period on the cubic spine, and a current-factor
condition on the exceptional $2\cdot3^k$ spine.

For each arm we derive exact norm, resultant, character, and polynomial
criteria, together with unconditional order lower bounds that settle all
sufficiently large primary factors relative to explicit regulators.  The
zero arm is proved at the first two power-of-two two-spine levels.  The
ordinary arm is reduced to selected trace and mixed-Jacobi coordinates;
the cubic arm to a selected reciprocal Wendt factor and prescribed-trace
character value; and the exceptional arm to nonvanishing of a selected
cyclotomic antiunit index, equivalently exclusion of a named reciprocal
cubic factor.  We also prove that the principal generic alternatives---
degree, norm, reciprocity, unweighted character products, bounded-support
arguments, and several natural descent schemes---do not determine these
selected values.

Thus the universal $0/1/4$ rule remains conjectural, but its proof frontier
is reduced to four explicit nonvanishing problems.  A separate affine-line
criterion characterizes Lenstra's weaker absolute-boundedness conjecture,
and the exceptional column admits the effective bound
$m_p<16(3^k)^4$.
\end{abstract}

\paragraph{Scope of the results.}
Statements presented as theorems, propositions, lemmas, and corollaries
are proved here or deduced from cited results.  Finite computations are
identified as such and are used for certification and falsification only;
they are not promoted to uniform theorems.  Values imported from the
published A380496 table are source-pinned inputs.  The four universal
order assertions are conjectures throughout.

\tableofcontents

\section{Introduction and main results}\label{sec:introduction}

Conway's nim-multiplication turns the ordinals into a field of
characteristic two.  Below \(\omega^{\omega^\omega}\), the multiplication
law is organized by a Kummer tower.  At an odd prime \(p\), its new carry
has the form
\[
 \alpha_p=\kappa_{f(p)}+m_p,\qquad f(p)=\ord_p(2),
\]
where the transfinite term \(\kappa_{f(p)}\) is structural and the finite
integer \(m_p\) is Lenstra's \emph{excess}.  Lenstra proved general lower
bounds but left absolute boundedness open.  All presently tabulated
values suggest the following much sharper rule.

\begin{conjecture}[the \(0/1/4\) rule]\label{conj:rule}
For every odd prime \(p\),
\[
  m_p=
  \begin{cases}
    0 & \text{if \(\Qset(f(p))\) is not a singleton odd prime-power},\\
    4 & \text{if \(f(p)=2\cdot3^k\) for some \(k\geq1\)},\\
    1 & \text{otherwise}.
  \end{cases}
\]
\end{conjecture}

The principal result of this note is a case-exhaustive reduction of the
conjecture to four explicit order problems.  The notation
\(\Pi_h,T_h,\mathcal L_{r,a},\gamma_k,\Psi_k,M_k\) is developed in the
corresponding arm sections below.

\begin{theorem}[four-arm reduction]\label{thm:rule-four-arms}
Conjecture~\ref{conj:rule} is equivalent to the conjunction of the
following four universal statements.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*),leftmargin=*]
\item[\((Z)\)] For every \(h>1\) for which \(\Qset(h)\) is not a
singleton odd prime-power,
\[
 \Pi_h\mid
 \ord\!\left(
   \Norm_{\F_{2^{d(\kappa_h)}}/\F_{2^h}}(\kappa_h)
 \right).
\]
\item[\((O)\)] For every odd prime \(r\ne3\) and every \(a\geq1\), the
class of \(y_a=\kappa_{r^a}+1\) in the projective quotient
\(G_{r,a}=F_a^\times/F_{a-1}^\times\) has maximal prime-to-\(r\)
order: for every prime \(p\ne r\) dividing
\(\mathcal L_{r,a}\),
\[
 v_p\!\left(\ord(y_aF_{a-1}^\times)\right)
   =v_p(\mathcal L_{r,a}).
\]
\item[\((C)\)] The Gaussian period
\(\gamma_k\in\F_{2^{3^k}}^\times\) is primitive for every \(k\geq1\).
\item[\((D)\)] The current exceptional factor satisfies
\(\Psi_k\mid\ord(M_k)\) for every \(k\geq1\).
\end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
If \(\Qset(h)\) is not a singleton odd prime-power, the conjecture asks
for \(m_p=0\) at every prime with \(f(p)=h\).
Proposition~\ref{prop:support} identifies this exactly with \((Z)\).

If \(\Qset(h)=\{r^a\}\) with \(r\ne3\), then
Proposition~\ref{prop:singleton-projective-spine} partitions the eligible
row primes among the primary divisors of \(\mathcal L_{r,a}\) and
identifies \(m_p=1\) with the equality in \((O)\).

For \(r=3\), one has \(d(\alpha_3)=2\), so the possible residue degrees
are \(3^a\) and \(2\cdot3^a\).  Proposition~\ref{prop:gauss-arm}
identifies the first family with \((C)\).  The unconditional lower bound
\(m_p\geq4\), together with Proposition~\ref{prop:dk-current-level},
identifies the second family with \((D)\).  These cases are disjoint and
exhaust the definition of Conjecture~\ref{conj:rule}.
\end{proof}

The extensive non-determination results below serve one purpose: they
separate these four selected-value problems from statements that follow
only from degree, trace, norm, reciprocity, or generic character-sum
bounds.  They are obstructions to standard proof strategies, not
additional conjectural arms.

\section{Mathematical setup}\label{sec:notation}

\subsection{Tower notation}

For an odd prime $p$:
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*,itemsep=0pt]
\item $f(p)=\ord_p(2)$, the multiplicative order of $2$ modulo $p$;
\item \(d(x)=[\F_2(x):\F_2]\), the least finite-field degree containing
the algebraic nimber \(x\);
\item $\kappa_h$ is the Lenstra/DiMuro tower element indexed by $h$;
\item $\Qset(h)$ is Lenstra's set of prime-power components appearing in
$\kappa_h$;
\item the \emph{excess} $m_p$ is the least finite $m$ such that
\(\kappa_{f(p)}+m\) has no \(p\)-th root in
\(\F_{2^{d(\kappa_{f(p)}+m)}}\);
\item the Kummer carry is $\alpha_p=\kappa_{f(p)}+m_p$.
\end{itemize}
For the ordinal tower, a row with $\Qset(f(p))=\{q\}$ and finite excess
$m$ gives the ordinal sum corresponding to $\kappa_q+m$. Addition of
nimbers is XOR; $\nimmul$ is the nim product.

The implementation boundary, external table provenance, and finite
certifications are collected in Appendix~\ref{sec:provenance}; none is
used as evidence for an unbounded theorem.
\section{The order criterion and structural norm reduction}
\label{sec:norm}

\subsection{The exact criterion}

Throughout, $E$ denotes the degree of the component field containing
$\beta=\kappa_{f(p)}+m$, so $f=f(p)\mid E$ and hence
$p\mid 2^{E}-1$.

\begin{proposition}[power criterion]\label{prop:power}
$\beta\in\F_{2^E}^{\times}$ has no $p$-th root in $\F_{2^E}$
iff $\beta^{(2^E-1)/p}\neq1$. Hence
\[
  m_p=\min\bigl\{m\geq0:\ (\kappa_{f(p)}+m)^{(2^E-1)/p}\neq1\bigr\}.
\]
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
$\F_{2^E}^{\times}$ is cyclic of order $2^E-1$ and $p\mid 2^E-1$; in a
cyclic group of order $N$, $\beta$ is a $p$-th power iff
$\beta^{N/p}=1$.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[order form]\label{cor:order}
If $v_p(2^E-1)=1$, then $\beta$ has no $p$-th root in
$\F_{2^E}$ iff $p\mid\ord(\beta)$, so
$m_p$ is the least $m$ with $p\mid\ord(\kappa_{f(p)}+m)$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{remark}[Wieferich caveat]\label{rem:wieferich}
In general ``no $p$-th root'' is equivalent to
$v_p(\ord\beta)=v_p(2^E-1)$, which collapses to
$p\mid\ord(\beta)$ only when $v_p(2^E-1)=1$. By lifting the exponent,
\[
v_p(2^E-1)=v_p(2^{f(p)}-1)+v_p(E/f(p)).
\]
Thus the hypothesis can fail either at base-$2$ Wieferich-type primes
with $p^2\mid 2^{f(p)}-1$ or when $p\mid E/f(p)$.  The only known
base-$2$ Wieferich primes are $p=1093$ and $p=3511$, both of which lie
inside the contributor's extended A380496 auxiliary range.  Its calculator
uses the exact test of Proposition~\ref{prop:power}, so the reported zeroes
assert full \(p^2\)-primary order; Appendix~\ref{sec:provenance} records
their thinner certificate boundary.  For those rows, and for any row with
$p\mid E/f(p)$, the exact power test (or the full-$p$-part order condition)
must be used. The local certifications of Section~\ref{sec:family} compute
orders exactly and include squarefreeness checks. The research-note
predecessor of this document stated only the order form; the power criterion
is the correct general statement.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Norm reduction}

\begin{proposition}[norm reduction]\label{prop:norm}
For $\beta\in\F_{2^E}^{\times}$ with $f=f(p)\mid E$,
\[
  \beta^{(2^E-1)/p}
  =
  \Norm_{\F_{2^E}/\F_{2^f}}(\beta)^{(2^f-1)/p}.
\]
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
$\Norm_{\F_{2^E}/\F_{2^f}}(\beta)=\beta^{(2^E-1)/(2^f-1)}$, and
$\bigl((2^E-1)/(2^f-1)\bigr)\cdot\bigl((2^f-1)/p\bigr)=(2^E-1)/p$.
\end{proof}

Frobenius conjugacy preserves multiplicative order, and relative norms
are Frobenius invariant.  Consequently each later selected-value test
depends only on the Conway conjugacy class fixed by the irreducible
Kummer steps, not on a choice of root or field presentation.

\section{The zero arm}\label{sec:zero}

\subsection{Primitive-support norm criterion}

For $h>1$, write
\[
  \Pi_h:=\prod_{\substack{\ell\ \mathrm{odd\ prime}\\
                    \ord_\ell(2)=h}}
          \ell^{v_\ell(2^h-1)}.
\]
This is the full prime-power part of $2^h-1$ supported at primes whose
order of $2$ is exactly $h$; using the full valuations, rather than only
the radical, incorporates the Wieferich cases.

\begin{proposition}[exact-order support]\label{prop:support}
Let $x=\kappa_h$, let $E=d(x)$, and put
\[
  T_h=\Norm_{\F_{2^E}/\F_{2^h}}(x).
\]
Then $m_p=0$ for every odd prime $p$ with $f(p)=h$ if and only if
\[
  \Pi_h\mid\ord(T_h).
\]
For an individual such $p$, $m_p=0$ iff
$v_p(\ord T_h)=v_p(2^h-1)$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The definition of $\kappa_h$ gives $h\mid E$.  Proposition~\ref{prop:norm}
with $\beta=x$ identifies the $p$-power test in $\F_{2^E}$ with the
same test on $T_h$ in $\F_{2^h}$.  In the cyclic group
$\F_{2^h}^{\times}$, failure to be a $p$-th power is exactly the presence
of the full $p$-primary part of the group order in $\ord(T_h)$.  Taking
the product over the distinct eligible primes proves the claim.  No
hypothesis on $p\nmid E/h$ is hidden here: if
$c=v_p((2^E-1)/(2^h-1))$, the norm exponent gives
$v_p(\ord T_h)=\max\{0,v_p(\ord x)-c\}$, so equality with
$v_p(2^h-1)$ is equivalent to $v_p(\ord x)=v_p(2^E-1)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[multicomponent Kummer no-collapse]
\label{prop:multicomponent-no-collapse}
Suppose \(\Qset(h)=\{q_1,\ldots,q_t\}\) with \(t\geq2\).
Put \(x_i=\kappa_{q_i}\),
\(x=\kappa_h=\sum_i x_i\), \(E_i=d(x_i)\), \(E=d(x)\), and
\(D=\operatorname{lcm}(E_1,\ldots,E_t)\).  For a prime \(p\) with
\(\ord_p(2)=h\), let
\[
 T_h=\Norm_{\mathbb F_{2^E}/\mathbb F_{2^h}}(x).
\]
Then \(p>h\), \(p\nmid D\), and, in additive notation for
\(\mathbb F_{2^E}^\times/(\mathbb F_{2^E}^\times)^p\),
\[
 [T_h]=\frac Eh[x].                                      \tag{\(\mathsf{MC}_1\)}
\]
The scalar \(E/h\) is nonzero modulo \(p\), so norm reduction is an
isomorphism on the relevant Kummer line.  On the other hand every
\(x_i\) is a \(p\)-th power in the common compositum
\(\mathbb F_{2^D}\).  Thus no product of the individual component
Kummer classes, or of their one-component norms, can produce the
obstruction carried by the additive sum \(x\).

If \(P_h(Y)\in\mathbb F_2[Y]\) is the minimal polynomial of
\(\kappa_h\), then
\[
 m_p=0
 \iff P_h(Y^p)\text{ is irreducible over }\mathbb F_2.    \tag{\(\mathsf{MC}_2\)}
\]
In contrast, every individual component has trivial \(p\)-Kummer
class.  Hence the multicomponent zero arm is a genuinely new family of
additive-resultant/Capelli irreducibility assertions, not a multiplicative
consequence of the singleton components.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Since \(h=\ord_p(2)\) divides \(p-1\), one has \(p>h\); this is
conditional on such a \(p\) and uses no Zsigmondy assertion.  Write
\(q_i=r_i^{a_i}\).  Lenstra gives \(E_i=q_i b_i\) with every prime
divisor of \(b_i\) smaller than \(r_i\).  Since \(r_i\mid h\), every
prime divisor of \(D\) is at most \(h\), so \(p\nmid D\).  Also
\(x\in\mathbb F_{2^D}\), hence \(E\mid D\) and \(p\nmid E/h\).

Writing \(n=E/h\), the norm exponent satisfies
\[
 \frac{2^E-1}{2^h-1}
 =1+2^h+\cdots+2^{(n-1)h}
 \equiv n=\frac Eh\pmod p,
\]
because \(2^h\equiv1\pmod p\).  This proves
\((\mathsf{MC}_1)\); the natural map from the \(p\)-Kummer quotient of
\(\mathbb F_{2^h}\) to that of \(\mathbb F_{2^E}\) is an isomorphism
because the same scalar \(n\) is nonzero modulo \(p\).

For each \(i\), \(q_i\mid h\) gives
\(\kappa_{q_i}\leq\kappa_h\).  If \(h\mid E_i\), the defining
minimality of \(\kappa_h\) gives the reverse inequality, hence
\(\kappa_h=\kappa_{q_i}\), contradicting uniqueness of the
multicomponent expansion.  Thus \(h\nmid E_i\), so
\(p\nmid2^{E_i}-1\).  The \(p\)-power map is consequently an
automorphism of \(\mathbb F_{2^{E_i}}^\times\), and \(x_i\) is a
\(p\)-th power there and hence in \(\mathbb F_{2^D}\).  Because
\(p\nmid D/E\), extension from \(\mathbb F_{2^E}\) to
\(\mathbb F_{2^D}\) is injective on \(p\)-Kummer quotients, so this
comparison loses no class of \(x\).

Finally Capelli's lemma says \(P_h(Y^p)\) is irreducible iff
\(Y^p-x\) is irreducible over \(\mathbb F_{2^E}\).  Since
\(\mu_p\subset\mathbb F_{2^E}\), the latter holds iff
\(x\notin(\mathbb F_{2^E}^\times)^p\), which is exactly \(m_p=0\).
This proves \((\mathsf{MC}_2)\).
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[synchronized additive resultants and the missing phase]
\label{prop:multicomponent-phase}
Put \(Q=2^h\), \(K=\mathbb F_Q\), and suppose
\(p\mid Q-1\).  For \(1\leq i\leq t\), let
\(L_i=\mathbb F_{Q^{r_i}}\) and choose \(u_i\in L_i\) of full degree
\(r_i\) over \(K\).  Set
\[
 R=\operatorname{lcm}(r_1,\ldots,r_t),\qquad
 M=\mathbb F_{Q^R},\qquad u=\sum_i u_i\ne0,
\]
and assume \(p\nmid R\).  Exponents in the following formula are read
modulo the corresponding \(r_i\).  The diagonal translation action on
\[
 \prod_i\mathbb Z/r_i\mathbb Z
\]
has orbit set
\[
 \Omega=
 \left(\prod_i\mathbb Z/r_i\mathbb Z\right)
 \big/\langle(1,\ldots,1)\rangle .
\]
For a representative \(\mathbf d=(d_i)\) of an orbit, define
\[
 F_{\mathbf d}(Z)=
 \prod_{j=0}^{R-1}
 \left(Z+\sum_i u_i^{Q^{j+d_i}}\right).                 \tag{\(\mathsf{MC}_3\)}
\]
Then \(F_{\mathbf d}\in K[Z]\), it depends only on the class of
\(\mathbf d\), and the ordinary componentwise additive composed product
factors as
\[
 \prod_{0\leq j_i<r_i}
 \left(Z+\sum_i u_i^{Q^{j_i}}\right)
 =\prod_{\mathbf d\in\Omega}F_{\mathbf d}(Z).           \tag{\(\mathsf{MC}_4\)}
\]
The distinguished common-embedding sum is the zero phase, and
\[
 F_{\mathbf0}(0)=\Norm_{M/K}(u),
 \qquad
 [F_{\mathbf0}(0)]=R[u]
 \quad\text{in }M^\times/M^{\times p}.                  \tag{\(\mathsf{MC}_5\)}
\]
Consequently
\[
 u\notin M^{\times p}
 \iff F_{\mathbf0}(0)\notin K^{\times p}.              \tag{\(\mathsf{MC}_6\)}
\]

If the \(r_i\) are pairwise coprime, \(\Omega\) is a singleton and the
ordinary additive resultant is the exact obstruction.  Otherwise it gives
only the product of several synchronized Frobenius phases.  Independently
replacing \(u_i\) by \(u_i^{Q^{e_i}}\) preserves every separate minimal
polynomial and merely permutes these phases.  Thus separate component
polynomials do not identify the distinguished factor \(F_{\mathbf0}\).

For the Conway components of
Proposition~\ref{prop:multicomponent-no-collapse}, take
\[
 r_i=\frac{E_i}{\gcd(E_i,h)},\qquad R=\frac Dh,\qquad
 L_i=K(x_i)
     =\mathbb F_{2^{\operatorname{lcm}(h,E_i)}},\qquad
 M=\mathbb F_{2^D}.
\]
Then \(p\nmid R\), and
\[
 F_{\mathbf0}(0)
 =\Norm_{\mathbb F_{2^D}/\mathbb F_{2^h}}(x)
 =T_h^{D/E}.
\]
Since \(p\nmid D/E\), this is exactly the same Kummer obstruction as
\((\mathsf{MC}_1)\), now expressed as one synchronized additive-resultant
factor rather than the product over all relative embeddings.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The diagonal element \((1,\ldots,1)\) has order \(R\), so its action is
free and its orbits partition the tuples \((j_i)\) into sets of size \(R\).
The \(Q\)-Frobenius cycles the factors in
\((\mathsf{MC}_3)\), proving that \(F_{\mathbf d}\) has coefficients in
\(K\); changing the representative translates the index \(j\).  Partitioning
the factors on the left of \((\mathsf{MC}_4)\) by these diagonal orbits proves
the factorization.

For the zero phase, the factors are precisely the \(M/K\)-conjugates of
\(u\), so the constant term is its norm (signs disappear in characteristic
two).  Under the natural inclusion of \(K^\times/K^{\times p}\) into
\(M^\times/M^{\times p}\), the norm exponent is
\[
 1+Q+\cdots+Q^{R-1}\equiv R\pmod p.
\]
The assumption \(p\nmid R\) proves \((\mathsf{MC}_5)\)--
\((\mathsf{MC}_6)\).  The description of \(\Omega\) proves the conditional
pairwise-coprime case, and independent Frobenius shifts translate the
corresponding phase class, proving the information-loss statement.

In the Conway application,
\(K(x_i)=\mathbb F_{2^{\operatorname{lcm}(h,E_i)}}\), whose degree over
\(K=\mathbb F_{2^h}\) is the displayed \(r_i\).  Taking least common
multiples gives \(R=D/h\).  Proposition~\ref{prop:multicomponent-no-collapse}
proves \(p\nmid D\), hence \(p\nmid R\), and norm transitivity gives the last
displayed equality.  It also gives \(p\nmid D/E\), completing the comparison.
No pairwise-coprimality assertion for the actual \(r_i\) is used.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[strict phase no-go]
\label{prop:multicomponent-phase-no-go}
Separate component degrees, minimal polynomials, and trivial
\(p\)-Kummer classes do not determine the Kummer class of an additively
composed sum, even when both possible sums have full compositum degree.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Here is an exact example.  In this proof, hexadecimal bit \(j\) denotes the
coefficient of \(T^j\).  Put
\[
 k=\mathbb F_2[T]/(m),\qquad m=\mathtt{11b}
   =T^8+T^4+T^3+T+1.
\]
Rabin's criterion is certified by
\[
 T^{256}=T\pmod m,\qquad T^{16}+T=\mathtt{5c}\pmod m,
\]
and the exact B\'ezout identity
\[
 \mathtt{13}\,m+\mathtt{51}\,\mathtt{5c}=1,
\]
so \(k=\mathbb F_{256}\).  Let \(K=\mathbb F_4\subset k\), put
\(\gamma=\mathtt{bc}\), so
\(\gamma^2+\gamma+1=0\), and take
\[
 a=\mathtt{0c}=T^3+T^2,\qquad
 b=\mathtt{2e}=T^5+T^3+T^2+T.
\]
Their \(4\)-Frobenius data are
\[
 a^4=\mathtt{b0},\quad a^{16}=a,
\]
\[
 b^4=\mathtt{37},\quad b^{16}=\mathtt{c3},\quad
 b^{64}=\mathtt{67},
\]
so \(a\) and \(b\) have degrees two and four over \(K\), respectively.
Moreover
\[
 a^{85}=b^{85}=1,
\]
and hence both are cubes in \(k^\times\).  The elements \(b\) and \(b^4\)
are \(K\)-conjugate and have the same minimal polynomial; explicitly
\[
 \min_K(a)=U^2+\gamma U+1,
\]
\[
 \min_K(b)=\min_K(b^4)
 =U^4+(\gamma+1)U^3+\gamma U^2+1.
\]
Nevertheless
\[
 a+b=\mathtt{22},\qquad (a+b)^{85}=\gamma\ne1,
\]
whereas
\[
 a+b^4=\mathtt{3b},\qquad (a+b^4)^{85}=1.              \tag{\(\mathsf{MC}_7\)}
\]
Both sums have degree four over \(K\): their \(4\)-Frobenius orbits are
\[
 (\mathtt{22},\mathtt{87},\mathtt{cf},\mathtt{d7}),
 \qquad
 (\mathtt{3b},\mathtt{73},\mathtt{6b},\mathtt{9e}).
\]
Since \(k^\times\) has order \(255\), a nonzero element is a cube exactly
when its \(85\)-th power is one.  Thus the first sum is a noncube and the
second is a cube, although the separate component data are identical.
As \(\mu_3\subset K\), Capelli's lemma says that composing their irreducible
degree-four minimal polynomials with \(X^3\) gives, respectively, an
irreducible and a reducible polynomial.  This proves the claimed strict
no-go.  It is a counterexample to a componentwise inference, not to the
distinguished Conway statement.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[two-component intersection resultant]
\label{cor:multicomponent-intersection-resultant}
In Proposition~\ref{prop:multicomponent-phase}, suppose \(t=2\).
Put
\[
 A=K(u_1)=\mathbb F_{Q^{r_1}},\qquad
 B=K(u_2)=\mathbb F_{Q^{r_2}},\qquad
 g=\gcd(r_1,r_2),\qquad J=A\cap B=\mathbb F_{Q^g},
\]
and let \(A_i(U)\) be the minimal polynomial of \(u_i\) over \(J\).
Then
\[
 \operatorname{Res}_U(A_1(U),A_2(U))
 =\Norm_{AB/J}(u_1+u_2),                                \tag{\(\mathsf{MC}_8\)}
\]
and
\[
 u_1+u_2\notin (AB)^{\times p}
 \iff
 \operatorname{Res}_U(A_1,A_2)\notin J^{\times p}.      \tag{\(\mathsf{MC}_9\)}
\]
Thus the exact positive additive invariant is a single resultant over the
intersection field, whose coefficients retain the relative Frobenius
alignment omitted by the separate \(K\)-polynomials.

Suppose in addition that \(p\nmid\ord(u_1)\ord(u_2)\), as for the Conway
components \(u_i=x_i\), which lie in the original fields
\(\mathbb F_{2^{E_i}}\) with \(p\nmid2^{E_i}-1\).  Then each \(u_i\) is a
\(p\)-th power and
\[
 [u_1+u_2]=\left[1+\frac{u_1}{u_2}\right].              \tag{\(\mathsf{MC}_{10}\)}
\]
Writing \(N=\ord(u_1/u_2)\), one has \(p\nmid N\).  After taking the
Teichm\"uller lift \(u_1/u_2\leftarrow\zeta_N\), the remaining assertion is
exactly nontriviality of the fixed power-residue symbol
\[
 \left(\frac{1+\zeta_N}{\mathfrak P}\right)_p
\]
at the prime above two selected by the common Conway embedding.  Hence the
two-component zero arm is a cyclotomic-unit Artin-symbol problem after the
intersection resultant has removed all generic additive data.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
The extensions \(A/J\) and \(B/J\) have coprime degrees
\(r_1/g\) and \(r_2/g\), so they are linearly disjoint and their pairs of
relative conjugates are exactly the \(AB/J\)-conjugates of \(u_1+u_2\).
Taking their product gives \((\mathsf{MC}_8)\).  Here
\(\mu_p\subset K\subset J\), and
\[
 [AB:J]=\frac Rg
\]
is prime to \(p\), because \(p\nmid R\).  After inclusion in
\((AB)^\times/(AB)^{\times p}\), the resultant class is therefore
\([AB:J][u_1+u_2]\), proving \((\mathsf{MC}_9)\).

Under the additional order hypothesis, division by the \(p\)-th power
\(u_2\) gives \((\mathsf{MC}_{10})\), and the ratio has order prime to
\(p\).  The standard definition of the
power-residue symbol identifies nontriviality of the residue class
\(1+u_1/u_2\) with the displayed symbol of its Teichm\"uller lift.  This proves
the final formulation.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[a power-of-two two-spine family has primitive support]
\label{prop:fermat-two-spine-support}
Let \(r\) be an odd prime for which
\[
 b_r:=d(\alpha_r)=2^t.
\]
Take \(a\geq t+1\), \(b\geq1\), and \(h=2^ar^b\).  Then
\[
 \Qset(h)=\{2^a,r^b\},\qquad d(\kappa_h)=h,
 \qquad \Pi_h=\Phi_h(2).                              \tag{\(\mathsf{MC}_{11}\)}
\]
Put \(x=\kappa_{r^b}\), \(c=\kappa_{2^a}\), and \(z=\kappa_h=x+c\),
and define the two maximal proper subfields
\[
 A=\mathbb F_{2^{h/2}},\qquad
 B=\mathbb F_{2^{h/r}},\qquad
 J=A\cap B=\mathbb F_{2^{h/(2r)}}.
\]
The primitive-support quotient is cyclic of order \(\Pi_h\):
\[
 \mathcal T_h
 :=\mathbb F_{2^h}^{\times}/(A^\times B^\times),
 \qquad |\mathcal T_h|=\Phi_h(2)=\Pi_h.              \tag{\(\mathsf{MC}_{12}\)}
\]
The class of \(z\) in \(\mathcal T_h\) is nontrivial.  Consequently
\(\ord(z)\) is divisible by at least one prime \(p\) with
\(\ord_p(2)=h\).  More precisely, the full zero-support assertion at
this \(h\) is equivalent to
\[
 \langle z(A^\times B^\times)\rangle=\mathcal T_h.   \tag{\(\mathsf{MC}_{13}\)}
\]
The full zero-support assertion in particular holds whenever
\(\Phi_h(2)\) is prime.  This gives, for
example, a proof without an order computation of the complete
multicomponent zero arm at \(h=12\) and \(h=24\), whose quotients have
orders \(13\) and \(241\), respectively.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
By the odd Kummer-spine degree formula,
\[
 d(\kappa_{r^b})=b_rr^b=2^tr^b.
\]
Lenstra's component recursion first constructs
\(\Qset(r^b)=\{r^b\}\), and then adjoins the \(2^a\)-component exactly
when \(2^a\nmid d(\kappa_{r^b})\).  The hypothesis \(a\geq t+1\)
therefore gives the first assertion in \((\mathsf{MC}_{11})\).  Moreover
\[
 d(\kappa_{2^a})=2^a,\qquad
 \operatorname{lcm}(2^a,2^tr^b)=h.
\]
Thus \(\kappa_h\) lies in \(\mathbb F_{2^h}\); its defining degree is
divisible by \(h\), so \(d(\kappa_h)=h\).

Write \(q=2^{h/(2r)}\).  The standard prime-power substitution identity
for cyclotomic polynomials gives
\[
 \Phi_h(2)=\Phi_{2r}(q)=\frac{q^r+1}{q+1}.
\]
If a prime \(\ell\) divides this value but
\(\ord_\ell(2)<h\), the cyclotomic-divisor criterion forces
\(\ell\mid h\).  The only possible odd exception is therefore \(r\).
The Kummer construction contains \(\mu_r\) in
\(\mathbb F_{2^{b_r}}\), so \(f(r)\mid b_r=2^t\); write
\(f(r)=2^u\) with \(u\leq t\).  The exceptional case would require
\[
 \frac{h}{\ord_r(2)}
 =2^{a-u}r^b
\]
to be a power of \(r\), which it is not because \(a\geq t+1>u\).
Hence every prime divisor of \(\Phi_h(2)\) has exact order \(h\), and
all of its valuation in \(2^h-1\) occurs for the first time in
\(\Phi_h(2)\).  This proves \(\Pi_h=\Phi_h(2)\).

The intersection and compositum rules for finite fields give
\(A\cap B=J\) and \(AB=\mathbb F_{2^h}\).  Since the multiplicative
group of a finite field is cyclic,
\[
 |A^\times B^\times|
 =\operatorname{lcm}(q^r-1,q^2-1)
 =\frac{(q^r-1)(q^2-1)}{q-1}.
\]
Its index in \(q^{2r}-1\) is
\[
 \frac{(q^{2r}-1)(q-1)}{(q^r-1)(q^2-1)}
 =\frac{q^r+1}{q+1}=\Phi_h(2),
\]
which proves \((\mathsf{MC}_{12})\).

It remains to show that \(z\notin A^\times B^\times\).  The degree
formulas and \(a\geq t+1\) give
\[
 x\in A\setminus J,\qquad c\in B\setminus J.
\]
Because \([A:J]=r\) and \([B:J]=2\) are coprime, multiplication induces
an isomorphism
\[
 A\otimes_J B\;\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }\;\mathbb F_{2^h}.
\]
Under it, \(z\) is the tensor
\[
 x\otimes1+1\otimes c.
\]
The pairs \(\{x,1\}\subset A\) and \(\{1,c\}\subset B\) are both
\(J\)-linearly independent, so this tensor has rank two.  Every element
of \(A^\times B^\times\), on the other hand, is the image of a nonzero
rank-one tensor \(u\otimes v\).  Thus \(z\notin A^\times B^\times\),
and its quotient class is nontrivial.

Every prime dividing the order of the quotient class divides \(\Pi_h\),
hence has exact order \(h\).  Conversely no such prime divides
\(|A^\times B^\times|\), so the full \(\Pi_h\)-part of \(\ord(z)\) is
exactly the order of its class in \(\mathcal T_h\).  This proves
\((\mathsf{MC}_{13})\).  If \(\Phi_h(2)\) is prime, nontriviality of the
class already means generation.  Finally
\(\Phi_{12}(2)=13\) and \(\Phi_{24}(2)=241\), so the complete zero arm
holds at those two levels.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[cyclic-variation sieve for the two-spine cross ratio]
\label{prop:two-spine-cross-ratio-sieve}
Retain the hypotheses and notation of
Proposition~\ref{prop:fermat-two-spine-support}.  Put \(q=2^{h/(2r)}\),
\(L_0=(q^r-1)(q+1)\), \(P=z^{L_0}\), and \(c'=c^q\).  The same
Hilbert--90 calculation gives the exact representative
\[
 P
 =\left(\frac{x+c'}{x+c}\right)^{q+1}
 =\frac{(x+c')(\zeta x+c)}
        {(x+c)(\zeta x+c')},                          \tag{\(\mathsf{MC}_{14}\)}
\]
where \(x^q=\zeta x\) and \(\zeta\) is a primitive \(r\)-th root in
\(J\).  Set
\[
 R=\frac{r-1}{2},\qquad
 \mathcal D_R=
 \left\{\mathbf d\in\mathbb Z^r:
   \sum_{j=0}^{r-1}d_j=0,\quad
   \sum_{j=0}^{r-1}|d_j|\leq R\right\},
 \qquad
 B_r^{\mathrm{cyc}}=|\mathcal D_R|.                  \tag{\(\mathsf{MC}_{15}\)}
\]
Then
\[
 \ord(P)
 =\ord\bigl(z(A^\times B^\times)\bigr)
 \geq B_r^{\mathrm{cyc}}.                            \tag{\(\mathsf{MC}_{16}\)}
\]
In particular,
\[
 B_r^{\mathrm{cyc}}
 \geq
 \sum_{\substack{k\geq0\\2k\leq R}}
   \binom r{2k}\binom{2k}k,                          \tag{\(\mathsf{MC}_{17}\)}
\]
so \(B_r^{\mathrm{cyc}}\geq1+r(r-1)\) for \(r\geq5\).
Consequently, if a prime \(p\mid\Phi_h(2)\) satisfies
\[
 p>\frac{\Phi_h(2)}{B_r^{\mathrm{cyc}}},              \tag{\(\mathsf{MC}_{18}\)}
\]
then the class of \(z\) contains the full \(p\)-primary part of the
primitive-support quotient.  This is an unconditional lower bound and
large-prime sieve; it does not prove generation when the quotient is
composite.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The order equality in \((\mathsf{MC}_{16})\) follows first from the
preceding proposition.  Indeed,
\(|A^\times B^\times|=L_0\), and every prime divisor of
\(\Phi_h(2)\) has exact order \(h\) for the base \(2\), so none divides
\(L_0\).  Thus exponentiation by \(L_0\) kills the subgroup
\(A^\times B^\times\) and is an automorphism on the quotient of order
\(\Phi_h(2)\).  Also \(P^{\Phi_h(2)}=z^{|\mathbb F_{2^h}^\times|}=1\),
so \(\langle P\rangle\) intersects \(A^\times B^\times\) trivially;
projection to the quotient is injective on \(\langle P\rangle\).

Index all subscripts modulo \(r\), and put
\[
 f_j=\frac{\zeta^jx+c'}{\zeta^jx+c},
 \qquad P_j=\frac{f_j}{f_{j+1}}.
\]
Since \(c'=c^q\), \(c'{}^q=c\), and \(\zeta\in J=\mathbb F_q\), one has
\[
 f_j^q=f_{j+1}^{-1},
 \qquad P_0=P,
 \qquad P_j=P^{(-q)^j}.                              \tag{\(\mathsf{MC}_{19}\)}
\]
The last identity follows inductively from \(P_j^q=P_{j+1}^{-1}\).

For \(\mathbf d\in\mathcal D_R\), there is a unique cyclic integer
vector \(\mathbf e=(e_j)\) with \(e_0=0\) and
\[
 d_j=e_j-e_{j-1}.
\]
Map \(\mathbf d\) to
\(W_{\mathbf d}=\prod_jP_j^{e_j}\).  By
\((\mathsf{MC}_{19})\), every \(W_{\mathbf d}\) is a power of \(P\).
We show that these powers are distinct.

Suppose \(W_{\mathbf d}=W_{\mathbf d'}\), put
\(g_j=e_j-e_j'\), and let
\(\delta_j=g_j-g_{j-1}=d_j-d_j'\).  Telescoping the \(f_j\)-exponents
gives
\[
 1=\prod_jP_j^{g_j}=\prod_jf_j^{\delta_j}.            \tag{\(\mathsf{MC}_{20}\)}
\]
After clearing denominators, the two sides of
\((\mathsf{MC}_{20})\) are products of the linear polynomials
\[
 \zeta^jX+c,\qquad \zeta^jX+c'\qquad(0\leq j<r)
\]
over \(B\), each of degree
\[
 \sum_j|\delta_j|
 \leq \sum_j|d_j|+\sum_j|d_j'|
 \leq2R=r-1.
\]
Their values agree at \(x\).  Since \([B(x):B]=r\), the two polynomial
products are therefore identical.

The displayed \(2r\) linear factors are pairwise nonassociate.  This is
clear within either family.  A cross-family association would give
\(c'/c\in\mu_r\).  But
\((c'/c)^{q+1}=1\), while \(r\mid q-1\) and \(r\) is odd, so
\(\gcd(r,q+1)=1\); hence it would force \(c'=c\), contrary to
\(c\in B\setminus J\).  Unique factorization now forces
\(\boldsymbol\delta=0\).  Thus \(\mathbf g\) is constant, and
\(g_0=0\) forces \(\mathbf g=0\), proving \(\mathbf d=\mathbf d'\).
This proves the injection \(\mathcal D_R\hookrightarrow\langle P\rangle\)
and hence \((\mathsf{MC}_{16})\).

For \((\mathsf{MC}_{17})\), take the vectors with \(k\) entries equal
to \(1\), \(k\) entries equal to \(-1\), and all other entries zero.
There are \(\binom r{2k}\binom{2k}k\) of them and their
\(\ell^1\)-norm is \(2k\).  Finally, write
\(D=\ord(P)\mid\Phi_h(2)\).  If \(D\) lacked the full \(p\)-primary
part, then \(D\mid\Phi_h(2)/p\), contradicting
\((\mathsf{MC}_{16})\) under \((\mathsf{MC}_{18})\).  This proves the
sieve.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[sharp boundary of the tensor argument]
\label{rem:two-spine-tensor-boundary}
Write more generally
\[
 d(\alpha_r)=2^t c,\qquad c>1\ \text{odd},
\]
and take \(a>t\), \(b\geq1\).  Over
\(J=\mathbb F_{2^{2^t}}\), the fields generated by
\(\kappa_{r^b}\) and \(\kappa_{2^a}\) have coprime relative degrees
\[
 m=cr^b,\qquad n=2^{a-t}.
\]
Tensor rank therefore still proves that their sum is nontrivial in the
product quotient of their compositum.  If \(q=|J|\), however, that
quotient has order
\[
 \frac{(q^{mn}-1)(q-1)}{(q^m-1)(q^n-1)}
 =
 \prod_{\substack{d_1\mid m,\ d_1>1\\
                   d_2\mid n,\ d_2>1}}
       \Phi_{d_1d_2}(q).                              \tag{\(\mathsf{MC}_{21}\)}
\]
The desired \(h=2^ar^b\) support occupies only the factor
\(\Phi_{nr^b}(q)\).  Tensor nontriviality may instead be supported on a
factor whose \(d_1\) uses a nontrivial divisor of \(c\); norming the
degree-\(hc\) compositum down to \(\mathbb F_{2^h}\) kills every such
prime-order component that does not divide \(2^h-1\).  Thus the
rank-two proof does not project to the required zero phase when \(c>1\).
The hypothesis \(d(\alpha_r)=2^t\) is exactly where the present
tensor/divisor argument isolates a single primitive-support factor; the
general case still needs a theorem controlling the synchronized norm.
\end{remark}

\section{Ordinary odd Kummer spines}\label{sec:ordinary}

\subsection{Transverse and projective reductions}

The singleton-odd case admits a sharper field square.  Let $r$ be an odd
prime, $q=r^a$, and set $b_r=d(\alpha_r)$, where
$\kappa_q^q=\alpha_r$.  Since $\alpha_r<\kappa_r$, Lenstra's field
description gives $r\nmid b_r$, while the successive Kummer equations
have degree $r$~\cite[Proposition~1.8]{Lenstra77}; hence
\[
  d(\kappa_q)=qb_r,\qquad \gcd(q,b_r)=1.
\]

\begin{proposition}[singleton transverse-norm criterion]
\label{prop:singleton-transverse}
Suppose $\Qset(h)=\{q\}$ with $q=r^a$ odd.  Then
$h=qs$ for a divisor $s\mid b_r$.  In the field square
\[
  F=\F_{2^{qb_r}},\quad B=\F_{2^{b_r}},\quad
  K=\F_{2^{qs}},\quad C=\F_{2^s},
\]
one has $B\cap K=C$ and $F=BK$.  Put $x=\kappa_q$ and
\[
  \Theta_{q,s}:=\Norm_{F/K}(x+1)\in K^{\times}.
\]
For every prime $p$ with $f(p)=h$:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*),leftmargin=*]
\item $x$ is a $p$-th power in $F$, so $m_p\geq1$;
\item $m_p=1$ iff
\[
  v_p(\ord\Theta_{q,s})=v_p(2^{qs}-1);
\]
\item the easy Kummer norm is
\[
  \Norm_{F/B}(x+1)=1+\alpha_r,
\]
but $p\nmid |B^{\times}|$, so this norm cannot see the required
$p$-primary obstruction.  The norm to $K$ is genuinely transverse.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Lenstra's component theorem gives
$\kappa_h=\sum_{t\in\Qset(h)}\kappa_t$ and, for each component $t$,
$t\mid h$ and $\gcd(t,h/t)=1$
\cite[Corollary~2.2]{Lenstra77}.  Thus the singleton assumption identifies
$\kappa_h=\kappa_q$.  Since $h\mid d(\kappa_h)=d(\kappa_q)=qb_r$, one
has $h=qs$ with $s\mid b_r$.  The
intersection and compositum statements follow from the gcd/lcm rules for
finite subfields and $\gcd(q,b_r)=1$.

Since $p>h\geq r$, one has $p\nmid q$.  Also $f(p)=qs\nmid b_r$, so
$p\nmid2^{b_r}-1$.  The relation $x^q=\alpha_r\in B$ therefore gives
$p\nmid\ord(x)$, proving (i), including the full $p$-primary statement.
Part (ii) is Proposition~\ref{prop:norm} applied to $x+1$.  Finally,
$X^q+\alpha_r$ is the minimal polynomial of $x$ over $B$, and evaluating
it at $1$ gives (iii); the invisibility follows again from
$p\nmid2^{b_r}-1$.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Hilbert--90 cross-ratio for the transverse class]
\label{cor:singleton-cross-ratio}
Retain Proposition~\ref{prop:singleton-transverse}, put
\(y=x+1\), and define
\[
 z=x^{2^{b_r}-1},
 \qquad
 U=y^{2^{b_r}-1}=\frac{zx+1}{x+1}.
\]
Then \(z\) has exact order \(q\), and
\[
 U\in\ker\!\left(\Norm_{F/B}:F^\times\longrightarrow B^\times\right).
\]
For every prime \(p\) with \(f(p)=h=qs\),
\[
 v_p(\ord U)
 =v_p(\ord(x+1))
 =v_p(\ord\Theta_{q,s}).                              \tag{\(\mathsf T\)}
\]
Thus the singleton-one arm is equivalently the assertion that the
explicit Hilbert--90 cross-ratio
\((1+zx)/(1+x)\) contains the full \(p\)-primary part.  In particular,
the large transverse norm may be replaced, for the unresolved primary
component, by one norm-one cyclotomic cross-ratio.  On the
one-dimensional quotient
\[
 \mathcal V=F^\times/(F^\times)^p
\]
the automorphism \(\sigma=\Frob^{b_r}\) acts by
\(\lambda=2^{b_r}\pmod p\), an element of exact order \(q\), and
\[
 [U]=(\lambda-1)[y],\qquad
 [\Theta_{q,s}]=(b_r/s)[y].                           \tag{\(\mathsf T_1\)}
\]
Both displayed scalars are nonzero, whereas
\[
 (1+\sigma+\cdots+\sigma^{q-1})[y]=0.                \tag{\(\mathsf T_2\)}
\]
Finally the same target has the exact translated-composition form
\[
 m_p=1
 \iff
 \bigl((T^p+1)^q+\alpha_r\bigr)
 \text{ is irreducible over }B.                       \tag{\(\mathsf T_3\)}
\]
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
The \(2^{b_r}\)-Frobenius generates
\(\operatorname{Gal}(F/B)\) and sends \(x\) to \(zx\).  Since
\(x^q=\alpha_r\in B\), the class \(xB^\times\) has exact order \(q\):
if \(x^d\in B\) for \(0<d<q\), then \(x\) would satisfy a polynomial
of degree \(d\) over \(B\), contradicting \([B(x):B]=q\).
In the cyclic group \(F^\times\), the map
\[
 F^\times/B^\times\longrightarrow F^\times,\qquad
 gB^\times\longmapsto g^{|B^\times|}
\]
is injective, because \(B^\times\) is the unique subgroup of order
\(|B^\times|=2^{b_r}-1\).  Its value at \(xB^\times\) is \(z\), so
\(z\) has exact order \(q\).  The displayed formula for \(U\) is
immediate, and its form \(y^{\Frob^{b_r}-1}\) gives norm one by
Hilbert 90.

Now \(f(p)=qs\nmid b_r\), so \(p\nmid2^{b_r}-1\).  Exponentiation by
\(2^{b_r}-1\) therefore preserves the \(p\)-primary part of the order
of \(y\), proving the first equality in \((\mathsf T)\).  On the other
hand
\[
 \Theta_{q,s}=y^A,
 \qquad
 A=\frac{2^{qb_r}-1}{2^{qs}-1}
   \equiv\frac{b_r}{s}\pmod p.
\]
Every prime divisor of \(b_r=d(\alpha_r)\) is smaller than \(r\), by
Lenstra's field description and
\(\alpha_r<\kappa_r\)~\cite[Proposition~1.8]{Lenstra77}; since
\(p>h\ge r\), one has \(p\nmid b_r/s\).  Thus \(p\nmid A\), so this
exponentiation also preserves the \(p\)-primary part, proving the
second equality.

The quotient \(\mathcal V\) has order \(p\).  Since
\(\operatorname{ord}_p(2)=qs\), \(b_r=s(b_r/s)\), and
\(\gcd(q,b_r)=1\), the order of \(2^{b_r}\) modulo \(p\) is exactly
\(q\).  The definitions give
\([U]=(\sigma-1)[y]=(\lambda-1)[y]\), while the preceding congruence
for \(A\) gives the second equality in \((\mathsf T_1)\).  The geometric
sum in \((\mathsf T_2)\) vanishes because \(\lambda\) has exact order
\(q\).  This is the representation-theoretic reason that the easy
\(B\)-valued norm sees none of the target class.

The minimal polynomial of \(y=x+1\) over \(B\) is
\((Y+1)^q+\alpha_r\).  Since \(p\) is odd, Capelli's lemma says that
its composition with \(T^p\) is irreducible exactly when \(y\) is not
a \(p\)-th power in \(F\).  Proposition~\ref{prop:singleton-transverse}
identifies this with \(m_p=1\), proving \((\mathsf T_3)\).
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[one projective-order target for each odd Kummer spine]
\label{prop:singleton-projective-spine}
Fix an odd prime \(r\), put \(b=d(\alpha_r)\), and define
\[
 x_0=\alpha_r,\qquad x_a=\kappa_{r^a}\ (a\geq1),\qquad
 y_a=x_a+1,\qquad F_a=\mathbb F_{2^{r^ab}}.
\]
Then, for every \(a\geq1\),
\[
 \Norm_{F_a/F_{a-1}}(y_a)=y_{a-1}.                   \tag{\(\mathsf P_1\)}
\]
The projective quotient
\[
 G_{r,a}=F_a^\times/F_{a-1}^\times
\]
is cyclic of order
\[
 \mathcal L_{r,a}
 =\frac{2^{r^ab}-1}{2^{r^{a-1}b}-1}
 =\Phi_{r^a}(2^b).                                   \tag{\(\mathsf P_2\)}
\]
If \(p\ne r\) is a prime divisor of \(\mathcal L_{r,a}\), then
\[
 \operatorname{ord}_p(2^b)=r^a,\qquad
 f(p)=r^as\quad\text{for some }s\mid b,
 \qquad \Qset(f(p))=\{r^a\}.                          \tag{\(\mathsf P_3\)}
\]
Conversely every prime \(p\) in a singleton row
\(\Qset(f(p))=\{r^a\}\) occurs in this way.  For every such \(p\),
\[
 m_p=1
 \iff
 v_p\!\left(\ord(y_aF_{a-1}^\times)\right)
   =v_p(\mathcal L_{r,a}).                            \tag{\(\mathsf P_4\)}
\]
Consequently every singleton-one test for a fixed \(r\) is a primary-order
test on this one projective class, simultaneously for all residue-degree
divisors \(s\mid b\).  For \(r\ne3\), the \(0/1/4\) rule asks that its
prime-to-\(r\) part be maximal.  For \(r=3\), the two cyclotomic halves
instead split as in the following remark.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The Kummer tower equations are
\(x_1^r=\alpha_r\) and \(x_a^r=x_{a-1}\) for \(a>1\).
The conjugates over \(F_{a-1}\) are \(\xi x_a\), \(\xi\in\mu_r\);
evaluating the corresponding binomial at one gives
\[
 \Norm_{F_a/F_{a-1}}(x_a+1)=x_{a-1}+1,
\]
which proves \((\mathsf P_1)\).  The field degrees give
\((\mathsf P_2)\).

For \(p\ne r\), the standard cyclotomic-divisor criterion applied to
\(p\mid\Phi_{r^a}(2^b)\) gives
\(\operatorname{ord}_p(2^b)=r^a\).  If \(h=f(p)\), then
\[
 \frac{h}{\gcd(h,b)}=r^a.
\]
Since \(h\mid r^ab\), this writes \(h=r^as\) with \(s\mid b\).
Now \(h\mid d(\kappa_{r^a})=r^ab\), so the defining minimality gives
\(\kappa_h\leq\kappa_{r^a}\).  But \(r^a\mid h\), and
\(\kappa_{r^a}\) is the least element whose degree is divisible by
\(r^a\), so the reverse inequality holds.  Hence
\(\kappa_h=\kappa_{r^a}\), and uniqueness in Lenstra's component
theorem gives \(\Qset(h)=\{r^a\}\).
Conversely Proposition~\ref{prop:singleton-transverse}
writes every singleton order as \(h=r^as\), \(s\mid b\), and then
\(\operatorname{ord}_p(2^b)=r^a\), so \(p\mid\mathcal L_{r,a}\).

Such a \(p\) does not divide \(|F_{a-1}^\times|\), because the order
of \(2^b\) modulo \(p\) is \(r^a\).  Its entire primary contribution
to \(|F_a^\times|\) therefore lies in the projective quotient.
The exact power criterion now identifies non-\(p\)-power of \(y_a\),
equivalently \(m_p=1\), with the full \(p\)-primary order of its
projective class.  This proves \((\mathsf P_4)\).
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[exact two-half decomposition of the cubic projective quotient]
\label{prop:cubic-projective-split}
Let \(a\geq1\), put \(h=3^a\), \(\zeta=\kappa_h\),
\(y=\zeta+1\), and
\(\gamma_a=\zeta+\zeta^{-1}\in\mathbb F_{2^h}^\times\).  Then
\[
 \Phi_h(4)=A_aB_a,
 \qquad A_a=\Phi_h(2),\qquad B_a=\Phi_{2h}(2),
\]
with \(\gcd(A_a,B_a)=1\) and \(v_3(B_a)=1\).  The projective order of
\(y\) in
\(\mathbb F_{2^{2h}}^\times/\mathbb F_{2^{2h/3}}^\times\) is
\[
 3\,\frac{\ord(\gamma_a)}
          {\gcd(\ord(\gamma_a),2^{h/3}-1)}.             \tag{\(\mathsf{CPS}\)}
\]
Consequently every prime \(\ell\ne3\) dividing \(B_a\) is absent from
the translate-one projective class, unconditionally; it satisfies
\(\ord_\ell(2)=2h\).  The \(A_a\)-part is full exactly when the new
projective part of \(\gamma_a\) is full, and the all-level assertion is
equivalent to Gaussian-period primitivity \(C_a\).  There is no overlap
between the ordinary and exceptional halves.  The missing
\(B_a/3=\Psi_a\)
part is precisely the translate-four residue-symbol problem \(D'_a\).
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The identity
\(\Phi_{3^a}(X^2)=\Phi_{3^a}(X)\Phi_{2\cdot3^a}(X)\)
gives the factorization.  With \(X=2^{3^{a-1}}\), the two factors are
\(X^2+X+1\) and \(X^2-X+1\); a common odd divisor divides their
difference \(2X\), so the gcd is one.  Since the exponent in \(X\) is
odd, LTE applied to \((X^3+1)/(X+1)\) gives \(v_3(B_a)=1\).

The half-angle identity gives
\[
 \ord(y)=3^{a+1}\ord(\gamma_a),
 \qquad \ord(\gamma_a)\mid2^h-1.
\]
The base group has \(3\)-valuation \(a\), and
\[
 \gcd(2^h-1,2^{2h/3}-1)=2^{h/3}-1.
\]
In a cyclic group, the order modulo its unique base subgroup is the
element order divided by its gcd with the base-group order.  This proves
\((\mathsf{CPS})\).  If \(\ell\ne3\) divides \(B_a\), the
cyclotomic-divisor criterion gives \(\ord_\ell(2)=2h\), so \(\ell\)
divides neither \(3^{a+1}\) nor \(2^h-1\).  The remaining assertions
follow from the two displayed factors and the definitions of \(C_a\)
and \(D'_a\).
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[the first nontrivial split]
At \(a=2\), one has
\(\ord(\gamma_2)=2^9-1=7\cdot73\), and therefore
\[
 \ord\bigl((\kappa_9+1)\mathbb F_{2^6}^\times\bigr)
 =3\cdot73,
 \qquad
 \Phi_9(4)=3\cdot19\cdot73.
\]
Thus the missing prime \(19\) is exactly the exceptional
\(\Phi_{18}(2)/3\) half, not a failure of the projective reduction.
\end{remark}

\subsection{The selected relative cyclotomic unit}

\begin{proposition}[selected-prime cyclotomic Kummer form]
\label{prop:singleton-cyclotomic-kummer}
Fix an odd prime \(r\), put \(b=d(\alpha_r)\) and
\(M_r=\ord(\alpha_r)\), and retain
\(x_a=\kappa_{r^a}\), \(y_a=x_a+1\),
\(F_a=\mathbb F_{2^{r^ab}}\).  Set \(N_a=r^aM_r\).  Then
\[
 \ord(x_a)=N_a,
 \qquad \ord_{N_a}(2)=r^ab.                              \tag{\(\mathsf{CK}_1\)}
\]
Let \(p\ne r\) divide
\(\mathcal L_{r,a}=\Phi_{r^a}(2^b)\), choose a primitive complex
\(N_a\)-th root \(\zeta_{N_a}\), and choose a prime
\(\mathfrak P\mid2\) of
\[
 K_{a,p}=\mathbb Q(\mu_{N_a},\mu_p)
\]
whose residue map sends \(\zeta_{N_a}\) to \(x_a\).  Its residue field
is \(F_a\).  For \(\varepsilon_a=1-\zeta_{N_a}\), the following are
equivalent:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*),leftmargin=*]
\item the class \(y_aF_{a-1}^\times\) has full \(p\)-primary order
\(p^{v_p(\mathcal L_{r,a})}\);
\item \(y_a\notin(F_a^\times)^p\);
\item \(\left(\frac{\varepsilon_a}{\mathfrak P}\right)_p\ne1\);
\item \(\Frob_{\mathfrak P}\) is nontrivial in
\(K_{a,p}(\sqrt[p]{\varepsilon_a})/K_{a,p}\).
\end{enumerate}
Thus, for \(r\ne3\), the predicted maximal prime-to-\(r\) projective
order is exactly a simultaneous nonsplitting assertion at the
Conway-selected primes over two for these cyclotomic \(S\)-unit Kummer
extensions.  If \(N_a\) is not a prime power, \(\varepsilon_a\) is a
global cyclotomic unit; in every case it is a unit at \(\mathfrak P\).
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The binomial \(X^r-\alpha_r\) is irreducible over
\(B=\mathbb F_{2^b}\).  Hence
\(\alpha_r\notin(B^\times)^r\).  In particular \(r\mid2^b-1\), and
cyclicity of \(B^\times\) gives
\[
 v_r(M_r)=v_r(2^b-1).
\]
If an element \(u\) has order \(r^c m\), with \(c\geq1\) and
\(r\nmid m\), and \(v^r=u\), then \(\ord(v)=r^{c+1}m\).
Applying this first to \(x_1^r=\alpha_r\) and then to
\(x_a^r=x_{a-1}\) proves \(\ord(x_a)=r^aM_r\).  Since
\(\mathbb F_2(x_a)=F_a\), the degree of a root of unity of order
\(N_a\) gives \(\ord_{N_a}(2)=r^ab\).

For \(p\ne r\) dividing \(\Phi_{r^a}(2^b)\), the
cyclotomic-divisor criterion gives \(\ord_p(2^b)=r^a\).  Hence \(p\)
divides \(|F_a^\times|\) but not \(|F_{a-1}^\times|\).  Also
\(p\nmid N_a\): if \(p\mid M_r\), then \(\ord_p(2)\mid b\),
contradicting \(\ord_p(2^b)=r^a>1\).  Therefore the entire \(p\)-Sylow
subgroup of the projective quotient comes from \(F_a^\times\), and in
a cyclic group an element has full \(p\)-primary order iff it is not a
\(p\)-th power.  This proves (i)\(\Leftrightarrow\)(ii).

The prime above two exists because \(x_a\) is a primitive
\(N_a\)-th root with degree \(r^ab\).  The completion of
\(\mathbb Q(\mu_{N_a})\) at this prime is the unramified extension of
\(\mathbb Q_2\) of degree \(\ord_{N_a}(2)=r^ab\).  Since
\(\ord_p(2)\mid r^ab\), its residue field already contains \(\mu_p\),
so adjoining \(\mu_p\) does not enlarge that residue field.  Its reduction
sends \(\varepsilon_a\) to \(1+x_a=y_a\).  The usual power-residue
formula gives
\[
 \left(\frac{\varepsilon_a}{\mathfrak P}\right)_p
 =\overline{\varepsilon_a}^{\,(|F_a|-1)/p},
\]
which is nontrivial exactly when the residue is not a \(p\)-th power,
proving (ii)\(\Leftrightarrow\)(iii).  Finally, \(p\) is odd and the
residue characteristic is two, so the \(p\)-power map is an automorphism
on the principal-unit group at \(\mathfrak P\).  The local Kummer
extension is therefore unramified of degree \(p\) when the residue is
not a \(p\)-th power, and split when it is; its Frobenius acts on
\(\sqrt[p]{\varepsilon_a}\) by the displayed residue symbol.  This
proves (iii)\(\Leftrightarrow\)(iv).
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[relative cyclotomic unit at the selected prime]
\label{prop:singleton-relative-unit}
Retain the notation of
Proposition~\ref{prop:singleton-cyclotomic-kummer}, and put
\[
 q_0=2^{r^{a-1}b},\qquad Q=q_0^r=2^{r^ab},\qquad
 N=N_a.
\]
The automorphism
\[
 \sigma(\zeta_N)=\zeta_N^{q_0}
\]
generates
\(\operatorname{Gal}(\mathbb Q(\mu_N)/\mathbb Q(\mu_{N/r}))\).
For \(\varepsilon=1-\zeta_N\), the relative cyclotomic unit
\[
 \rho=\frac{\sigma(\varepsilon)}{\varepsilon}
      =\frac{1-\zeta_N^{q_0}}{1-\zeta_N}                 \tag{\(\mathsf{RU}_1\)}
\]
is a global unit and has relative norm one.  If \(p\ne r\) divides
\(\mathcal L_{r,a}\), extend \(\sigma\) to
\(K_{a,p}=\mathbb Q(\mu_N,\mu_p)\) by fixing \(\mu_p\), and let
\(\mathfrak P\) be the Conway-selected prime of the preceding
proposition.  Then
\[
 \bar\rho=y_a^{q_0-1},\qquad
 \left(\frac{\rho}{\mathfrak P}\right)_p
 =\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{\mathfrak P}\right)_p^{\,q_0-1}.
                                                               \tag{\(\mathsf{RU}_2\)}
\]
The residue class \(\lambda=q_0\pmod p\) has exact order \(r\).
Consequently \(\lambda-1\ne0\), and the four equivalent conditions
of Proposition~\ref{prop:singleton-cyclotomic-kummer} are also
equivalent to
\[
 \left(\frac{\rho}{\mathfrak P}\right)_p\ne1.            \tag{\(\mathsf{RU}_3\)}
\]
Thus the ordinary target is already a residue-symbol problem for an
explicit norm-one \emph{global unit}; the inherited lower-layer factor
has been removed.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Since \(x_a^r=x_{a-1}\in F_{a-1}\), one has
\(q_0\equiv1\pmod{N/r}\).  The \(q_0\)-Frobenius has exact order \(r\)
on \(F_a/F_{a-1}\), so \(q_0\) has exact order \(r\) modulo \(N\).
Moreover \(r\mid M_r\) by the preceding proof, so
\([\mathbb Q(\mu_N):\mathbb Q(\mu_{N/r})]=r\).  This proves the
assertion about \(\sigma\).  The quotient in
\((\mathsf{RU}_1)\) is a global unit: if \(N\) is not a prime power,
both numerator and denominator are cyclotomic units, while if \(N\)
is a prime power their principal ideals are the same unique prime
above the conductor prime.  Telescoping gives
\[
 \Norm_{\mathbb Q(\mu_N)/\mathbb Q(\mu_{N/r})}(\rho)
 =\prod_{j=0}^{r-1}\frac{\sigma^{j+1}(\varepsilon)}
                           {\sigma^j(\varepsilon)}=1.
\]

Reduction at \(\mathfrak P\), in characteristic two, gives
\[
 \bar\rho=\frac{1+x_a^{q_0}}{1+x_a}
          =\frac{(1+x_a)^{q_0}}{1+x_a}=y_a^{q_0-1},
\]
and the power-residue formula gives \((\mathsf{RU}_2)\).
Write \(h=\ord_p(2)=r^as\), where \(s\mid b\), as in
\((\mathsf P_3)\).  Every prime divisor of \(b=d(\alpha_r)\) is
smaller than \(r\), so \(r\nmid b\).  Therefore
\[
 \ord_p(q_0)
 =\frac{r^as}{\gcd(r^as,r^{a-1}b)}=r.
\]
Raising an element of \(\mu_p\) to \(q_0-1\), equivalently to
\(\lambda-1\), is therefore an automorphism.  This proves
\((\mathsf{RU}_3)\).
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[ordinary projective polynomial and binary sections]
\label{prop:ordinary-binary-sections}
Retain Proposition~\ref{prop:singleton-relative-unit}, and write
\[
 F_0=F_{a-1}=\mathbb F_{q_0},\qquad F=F_a,\qquad
 x=x_a,\qquad A=x_{a-1}=x^r,\qquad y=1+x.
\]
Put
\[
 \zeta=x^{q_0-1}\in F_0,\qquad
 \rho=y^{q_0-1}=\frac{1+\zeta x}{1+x},\qquad
 L=\frac{q_0^r-1}{q_0-1}.
\]
Then \(\zeta\) has exact order \(r\), and the monic irreducible
polynomial of \(\rho\) over \(F_0\) is
\[
 H_{r,a}(T)
 =\frac{(T+1)^r+A(T+\zeta)^r}{1+A}.                   \tag{\(\mathsf{OB}_1\)}
\]
In particular,
\[
\begin{aligned}
 H_{r,a}(0)&=1,&
 \operatorname{Tr}_{F/F_0}(\rho)&=\frac{1+A\zeta}{1+A},\\
 H_{r,a}(1)&=\frac{A(1+\zeta)^r}{1+A},&
 H_{r,a}(\zeta)&=\frac{(1+\zeta)^r}{1+A}.
\end{aligned}                                         \tag{\(\mathsf{OB}_2\)}
\]
Also \(\Norm_{F/F_0}(\rho)=1\), so \(H_{r,a}\mid T^L-1\).

Let \(p\ne r\) be a prime divisor of \(L\), and put \(e=L/p\).
The ordinary arm succeeds at \(p\), with its full \(p\)-primary
valuation, exactly when
\[
 H_{r,a}(T)\nmid T^e-1.                               \tag{\(\mathsf{OB}_3\)}
\]
For \(0\leq k<r\), define the binary section polynomial
\[
 S_{e,k}(Z)
 =\sum_{\substack{t\geq0\\k+tr\leq e}}
   \left(\binom e{k+tr}\bmod2\right)Z^t.
\]
Then failure at \(p\) is exactly the simultaneous selected-value
system
\[
 S_{e,k}(A)=0\qquad(1\leq k<r).                       \tag{\(\mathsf{OB}_4\)}
\]

This system descends all the way to the original Conway carry.  Put
\(n=r^{a-1}\), let \(P_{\alpha_r}\) be the minimal polynomial of
\(\alpha_r\) over \(\mathbb F_2\), and, for
\(1\leq c<r^a\) with \(r\nmid c\), define
\[
 R_{e,c}(Z)
 =\sum_{\substack{v\geq0\\c+r^av\leq e}}
   \left(\binom e{c+r^av}\bmod2\right)Z^v.
\]
Then
\[
 \text{failure at }p
 \iff
 P_{\alpha_r}(Z)\mid R_{e,c}(Z)
 \quad\text{for every }c\pmod{r^a}\text{ with }r\nmid c.
                                                               \tag{\(\mathsf{OB}_5\)}
\]
Thus the unresolved inverse-Fourier phase has an equivalent purely
binary form, but it is still a divisibility assertion at the selected
minimal polynomial \(P_{\alpha_r}\), not a generic Kummer-step
identity.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The relative Frobenius sends \(x\) to \(\zeta x\), and its order is
\(r\); hence \(\zeta\in F_0\) has exact order \(r\).  Solving
\[
 T=\frac{1+\zeta x}{1+x}
\]
for \(x\) gives \(x=(T+1)/(T+\zeta)\).  Substitution in \(x^r=A\)
gives \((\mathsf{OB}_1)\).  The inverse Möbius formula shows that
\(F_0(\rho)=F_0(x)=F\), so this degree-\(r\) polynomial is irreducible.
Its constant term, next-to-leading coefficient, and the two displayed
values give \((\mathsf{OB}_2)\).  Telescoping the Frobenius conjugates
of \(y^{q_0-1}\) proves its norm is one.

The prime \(p\) does not divide \(q_0-1\), because
\(\ord_p(q_0)=r\).  Therefore the full \(p\)-primary order of \(\rho\)
equals that of the projective class of \(y\).  In a cyclic group of
order \(L\), an element contains the full \(p\)-primary part exactly
when its \(L/p\)-th power is nonidentity, with no squarefreeness
hypothesis.  This proves \((\mathsf{OB}_3)\).

In the \(F_0\)-basis \(1,x,\ldots,x^{r-1}\), reduction by \(x^r=A\)
gives
\[
 (1+x)^e=\sum_{k=0}^{r-1}x^kS_{e,k}(A).               \tag{\(\mathsf{OB}_6\)}
\]
Now \(\rho^e=1\) exactly when \(y^e\in F_0^\times\).  The nonconstant
coordinates in \((\mathsf{OB}_6)\) therefore prove both directions of
\((\mathsf{OB}_4)\).

Finally \(A^n=\alpha_r\), and
\(1,A,\ldots,A^{n-1}\) is a basis over
\(\mathbb F_{2^b}\).  Writing \(t=u+nv\) in each section gives
\[
 S_{e,k}(A)
 =\sum_{u=0}^{n-1}A^u R_{e,k+ru}(\alpha_r).
\]
As \(k\) and \(u\) vary, the residues \(k+ru\) are exactly the
nonmultiples of \(r\) modulo \(r^a\).  Basis independence, followed
by the minimal-polynomial criterion over \(\mathbb F_2\), proves
\((\mathsf{OB}_5)\).
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[full cyclotomic ancestry of an ordinary carry]
\label{prop:ordinary-full-conductor}
Let \(\beta\) be an element of exact odd order \(M\) over \(\F_2\), put
\(b=\ord_M(2)\), and let \(r\) be an odd prime such that
\[
 v_r(M)=v_r(2^b-1)\geq1.                              \tag{\(\mathsf{FC}_1\)}
\]
For \(R=r^s\), every root \(\theta\) of
\(\theta^R=\beta\) has exact order \(RM\), and
\[
 \ord_{RM}(2)=bR.                                    \tag{\(\mathsf{FC}_2\)}
\]
Consequently, if \(P_\beta\) is the minimal polynomial of \(\beta\),
then
\[
 P_\beta(X^R)\ \text{is irreducible of degree }bR,\qquad
 \Phi_{RM}(X)=\Phi_M(X^R).                           \tag{\(\mathsf{FC}_3\)}
\]

In the ordinary Conway arm, put
\[
 b=d(\alpha_r),\quad M=\ord(\alpha_r),\quad
 R=r^{a-1},\quad C=RM,\quad A=x_{a-1}.
\]
The Kummer carry supplies \((\mathsf{FC}_1)\), and hence
\[
 P_A(X)=P_{\alpha_r}(X^R).                            \tag{\(\mathsf{FC}_4\)}
\]
Thus the failure system in Proposition~\ref{prop:ordinary-binary-sections}
is located at the actual full-conductor factor
\(P_{\alpha_r}(X^R)\mid\overline\Phi_C(X)\).  At the top conductor
\(N=r^aM\), failure at a current prime \(p\) is equivalently
\[
 P_{\alpha_r}(X^{r^a})\mid
 \gcd\!\left(
   \overline\Phi_N(X),
   (1+X)^{(2^{r^ab}-1)/p}-1
 \right).                                             \tag{\(\mathsf{FC}_5\)}
\]
In particular a model with the wrong lower conductor cannot represent
the selected full-conductor factor.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Write \(\ord(\theta)=r^uD\), with \(r\nmid D\).  Since
\(\theta^R=\beta\), comparison of the prime-to-\(r\) parts gives
\(D=M/r^{v_r(M)}\), while the \(r\)-part gives
\(u=s+v_r(M)\).  Hence \(\ord(\theta)=RM\).
LTE and \((\mathsf{FC}_1)\) show that adjoining one further factor
of \(r\) to the modulus multiplies the order of \(2\) by \(r\);
iteration gives \((\mathsf{FC}_2)\).  Therefore every root of
\(P_\beta(X^R)\) has an absolute Frobenius orbit of its full degree
\(bR\), proving irreducibility.  The cyclotomic identity in
\((\mathsf{FC}_3)\) is the standard prime-divisor identity for
\(r\mid M\).

For the Conway carry, the exact \(r\)-valuation is part of the Kummer
construction of \(M=\ord(\alpha_r)\), and \(A^R=\alpha_r\).  The
degree just proved identifies its minimal polynomial, giving
\((\mathsf{FC}_4)\).  Finally \(x_a\) has exact order \(N\) and
minimal polynomial \(P_{\alpha_r}(X^{r^a})\).  Failure says precisely
\((1+x_a)^{(2^{r^ab}-1)/p}=1\); the minimal-polynomial criterion and
the order-\(N\) cyclotomic factor give \((\mathsf{FC}_5)\).
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[singleton dependency-section descent]
\label{prop:ordinary-dependency-descent}
Let \(Q=s^v\) be an odd prime power, with \(s\) an odd prime, and
suppose a singleton Conway
dependency has
\[
 \theta^Q=\alpha_s,\qquad
 \alpha_r=\theta+\varepsilon,qquad \varepsilon\in\F_2,qquad
 [\F_2(\theta):\F_2(\alpha_s)]=Q.                    \tag{\(\mathsf{DD}_1\)}
\]
Then
\[
 P_{\alpha_r}(Z)
 =P_{\alpha_s}\bigl((Z+\varepsilon)^Q\bigr)
 =\operatorname{Res}_Y\!\left(
   P_{\alpha_s}(Y),(Z+\varepsilon)^Q+Y
 \right).                                             \tag{\(\mathsf{DD}_2\)}
\]
Every \(R(Z)\in\F_2[Z]\) has a unique section decomposition
\[
 R(Z)=\sum_{j=0}^{Q-1}(Z+\varepsilon)^j
        R_j\bigl((Z+\varepsilon)^Q\bigr),             \tag{\(\mathsf{DD}_3\)}
\]
and the selected divisibility descends exactly:
\[
 P_{\alpha_r}\mid R
 \iff
 P_{\alpha_s}\mid R_j\quad(0\leq j<Q).              \tag{\(\mathsf{DD}_4\)}
\]
Thus every odd-Kummer singleton \(0/1\)-excess edge satisfying
\((\mathsf{DD}_1)\) can be pushed to its unique predecessor.  At a
two-primary Artin--Schreier node one must instead use its quadratic
ancestry relation.  At a multicomponent or excess-\(4\) node, the
synchronized orbit resultant is essential: separate univariate
compositions discard the phase.
Even along an all-singleton chain, \((\mathsf{DD}_4)\) is an exact
ancestral reformulation, not a degree-forced nondivisibility theorem.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The first two relations in \((\mathsf{DD}_1)\) make the right side of
\((\mathsf{DD}_2)\) vanish at \(\alpha_r\); the degree hypothesis
shows that both monic polynomials have the same degree and proves the
identity.  Grouping the powers of \(Z+\varepsilon\) by their residue
modulo \(Q\) gives the unique decomposition \((\mathsf{DD}_3)\).
After evaluation at \(Z=\alpha_r\), it becomes
\[
 R(\alpha_r)=\sum_{j=0}^{Q-1}\theta^jR_j(\alpha_s).
\]
The elements \(1,\theta,\ldots,\theta^{Q-1}\) are a basis over
\(\F_2(\alpha_s)\), so this value is zero exactly when all the
coefficients vanish.  Applying the two minimal-polynomial criteria
proves \((\mathsf{DD}_4)\).
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[norm compression of the dependency sections]
\label{prop:ordinary-dependency-norm}
Retain Proposition~\ref{prop:ordinary-dependency-descent}.  For
\(R(Z)\in\F_2[Z]\), define
\[
 \mathcal K_{Q,\varepsilon}(R)(Y)
 =\operatorname{Res}_T\!\left(
   T^Q+Y,R(T+\varepsilon)
 \right)\in\F_2[Y].                                  \tag{\(\mathsf{DN}_1\)}
\]
Then
\[
 \mathcal K_{Q,\varepsilon}(R)(\alpha_s)
 =\Norm_{\F_2(\theta)/\F_2(\alpha_s)}
    R(\alpha_r),                                      \tag{\(\mathsf{DN}_2\)}
\]
and hence
\[
 \boxed{
 P_{\alpha_r}\mid R
 \iff
 P_{\alpha_s}\mid\mathcal K_{Q,\varepsilon}(R).}    \tag{\(\mathsf{DN}_3\)}
\]
Thus the \(Q\) simultaneous conditions in
  \((\mathsf{DD}_4)\) may equivalently be compressed to one lower
resultant.  Applied to every actual \(R_{e,c}\) in
\((\mathsf{OB}_5)\), iteration through an all-odd-Kummer singleton
dependency chain ending at \(\F_2\) produces one terminal scalar for each
\(c\).  The
compression stops at the same declared boundaries as the section
descent: a two-primary node uses its quadratic ancestry, while a
multicomponent or excess-\(4\) node retains a synchronized phase.

This norm compression is lossless, not a nonvanishing theorem.  Its
terminal scalar is zero exactly when the original selected binary
section is zero; the norm identity alone forces no leading, constant,
or reciprocal coefficient condition that excludes that event.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
After specializing \(Y=\alpha_s\), the first polynomial in
\((\mathsf{DN}_1)\) is the monic minimal polynomial
\(T^Q+\alpha_s\) of \(\theta\) over \(\F_2(\alpha_s)\).
Its roots are the relative conjugates of \(\theta\), and translation
by \(\varepsilon\in\F_2\) sends them to the conjugates of
\(\alpha_r\).  The product formula for the resultant therefore gives
\((\mathsf{DN}_2)\).  A field norm vanishes exactly when its argument
vanishes.  Applying the two minimal-polynomial criteria proves
\((\mathsf{DN}_3)\), and iteration proves the final assertions.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[the universal order-indicator word]
\label{prop:order-indicator-word}
Let \(D>1\) be odd, and put
\[
 \epsilon_D(e)=
 \begin{cases}
  0,&D\mid e,\\
  1,&D\nmid e.
 \end{cases}
\]
Then \(\epsilon_D\) has least period \(D\), and its generating function over
\(\F_2\) is
\[
 \sum_{e\geq0}\epsilon_D(e)T^e
 =\frac{T+T^2+\cdots+T^{D-1}}{1+T^D}.
                                                               \tag{\(\mathsf{OIW}_1\)}
\]
After cancellation this fraction has denominator
\[
 H_D(T)=1+T+\cdots+T^{D-1}.                           \tag{\(\mathsf{OIW}_2\)}
\]
Consequently the word has exact linear complexity \(D-1\).  Its
\(2\)-kernel, and equivalently its minimal binary DFAO, has exactly \(D\)
states.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The zero set is exactly \(D\mathbb Z_{\geq0}\), which proves the least-period
claim and the geometric-series formula.  A nontrivial \(D\)-th root of unity
is not a root of the numerator: the complete geometric sum vanishes there,
so the sum with the constant term removed is one.  At \(T=1\), the denominator
has a simple root and the numerator vanishes.  Thus their greatest common
divisor is exactly \(T+1\), and the reduced denominator is \(H_D\), proving
the linear-complexity assertion.

Every sequence in the \(2\)-kernel tests one residue class modulo \(D\),
because multiplication by \(2\) is invertible modulo the odd integer \(D\).
All \(D\) residue classes occur once the section depth satisfies \(2^j\geq D\),
and they are distinguished by the location of their next zero.  The same
residue transition \(r\mapsto2r+b\pmod D\) is the minimal DFAO.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[closed ordinary section words and their aggregate]
\label{prop:ordinary-section-word}
Retain Proposition~\ref{prop:ordinary-binary-sections}, and put
\[
 R_0=r^a,\qquad K=\F_2(\alpha_r),\qquad
 u_c(e)=R_{e,c}(\alpha_r)
 \quad(0<c<R_0,\ r\nmid c).
\]
Then, in \(K[[T]]\), every individual selected section has the reduced
rational generating function
\[
 \boxed{
 \sum_{e\geq0}u_c(e)T^e
 =\frac{T^c(1+T)^{R_0-c-1}}
        {(1+T)^{R_0}+\alpha_rT^{R_0}}.}              \tag{\(\mathsf{OSW}_1\)}
\]
Its least period is \(\ord(1+x_a)\).

Let
\[
 t_c(e)=\Norm_{K/\F_2}u_c(e),\qquad
 \mathcal T_{r,a}(e)
 =1+\prod_{\substack{0<c<R_0\\r\nmid c}}(1+t_c(e)),
\]
and let
\[
 d_{r,a}=\ord\bigl((1+x_a)F_{a-1}^{\times}\bigr)
\]
be the actual projective order.  Then the aggregate word is exactly
\[
 \boxed{\mathcal T_{r,a}(e)=\epsilon_{d_{r,a}}(e).}  \tag{\(\mathsf{OSW}_2\)}
\]
It therefore has least period \(d_{r,a}\), linear complexity
\(d_{r,a}-1\), and \(d_{r,a}\) states in both its \(2\)-kernel and minimal
binary DFAO.  In particular, for a current prime \(p\mid\mathcal L_{r,a}\),
\[
 \boxed{
 \mathcal T_{r,a}(\mathcal L_{r,a}/p)=1
 \iff
 v_p(d_{r,a})=v_p(\mathcal L_{r,a}).}                \tag{\(\mathsf{OSW}_3\)}
\]
Thus the entire family of dependency-section terminal scalars has a closed
automatic description, but its minimal state size is the unknown selected
projective order itself.  The compression is an exact order oracle, not a
smaller universal nonvanishing problem.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The standard binomial generating function gives
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 \sum_{e\geq0}u_c(e)T^e
 &=\sum_{v\geq0}\alpha_r^v
   \frac{T^{c+R_0v}}{(1+T)^{c+R_0v+1}}\\
 &=\frac{T^c(1+T)^{R_0-c-1}}
        {(1+T)^{R_0}+\alpha_rT^{R_0}}.
 \end{aligned}
\]
The numerator can vanish only at zero or one, while the denominator is
nonzero at both points.  Hence the fraction is reduced.  Its denominator is
the reciprocal of the translated irreducible polynomial
\[
 (X+1)^{R_0}+\alpha_r
\]
up to a nonzero scalar; its roots are the inverses of the conjugates of
\(1+x_a\).  It is separable because \(R_0\) is odd.  Therefore the least
period of the reduced recurrence is the common multiplicative order
\(\ord(1+x_a)\).

The norm \(t_c(e)\) is zero precisely when the selected section vanishes.
Hence \(\mathcal T_{r,a}(e)=0\) precisely when every nonconstant section
vanishes.  Regrouping the binary expansion of \((1+x_a)^e\) modulo \(R_0\)
shows that this happens exactly when \((1+x_a)^e\in F_{a-1}^{\times}\),
equivalently \(d_{r,a}\mid e\).  This proves \((\mathsf{OSW}_2)\), and
Proposition~\ref{prop:order-indicator-word} supplies the period, complexity,
and automaton assertions.  Since \(d_{r,a}\mid\mathcal L_{r,a}\), evaluation
at \(\mathcal L_{r,a}/p\) proves \((\mathsf{OSW}_3)\), including the full
\(p\)-primary valuation.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[norm and complement are automatic in the ordinary sections]
\label{cor:ordinary-sections-no-go}
Retain Proposition~\ref{prop:ordinary-binary-sections}.  Write
\(e=rE\).  Then
\[
 \frac Lr=pE\equiv1\pmod{q_0-1}.                      \tag{\(\mathsf{ON}_1\)}
\]
Under failure, the sole scalar coordinate in
\((\mathsf{OB}_6)\) is forced to be
\[
 S_{e,0}(A)=(1+A)^E,                                  \tag{\(\mathsf{ON}_2\)}
\]
and the sections satisfy the reciprocal symmetry
\[
 S_{e,r-k}(Z)=Z^{E-1}S_{e,k}(Z^{-1})
 \qquad(1\leq k<r).                                   \tag{\(\mathsf{ON}_3\)}
\]
Thus relative norm and binary complementation are exactly compatible
with the simultaneous vanishings in \((\mathsf{OB}_4)\).
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
LTE gives \(v_r(L)=1\), since \(r\mid q_0-1\), while \(p\ne r\);
hence \(e=rE\).  Write
\[
 L-r=(q_0-1)D.
\]
Modulo \(r\), the usual geometric-sum quotient gives
\[
 D\equiv\sum_{j=1}^{r-1}j=0,
\]
so division by \(r\) proves \((\mathsf{ON}_1)\).  If failure holds,
\((1+x)^e=S_{e,0}(A)\in F_0^\times\).  Raising to \(p\) and using
\[
 (1+x)^L=\Norm_{F/F_0}(1+x)=1+A
\]
together with \((\mathsf{ON}_1)\) proves
\((\mathsf{ON}_2)\).  Finally \(e\) is odd, and complementing a
binary submask \(j=k+tr\) sends it to
\[
 e-j=(r-k)+r(E-t-1).
\]
Pairing the coefficients gives \((\mathsf{ON}_3)\).
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[reduced reciprocal divisor of ordinary failure]
\label{prop:ordinary-reduced-failure-divisor}
Retain the ordinary current-prime notation, and write
\[
 x=x_a,\qquad A=x_{a-1}=x^r,\qquad
 L=\Phi_{r^a}(2^b),\qquad e=L/p=rE.
\]
Then \(E\) is odd.  Put
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 F_{r,E}(X)&=(1+X)^{rE}+(1+X^r)^E,\\
 U(X)&=(1+X)^r,\qquad V(X)=1+X^r,\\
 W_{r,E}(X)&=\sum_{j=0}^{E-1}U(X)^{E-1-j}V(X)^j.
 \end{aligned}                                      \tag{\(\mathsf{RD}_1\)}
\]
Let
\[
 P_x(X)=P_{\alpha_r}(X^{r^a})
\]
be the actual full-conductor minimal polynomial of \(x\), of degree
\(D=r^ab\).  Then
\[
 \boxed{
 \text{failure at }p
 \iff P_x\mid F_{r,E}
 \iff P_x\mid W_{r,E}.}                             \tag{\(\mathsf{RD}_2\)}
\]
The second polynomial is monic and self-reciprocal of degree \(e-r\).
Consequently its reciprocal selected factor \(P_x^*\) also divides it, and
\[
 P_x\ne P_x^*\quad\Longrightarrow\quad 2D\leq e-r. \tag{\(\mathsf{RD}_3\)}
\]

Assume failure for the remaining assertions.  Then \(E>1\), since \(E=1\)
would make \(W_{r,E}=1\).  The hypothetical intersection is nevertheless
transverse.
With
\(\theta=(1+A)^E\),
\[
 \boxed{
 F_{r,E}'(x)
 =\theta\frac{1+x^{r-1}}{(1+x)(1+A)}\ne0,}          \tag{\(\mathsf{RD}_4\)}
\]
so
\[
 P_x^2\nmid F_{r,E},\qquad P_x^2\nmid W_{r,E}.      \tag{\(\mathsf{RD}_5\)}
\]
Its exact transverse norm is
\[
 \boxed{
 \Norm_{F_a/F_{a-1}}F_{r,E}'(x)
 =(1+A)^{e-r-1}(1+A^{r-1}).}                        \tag{\(\mathsf{RD}_6\)}
\]
Iteration through the actual \(r\)-tower gives
\[
 \boxed{
 \Norm_{F_a/\F_2(\alpha_r)}F_{r,E}'(x)
 =(1+\alpha_r)^{e-r-1}(1+\alpha_r^{r-1})\ne0.}     \tag{\(\mathsf{RD}_7\)}
\]
Its further absolute norm to \(\F_2\) is therefore one.  Wherever the
odd-Kummer dependency norm of Proposition~\ref{prop:ordinary-dependency-norm}
continues to the bottom, it computes this same terminal bit.

Thus a hypothetical ordinary failure puts every original nonconstant binary
section on its zero terminal branch while the canonical transverse derivative
lies on a one branch.  This proves that the selected full-conductor
intersection is reduced.  The displayed derivative is a transversality
certificate, not an additional condition excluding that intersection.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Corollary~\ref{cor:ordinary-sections-no-go} gives \(e=rE\), and all the
integers involved are odd.  Failure is equivalent to
\[
 (1+x)^e=(1+A)^E=(1+x^r)^E,
\]
which is exactly \(F_{r,E}(x)=0\).  The full-conductor minimal-polynomial
criterion proves the first equivalence in \((\mathsf{RD}_2)\).

Since \(E\) is odd, the geometric-sum identity in characteristic two gives
\[
 F_{r,E}=U^E+V^E=(U+V)W_{r,E}.                      \tag{\(\mathsf{RD}_8\)}
\]
Both \(U\) and \(V\) are monic reciprocal polynomials of degree \(r\).
Hence \(W_{r,E}\) is monic and reciprocal of degree
\(r(E-1)=e-r\).  The leading terms in \(U+V\) cancel, while the next
coefficient is \(r=1\) in \(\F_2\), so \(\deg(U+V)=r-1<D\).
Thus the irreducible polynomial \(P_x\) cannot divide \(U+V\), and
\((\mathsf{RD}_8)\) proves the second equivalence.  Reciprocity proves
\((\mathsf{RD}_3)\).

Differentiate \((\mathsf{RD}_1)\).  Since \(r,E,e\) are odd,
\[
 F_{r,E}'(X)
 =(1+X)^{e-1}+X^{r-1}(1+X^r)^{E-1}.
\]
Under failure, substitute \(\theta=(1+A)^E=(1+x)^e\) and \(A=x^r\);
bringing the two terms to a common denominator gives
\((\mathsf{RD}_4)\).  Its numerator is nonzero because the exact order of
\(x\) contains \(r\), so \(x^{r-1}\ne1\).  Also \((U+V)(x)\ne0\) by the
degree argument above.  Differentiating \((\mathsf{RD}_8)\) at \(x\) now
proves both assertions in \((\mathsf{RD}_5)\).

Taking the relative norm of \((\mathsf{RD}_4)\) uses
\[
 \Norm(1+x)=1+A,qquad
 \Norm(1+x^{r-1})=1+A^{r-1}.
\]
The base scalar \(\theta\) has norm \(\theta^r\); collecting the powers of
\(1+A\) gives \((\mathsf{RD}_6)\).  The two displayed norm identities
retain the same form at every preceding Kummer step, so iteration gives
\((\mathsf{RD}_7)\).  Its factors are nonzero because
\(\alpha_r\ne1\) and the exact order of \(\alpha_r\) contains \(r\).
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[four exact blind spots for the relative unit]
\label{prop:singleton-relative-unit-blindness}
With the preceding notation, let
\[
 \nu(T)=1+T+\cdots+T^{r-1},\qquad
 \mathcal L=\nu(q_0)=\Phi_{r^a}(2^b),\qquad
 \lambda=q_0\pmod p.
\]
The following standard structural inputs do not determine
\(\left(\frac{\rho}{\mathfrak P}\right)_p\).
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*),leftmargin=*]
\item Relative norm and distribution give only
\[
 \prod_{j=0}^{r-1}
 \left(\frac{\sigma^j(\varepsilon)}{\mathfrak P}\right)_p
 =A^{\nu(\lambda)}=1,
 \qquad
 A=\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{\mathfrak P}\right)_p,       \tag{\(\mathsf{RUB}_1\)}
\]
and \(\nu(\lambda)=0\) has a simple zero.
\item For the finite-field norm
\(\Norm:F_a^\times\to F_{a-1}^\times\), every nonempty norm fibre
maps surjectively to
\[
 F_a^\times/(F_a^\times)^p.                              \tag{\(\mathsf{RUB}_2\)}
\]
In particular, fixing the exact lower norm---even norm one---permits
both the trivial and every nontrivial \(p\)-Kummer class.
\item Put \(H=\langle\sigma\rangle\),
\(R=\mathbb Z_p[H]\), and
\(\mathfrak m=(p,\sigma-q_0)\).  In \(R_{\mathfrak m}\), if
\(u=\mathcal L/p\), then the complete first-order group-ring relation is
\[
 0=pu+\nu'(q_0)(\sigma-q_0)\pmod{\mathfrak m^2},          \tag{\(\mathsf{RUB}_3\)}
\]
where \(\nu'(q_0)\) is a unit modulo \(\mathfrak m\).  This
Bockstein/cotangent relation depends only on the cyclotomic layer and
contains no coordinate of \(\rho\).
\item Global \(p\)-Hilbert reciprocity for the pair \((\rho,2)\) is
an identity independently of the selected local symbol.  More
precisely, if \(\mathfrak q\mid2\) is the selected prime of
\(\mathbb Q(\mu_N)\), its \(p-1\) lifts \(\mathfrak P_c\) to
\(K_{a,p}\), indexed by \(c\in\mathbb F_p^\times\), satisfy
\[
 (\rho,2)_{\mathfrak P_c,p}=R_2^c,
 \qquad
 \prod_{c\in\mathbb F_p^\times}(\rho,2)_{\mathfrak P_c,p}
 =R_2^{p(p-1)/2}=1                                      \tag{\(\mathsf{RUB}_4\)}
\]
for an arbitrary \(R_2\in\mu_p\).
\end{enumerate}
Hence all four displayed consequences are compatible with every value
of the selected symbol.  Without an additional identity peculiar to
\(\rho\), they do not determine \((\mathsf{RU}_3)\).  An argument must
evaluate the distinguished additive unit \(1-\zeta_N\), or an
equivalent explicit \(p\)-adic or Jacobi-sum congruence, beyond ideal
factorization.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The conjugates of \(\varepsilon\) in the current degree-\(r\) layer
have reductions
\[
 1+x_a^{q_0^j}=(1+x_a)^{q_0^j}=y_a^{q_0^j}.
\]
Their residue symbols are therefore \(A^{\lambda^j}\), proving
\((\mathsf{RUB}_1)\).  Equivalently, their product is the reduction of
\(1-\zeta_N^r\), which lies in \(F_{a-1}^\times\); it is automatically
a \(p\)-th power because \(p\nmid|F_{a-1}^\times|\).  Differentiating
\((T-1)\nu(T)=T^r-1\) at \(T=\lambda\) gives
\[
 (\lambda-1)\nu'(\lambda)=r\lambda^{r-1}\ne0,
\]
so the vanishing is simple but supplies no value of \(A\).

The kernel of the finite-field norm has order
\(\mathcal L=(Q-1)/(q_0-1)\).  Since \(p\mid\mathcal L\) but
\(p\nmid q_0-1\), the full \(p\)-Sylow subgroup of \(F_a^\times\)
lies in this kernel.  Its image in the cyclic order-\(p\) quotient
\(F_a^\times/(F_a^\times)^p\) is nontrivial, hence surjective.
Every norm fibre is a coset of the kernel, which proves
\((\mathsf{RUB}_2)\).

In the group ring, \((\sigma-1)\nu(\sigma)=0\).  The element
\(\sigma-1\) is a unit after localization at \(\mathfrak m\), because
its residue is \(\lambda-1\ne0\); hence \(\nu(\sigma)=0\) there.
Taylor expansion about the integer \(q_0\), together with
\(\nu(q_0)=\mathcal L=pu\), gives \((\mathsf{RUB}_3)\).  The derivative
is a unit by the preceding simple-root calculation.  In particular
this is a universal relation in \(\mathbb Z_p[H]\), not an evaluation
of a Kummer class.

Finally set \(K=\mathbb Q(\mu_N)\) and \(E=K(\mu_p)\).  Since
\(p\nmid N\), one has \(K\cap\mathbb Q(\mu_p)=\mathbb Q\).  The local
field \(K_{\mathfrak q}\) already contains \(\mu_p\), because its
residue degree is \(r^ab\) and \(\ord_p(2)\mid r^ab\).  Thus
\(\mathfrak q\) splits completely in \(E/K\).  Galois equivariance of
the Hilbert symbol under the automorphisms
\(\zeta_p\mapsto\zeta_p^c\) proves \((\mathsf{RUB}_4)\); at a prime above
two, \(R_2\) is the residue symbol of \(\rho\), up to the harmless
choice of inversion convention.

For completeness, all remaining local factors are one.  Away from
\(2p\), both entries are units and the tame Hilbert symbol is trivial.
At a prime above \(p\), \(K/\mathbb Q\) is unramified locally and both
entries come from that unramified subfield.  Their Teichmuller parts
are \(p\)-th powers; their principal parts in \(E\) lie in
\(U_{p-1}=1+p\mathcal O_E\), and the standard unit-filtration
orthogonality gives
\((U_{p-1},U_{p-1})_p=1\), since \(2p-2>p\).  The archimedean places
are complex.  Global reciprocity therefore reduces to the already
trivial products \((\mathsf{RUB}_4)\), one for each prime of \(K\) above
two.  Moreover \((\rho)=1\), so ideal-theoretic Stickelberger or
Jacobi-sum factorizations alone retain no datum that can distinguish
the selected value.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[every unweighted base-field selector cancels]
\label{rem:base-selector-cancel}
The cancellation in \((\mathsf{RUB}_4)\) is not peculiar to the second
argument \(2\).  Retain \(K=\mathbb Q(\mu_N)\), \(E=K(\mu_p)\), and the
selected prime \(\mathfrak q\mid2\).  Label its \(p-1\) lifts
\(\mathfrak P_c\), \(c\in\mathbb F_p^\times\), so that the
automorphism carrying \(\mathfrak P_1\) to \(\mathfrak P_c\) sends
\(\zeta_p\) to \(\zeta_p^c\).  For every \(w\in K^\times\), if
\[
 R_w=(\rho,w)_{\mathfrak P_1,p},
\]
then Galois equivariance gives
\[
 (\rho,w)_{\mathfrak P_c,p}=R_w^c,
 \qquad
 \prod_{c\in\mathbb F_p^\times}
 (\rho,w)_{\mathfrak P_c,p}
 =R_w^{p(p-1)/2}=1.                                  \tag{\(\mathsf{RUB}_5\)}
\]
Thus no unweighted selector from the base cyclotomic field can make
global reciprocity isolate one chosen lift above two.  A weighted
eigenspace identity or a Jacobi-sum selector outside \(K\) may evade
\((\mathsf{RUB}_5)\), but ideal factorization alone then leaves local
unit symbols to evaluate.  The same cancellation applies to the
exceptional arm with the current prime \(\ell\) in place of \(p\):
since \(\ell\nmid15h\) and \(\ord_\ell(2)=2h\mid4h\), each of the two
base primes above two splits into \(\ell-1\) lifts and its unweighted
orbit product is one.  Thus merely distinguishing the two base primes
does not distinguish their individual lifts.
\end{remark}

\begin{proposition}[Teichmuller-weighted selector reciprocity]
\label{prop:weighted-selector-reciprocity}
Let \(M\) be odd, let \(p\nmid M\) be an odd prime, and put
\[
 K=\mathbb Q(\mu_M),\qquad E=K(\mu_p),\qquad
 f=\ord_p(2),\qquad d=\ord_M(2).
\]
Assume \(f\mid d\).  For each prime \(\mathfrak q\mid2\) of \(K\),
choose \(\mathfrak P_{\mathfrak q,1}\mid\mathfrak q\) in \(E\), and
write
\[
 \mathfrak P_{\mathfrak q,c}
 =\tau_c\mathfrak P_{\mathfrak q,1},\qquad
 \tau_c(\zeta_p)=\zeta_p^c
 \quad(c\in\mathbb F_p^\times).
\]
Normalize every valuation above two by
\(v_{\mathfrak P}(2)=1\).  For a global unit
\(u\in\mathcal O_K^\times\), set
\[
 A_{\mathfrak q}=(u,2)_{\mathfrak P_{\mathfrak q,1},p},\qquad
 W_{\mathfrak q}(w)
 =\sum_{c\in\mathbb F_p^\times}
 c\,v_{\mathfrak P_{\mathfrak q,c}}(w)
 \in\mathbb F_p.
\]
Then, for every \(w\in E^\times\),
\[
 \prod_{c\in\mathbb F_p^\times}
 (u,w)_{\mathfrak P_{\mathfrak q,c},p}
 =A_{\mathfrak q}^{W_{\mathfrak q}(w)}.              \tag{\(\mathsf{WS}_1\)}
\]
If the divisor of \(w\) is supported above two, global reciprocity
becomes the exact weighted identity
\[
 \prod_{\mathfrak q\mid2}
 A_{\mathfrak q}^{W_{\mathfrak q}(w)}
 =\prod_{\lambda\mid p}(u,w)_{\lambda,p}^{-1}.        \tag{\(\mathsf{WS}_2\)}
\]
Moreover
\[
 W_{\mathfrak q}(\tau_e w)=eW_{\mathfrak q}(w),       \tag{\(\mathsf{WS}_3\)}
\]
and
\[
 W_{\mathfrak q}(w)=0
 \quad\hbox{if }w\in K^\times
 \quad\hbox{or }w\in\mathbb Q(\mu_p)^\times.        \tag{\(\mathsf{WS}_4\)}
\]
Thus a selector which isolates a chosen lift above two must be
genuinely mixed between the \(M\)- and \(p\)-cyclotomic directions.
Even then, \((\mathsf{WS}_2)\) retains the local factors above \(p\):
they are not determined by the ideal-theoretic weights.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The completion \(K_{\mathfrak q}\) has residue degree \(d\), so
\(f\mid d\) implies \(\mu_p\subset K_{\mathfrak q}\).  Hence
\(\mathfrak q\) splits completely in \(E/K\).  At a place above two,
the tame \(p\)-Hilbert symbol of two local units is one.  Since \(u\)
is a unit, bilinearity therefore gives
\[
 (u,w)_{\mathfrak P,p}
 =(u,2)_{\mathfrak P,p}^{v_{\mathfrak P}(w)}.
\]
Galois equivariance gives
\[
 (u,2)_{\mathfrak P_{\mathfrak q,c},p}
 =\tau_c(A_{\mathfrak q})=A_{\mathfrak q}^{c},
\]
which proves \((\mathsf{WS}_1)\).  If \((w)\) is supported above two,
