Thus, whenever \(x+y=S\) and \(xy=P\),
\[
 \mathcal D_r(S,P)=x^r+y^r.
\]

Fix \(n\geq2\) and a prime \(\ell\mid F_n\).  Put \(r_{-1}=1\), and
for \(0\leq j<n\) let \(r_j\in E_j^\times\) be the unique element
such that
\[
 r_j^\ell=a_j.
\]
Then, for every \(0\leq j<n\),
\[
 \Norm_{E_j/E_{j-1}}(r_j)=r_{j-1}^3,
 \qquad
 \mathcal D_\ell\!\left(
   \operatorname{Tr}_{E_j/E_{j-1}}(r_j),r_{j-1}^3
 \right)=r_{j-1}^\ell.                               \tag{\(\mathsf{RA}\)}
\]

Set \(b=r_{n-1}\).  Failure of the \(\ell\)-primary
Conway--Fermat condition at the top step has the exact descended form
\[
 c_n\in(E_n^\times)^\ell
 \iff
 \text{there exists }s\in E_{n-1}
 \text{ such that }\mathcal D_\ell(s,b)=1.            \tag{\(\mathsf{RD}\)}
\]
If additionally \(\delta_j=F_j\) for every \(1\leq j<n\), then, with
\(q=|E_{n-1}|=2^{2^n}\),
\[
 \ord(b)=\ord(a_{n-1})=\frac{q-1}{3}.
\]
Consequently all exact lower trace, norm, and multiplicative-order
data are automatically compatible with a hypothetical top failure;
the single selected equation \((\mathsf{RD})\) is the remaining
obstruction.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Because \(\ell\mid F_n\), it is coprime to \(|E_j^\times|\) for every
\(j<n\).  Hence the \(\ell\)-power map is an automorphism on every
lower multiplicative group and the elements \(r_j\) are unique.
Applying the \(\ell\)-power map to the norm and using
Proposition~\ref{prop:fermat-ancestry-exhaustion} gives
\[
 \Norm(r_j)^\ell
 =\Norm(a_j)
 =a_{j-1}^3
 =(r_{j-1}^3)^\ell.
\]
Injectivity of the \(\ell\)-power map on \(E_{j-1}^\times\) proves
the first identity in \((\mathsf{RA})\).  If \(r_j'\) is the relative
conjugate of \(r_j\), then
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 \mathcal D_\ell\!\left(r_j+r_j',r_jr_j'\right)
 &=r_j^\ell+(r_j')^\ell\\
 &=\operatorname{Tr}_{E_j/E_{j-1}}(a_j)
 =a_{j-1}=r_{j-1}^\ell,
 \end{aligned}
\]
which proves the second.

The group \(E_{n-1}^\times\) is uniquely \(\ell\)-divisible, while
\(E_n^\times/E_{n-1}^\times\) is cyclic.  Hence the coset of \(c_n\)
misses the full \(\ell\)-primary part of the quotient if and only if
it is an \(\ell\)-th power there, equivalently if and only if
\(c_n\in(E_n^\times)^\ell\).

Suppose first that \(c_n=t^\ell\).  Since
\(\operatorname{Tr}_{E_n/E_{n-1}}(c_n)=1\) and
\(\Norm_{E_n/E_{n-1}}(c_n)=a_{n-1}\), injectivity below gives
\[
 \Norm(t)=b.
\]
For \(s=\operatorname{Tr}(t)\), the power-sum identity therefore gives
\[
 \mathcal D_\ell(s,b)=\operatorname{Tr}(t^\ell)=1.
\]

Conversely, suppose \(\mathcal D_\ell(s,b)=1\), and let \(t,t'\) be
the roots of
\[
 X^2+sX+b.
\]
They lie in the unique extension of \(E_{n-1}\) of degree at most two,
hence in \(E_n\).  Their \(\ell\)-th powers satisfy
\[
 t^\ell+(t')^\ell=1,\qquad
 t^\ell(t')^\ell=b^\ell=a_{n-1}.
\]
Thus \(t^\ell,(t')^\ell\) are the two roots of
\[
 X^2+X+a_{n-1},
\]
namely \(c_n,c_n+1\).  In particular \(c_n\) is an \(\ell\)-th
power, proving \((\mathsf{RD})\).

Finally, prior maximality, Corollary~\ref{cor:fermat-popovych-equivalence},
and the \(n=1\) base case established in its proof give
\[
 \ord(a_{n-1})
 =\prod_{j=1}^{n-1}F_j
 =\frac{q-1}{3}.
\]
Taking the unique \(\ell\)-th root preserves this order because
\(\ell\) is coprime to \(q-1\).
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[choice-free lower root]
\label{cor:fermat-explicit-lower-root}
Retain Proposition~\ref{prop:fermat-root-ancestry}, and put
\[
 q=|E_{n-1}|,\qquad d=\frac{q+1}{\ell},\qquad Q=\frac q2,
 \qquad a=a_{n-1},\qquad b=r_{n-1}.
\]
Then the unique lower \(\ell\)-th root occurring in the Dickson
obstruction is determined without any root choice:
\[
 b^2=a^d,
 \qquad
 b=(a^d)^Q.                                           \tag{\(\mathsf{RE}\)}
\]
Consequently the exact failure criterion \((\mathsf{RD})\) is
\[
 c_n\in(E_n^\times)^\ell
 \iff
 \text{there exists }s\in E_{n-1}\text{ such that }
 \mathcal D_\ell\!\left(s,(a^d)^Q\right)=1.           \tag{\(\mathsf{RD}'\)}
\]
Thus the descent contains no auxiliary choice of a compatible lower root.
It still asks whether one selected Dickson value vanishes, so
\((\mathsf{RE})\) is a lossless normalization rather than a
nonvanishing theorem.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Since \(b\in\mathbb F_q\), one has \(b^q=b\).  The identities
\(b^\ell=a\) and \(\ell d=q+1\) give
\[
 b^2=b^{q+1}=b^{\ell d}=a^d.
\]
As \(q=2Q\), Frobenius fixity then gives
\[
 b=b^q=(b^2)^Q=(a^d)^Q.
\]
Substitution in \((\mathsf{RD})\) proves \((\mathsf{RD}')\).
The two displayed identities are formalized in the companion Lean file.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Dickson all-or-nothing factorization]
\label{prop:fermat-dickson-dichotomy}
Retain Proposition~\ref{prop:fermat-root-ancestry}.  Thus \(n\geq2\),
\[
 K=E_{n-1}=\mathbb F_q,\qquad L=E_n=\mathbb F_{q^2},
 \qquad a=a_{n-1},\qquad b=r_{n-1},\qquad b^\ell=a,
\]
where the prime \(\ell\) divides \(F_n=q+1\).  Put \(c=c_n\), so
that
\[
 c^2+c=a,\qquad c^q=c+1,
\]
and define
\[
 f_{\ell,n}(X)=\mathcal D_\ell(X,b)+1\in K[X].
\]
Then \(f_{\ell,n}\) is separable of degree \(\ell\), and exactly one
of the following occurs:
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)}]
\item \(c\in L^{\times\ell}\), in which case \(f_{\ell,n}\) splits
      into \(\ell\) distinct linear factors over \(K\);
\item \(c\notin L^{\times\ell}\), in which case \(f_{\ell,n}\) is
      irreducible over \(K\).
\end{enumerate}
Consequently the Dickson equation in \((\mathsf{RD})\) never has an
intermediate number of roots: it has either \(\ell\) roots in \(K\)
or none.

More explicitly, choose \(t\in\overline K\) with \(t^\ell=c\), and put
\[
 \vartheta=\frac{t^{q+1}}b\in\mu_\ell.
\]
This element is independent of the chosen \(\ell\)-th root, while the
standard finite-field Kummer symbol is
\[
 c^{(q^2-1)/\ell}=\vartheta^{-2}.
\]
With the convention
\(\operatorname{Res}(f,g)=\prod_{f(s)=0}g(s)\), one has
\[
 \operatorname{Res}_X
 \bigl(f_{\ell,n}(X),X^q-X\bigr)
 =(1+\vartheta)^\ell.                                 \tag{\(\mathsf{DF}\)}
\]
Thus the resultant vanishes precisely in the Conway--Fermat failure
case.

Finally, if \(A_{n-1}\) is the minimal polynomial of \(a_{n-1}\) over
\(\mathbb F_2\), then
\[
 \mathcal C_n(Y):=A_{n-1}(Y^2+Y)
\]
is the degree-\(2^{n+1}\) minimal polynomial of \(c_n\).  The absolute
composition
\[
 \mathcal C_n(X^\ell)
 =A_{n-1}(X^{2\ell}+X^\ell)                            \tag{\(\mathsf{AC}\)}
\]
is irreducible of degree \(2^{n+1}\ell\) in case~\textup{(ii)}, while
in case~\textup{(i)} it is the product of exactly \(\ell\) distinct
irreducible polynomials, each of degree \(2^{n+1}\).
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Because \(\ell\mid q+1\), one has \(\mu_\ell\subset L\) and
\(q\equiv-1\pmod\ell\).  Fix a primitive \(\ell\)-th root
\(\zeta\), choose \(t^\ell=c\), and put \(\widetilde t=b/t\).  Since
\[
 \widetilde t^\ell=\frac{b^\ell}{c}
 =\frac ac=c+1,
\]
the \(\ell\) elements, indexed by \(\mathbb Z/\ell\mathbb Z\),
\[
 s_i=t\zeta^i+\widetilde t\zeta^{-i}
\]
satisfy
\[
 \mathcal D_\ell(s_i,b)
 =(t\zeta^i)^\ell+(\widetilde t\zeta^{-i})^\ell
 =c+(c+1)=1.
\]
They are distinct.  Indeed, equality \(s_i=s_j\) identifies the
unordered root pairs of the corresponding polynomials
\(X^2+s_iX+b\).  Besides \(i=j\), the only possible pairing would
identify \(t\zeta^i\) with \(\widetilde t\zeta^{-j}\); taking
\(\ell\)-th powers would give \(c=c+1\).  Hence the \(s_i\) are all
the roots of the monic degree-\(\ell\) polynomial \(f_{\ell,n}\).

There is a unique \(k\in\mathbb Z/\ell\mathbb Z\) such that
\[
 t^q=\widetilde t\zeta^k.
\]
Since \(\zeta^q=\zeta^{-1}\), direct substitution gives
\[
 s_i^q=s_{i-k},                                        \tag{\(\mathsf{DF}_1\)}
\]
and applying \(q\)-Frobenius once more gives
\[
 t^{q^2}=t\zeta^{-2k}.                                 \tag{\(\mathsf{DF}_2\)}
\]
As \(\ell\) is odd, \(k=0\) if and only if \(t\in L\).  Because all
\(\ell\)-th roots of \(c\) differ by an element of
\(\mu_\ell\subset L\), this is equivalent to
\(c\in L^{\times\ell}\).  If \(k=0\), every \(s_i\) is fixed by
\(q\)-Frobenius.  If \(k\ne0\), translation by \(-k\) is one
\(\ell\)-cycle because \(\ell\) is prime.  Equation
\((\mathsf{DF}_1)\) therefore proves the split/irreducible dichotomy.

The quotient \(t^{q+1}/b\) is \(\zeta^k\), and replacing \(t\) by
another \(\ell\)-th root does not change it because
\(\ell\mid q+1\).  Equation \((\mathsf{DF}_2)\) yields
\[
 c^{(q^2-1)/\ell}=t^{q^2-1}=\vartheta^{-2}.
\]
For the resultant, characteristic two and \((\mathsf{DF}_1)\) give
\[
 \operatorname{Res}(f_{\ell,n},X^q-X)
 =\prod_i(s_{i-k}+s_i).
\]
Moreover
\[
 s_{i-k}+s_i
 =(1+\vartheta)
 \left(\vartheta^{-1}t\zeta^i+\widetilde t\zeta^{-i}\right).
\]
After pulling out the factors \(\zeta^{-i}\), whose product is one,
the fact that multiplication by two permutes
\(\mathbb Z/\ell\mathbb Z\) gives
\[
 \prod_i
 \left(\vartheta^{-1}t\zeta^i+\widetilde t\zeta^{-i}\right)
 = (\vartheta^{-1}t)^\ell+\widetilde t^\ell
 =c+(c+1)=1.
\]
This proves \((\mathsf{DF})\).

The element \(a\) has absolute trace one, so \(Y^2+Y+a\) is
irreducible over \(K\).  Since \(a=c^2+c\) and
\([K:\mathbb F_2]=2^n\), the element \(c\) has degree \(2^{n+1}\)
over \(\mathbb F_2\); hence \(\mathcal C_n\) is its minimal
polynomial.  Capelli's lemma now says that
\(\mathcal C_n(X^\ell)\) is irreducible over \(\mathbb F_2\) if and
only if \(X^\ell-c\) is irreducible over \(L\).  Since
\(\mu_\ell\subset L\) and \(\ell\) is prime, this holds exactly when
\(c\notin L^{\times\ell}\).

In the other case every root \(x\) of \(\mathcal C_n(X^\ell)\) lies
in \(L\), because its \(\ell\)-th power is a Frobenius conjugate of
\(c\) and all its \(\ell\)-th roots lie in \(L\).  That conjugate has
degree \(2^{n+1}\), so
\[
 2^{n+1}\leq[\mathbb F_2(x):\mathbb F_2]\leq[L:\mathbb F_2]
 =2^{n+1}.
\]
Separability then partitions the \(2^{n+1}\ell\) roots into exactly
\(\ell\) distinct Frobenius orbits of length \(2^{n+1}\), proving
the final assertion.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[reverse-Dickson trace divisor]
\label{prop:fermat-trace-divisor}
Retain Proposition~\ref{prop:fermat-dickson-dichotomy}, and put
\[
 m=2^n,\qquad q=2^m,\qquad K=\F_q.
\]
Let $A_{n-1}\in\F_2[T]$ be the degree-$m$ minimal polynomial of
$a=a_{n-1}$, and define
\[
 \begin{split}
 R_\ell(Z)&=Z^\ell\mathcal D_\ell(Z^{-1},1)
            =\mathcal D_\ell(1,Z)^2,\\
 P_{\ell,a}(Z)&=Z^\ell+aR_\ell(Z),\\
 H_m(Z)&=1+\sum_{j=0}^{m-1}Z^{2^j},\\
 \mathcal P_{\ell,n}(Z)
   &=\prod_{r=0}^{m-1}P_{\ell,a^{2^r}}(Z)
     =R_\ell(Z)^m
       A_{n-1}\!\left(\frac{Z^\ell}{R_\ell(Z)}\right).
 \end{split}
                                                               \tag{\(\mathsf{TD}\)}
\]
The displayed rational expression is a polynomial in \(\F_2[Z]\).
Moreover
\[
 R_\ell\in\F_2[Z^2],\qquad R_\ell(0)=1,
 \qquad [Z^{\ell-1}]R_\ell=1,
\]
and the following are equivalent:
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)}]
\item $c_n\in E_n^{\times\ell}$, so the singleton-even arm fails at
      \(\ell\);
\item $P_{\ell,a}$ splits completely over $K$;
\item
\[
       \mathcal P_{\ell,n}(Z)\mid H_m(Z)
       \qquad\hbox{in }\F_2[Z].                         \tag{\(\mathsf{TD}_1\)}
\]
\end{enumerate}
In the failure case every root of $P_{\ell,a}$ has absolute trace
one, and
\[
 \sum_{P_{\ell,a}(z)=0}z
 =\prod_{P_{\ell,a}(z)=0}z=a.                          \tag{\(\mathsf{TD}_2\)}
\]
There is also an absolute all-or-nothing alternative:
\(
\mathcal P_{\ell,n}
\)
is irreducible over \(\F_2\) of degree $m\ell$ on success, while on
failure it is the product of exactly \(\ell\) distinct irreducible
polynomials of degree $m$.

Consequently failure forces
\[
 m\ell\leq\frac q2.
                                                               \tag{\(\mathsf{TD}_3\)}
\]
Equivalently, for the complementary divisor $d=(q+1)/\ell$, one
must have $d\geq2m+1$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The Dickson polynomial \(\mathcal D_\ell(X,1)\) contains only odd
powers because \(\ell\) is odd.  Termwise comparison gives the first
identity in \((\mathsf{TD})\) and the three assertions about
\(R_\ell\).  In particular
\[
 P_{\ell,a}'(Z)=Z^{\ell-1};
\]
since $P_{\ell,a}(0)=a\ne0$, this polynomial is separable.

For $s\ne0$, set $z=b/s^2$.  Dickson homogeneity gives
\[
 \mathcal D_\ell(1,z)
   =s^{-\ell}\mathcal D_\ell(s,b),
\]
and hence
\[
 P_{\ell,a}(z)
 =\frac{a}{s^{2\ell}}
   \left(1+\mathcal D_\ell(s,b)^2\right).              \tag{\(\mathsf{TD}_4\)}
\]
Thus $s\mapsto b/s^2$ is a Frobenius-equivariant bijection between
the roots of
\(\mathcal D_\ell(X,b)+1\) and the roots of $P_{\ell,a}$; its
inverse sends $z$ to the unique square root of $b/z$.  The
split/irreducible dichotomy in
Proposition~\ref{prop:fermat-dickson-dichotomy} therefore proves the
equivalence of \textup{(i)} and \textup{(ii)}.

Suppose failure holds, and let $s\in K$ be one of the Dickson roots.
If $t,t'$ are the roots of $X^2+sX+b$, then
\[
 \{t^\ell,(t')^\ell\}=\{c_n,c_n+1\}.
\]
Neither right-hand element belongs to $K$, so the quadratic is
irreducible over $K$.  The Artin--Schreier criterion now gives
\[
 \operatorname{Tr}_{K/\F_2}(b/s^2)=1.
\]
Thus the \(\ell\) roots of $P_{\ell,a}$ are distinct trace-one
elements of $K$.

The Frobenius conjugates of $a$ are distinct.  The root sets of two
different conjugate polynomials $P_{\ell,a^{2^r}}$ are disjoint:
a common root $z$ would force $R_\ell(z)=0$, then $z^\ell=0$,
contrary to $R_\ell(0)=1$.  Therefore failure supplies $m\ell$
distinct trace-one roots of \(\mathcal P_{\ell,n}\).  The roots of
\(H_m\) are exactly the trace-one elements of $K$, proving
\textup{(iii)}.  Conversely, \textup{(iii)} puts every root of
\(P_{\ell,a}\) in $K$; equation \((\mathsf{TD}_4)\) then puts every
Dickson root in $K$, so Proposition~\ref{prop:fermat-dickson-dichotomy}
gives failure.  The coefficient and constant terms of $P_{\ell,a}$
give \((\mathsf{TD}_2)\).

On success $P_{\ell,a}$ is irreducible over $K$.  If $z$ is one
of its roots, then
\[
 a=\frac{z^\ell}{R_\ell(z)}\in\F_2(z),
\]
so $K=\F_2(a)\subseteq\F_2(z)$, and $z$ has absolute degree
$m\ell$.  Hence \(\mathcal P_{\ell,n}\) is its irreducible
polynomial.  On failure every root $z\in K$ still has absolute
degree $m$, by the same displayed identity, and the disjoint
conjugate fibres form exactly \(\ell\) Frobenius orbits.  This proves
the factorization alternative.  Finally
\(\deg H_m=2^{m-1}=q/2\), giving \((\mathsf{TD}_3)\); the equivalent
form follows from $q\geq2m$.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[the Conway trace is the top-bit functional]
\label{prop:fermat-top-bit-trace}
For every \(n\geq0\), put
\(m=2^n\), \(q=2^m\), \(K=E_{n-1}=\F_q\), and
\(a=a_{n-1}=\prod_{j<n}c_j\), with the empty product
\(a_{-1}=1\).  In the ordinary binary
representatives of the nimbers in \(K\), one has
\[
 a=\prod_{j=0}^{n-1}c_j=2^{m-1}=q/2.                  \tag{\(\mathsf{TB}_1\)}
\]
For the Conway bit basis \(e_i=2^i\), \(0\leq i<m\),
\[
 \operatorname{Tr}_{K/\F_2}(e_i)=
 \begin{cases}
  0,&i<m-1,\\
  1,&i=m-1.
 \end{cases}                                          \tag{\(\mathsf{TB}_2\)}
\]
Consequently absolute trace is literally the top-bit coordinate:
\[
 \ker\operatorname{Tr}_{K/\F_2}=\{0,\ldots,q/2-1\},
 \qquad
 \operatorname{Tr}_{K/\F_2}^{-1}(1)=\{q/2,\ldots,q-1\}.
                                                               \tag{\(\mathsf{TB}_3\)}
\]
In particular \(a\) is the least Conway ordinal of absolute trace one.

Now assume \(n\geq2\), let the prime \(\ell\mid F_n\), and retain
the polynomial \(P_{\ell,a}\) of
Proposition~\ref{prop:fermat-trace-divisor}.  Let
\[
 L_m(Z)=\sum_{i=0}^{m-1}Z^{2^i}.
\]
Then the singleton-even arm fails at \(\ell\) exactly when
\[
 \boxed{P_{\ell,a}(Z+a)\mid L_m(Z)\quad\hbox{in }K[Z].}
                                                               \tag{\(\mathsf{TB}_4\)}
\]
Thus the bad reverse-Dickson fibre is centered, at the literal least
trace-one nimber, inside the trace-zero linearized polynomial.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Products of distinct Conway--Fermat generators require no quadratic
reduction, and therefore agree with their ordinary binary product.  Hence
\[
 a=\prod_{j<n}2^{2^j}
   =2^{\sum_{j<n}2^j}=2^{2^n-1}=2^{m-1},
\]
which proves \((\mathsf{TB}_1)\).

We prove \((\mathsf{TB}_2)\) by induction on \(n\).  The assertion for
\(K=\F_2\) is immediate.  For \(n\geq1\), put \(d=m/2\) and
\(K_0=E_{n-2}=\F_{2^d}\).  If \(i<d\), then \(e_i\in K_0\), so its
relative trace through the quadratic extension \(K/K_0\) is zero.  If
\(i\geq d\), disjoint Fermat support gives
\[
 e_i=c_{n-1}e_{i-d}.
\]
The two roots of the Conway equation for \(c_{n-1}\) differ by one;
hence \(\operatorname{Tr}_{K/K_0}(c_{n-1})=1\), and
\[
 \operatorname{Tr}_{K/K_0}(e_i)=e_{i-d}.
\]
Trace transitivity and the induction hypothesis leave the value one
only at \(i-d=d-1\), namely \(i=m-1\).  Linearity now proves
\((\mathsf{TB}_3)\).

By Proposition~\ref{prop:fermat-trace-divisor}, failure is equivalent
to complete splitting of the separable polynomial \(P_{\ell,a}\) over
\(K\), and every one of its roots has trace one.  Since \(a\) also has
trace one, translation by \(a\) puts all those roots in the trace-zero
space.  Its vanishing polynomial is the separable linearized polynomial
\(L_m\), proving the forward implication in \((\mathsf{TB}_4)\).
Conversely, the divisibility puts every root of \(P_{\ell,a}(Z+a)\),
and hence every root of \(P_{\ell,a}\), in \(K\).  The same proposition
then gives failure.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[additive top-bit countermodels]
\label{prop:fermat-top-bit-countermodel}
Let \(m=2^n\geq2\), put \(q=2^m\), and write
\[
 K=\F_q,\qquad L=\F_{q^2},\qquad
 U=\ker\!\left(\Norm_{L/K}\right)=\mu_{q+1}.
\]
Let the odd prime \(\ell\mid q+1\) satisfy \((q+1)/\ell>1\).
Choose
\[
 w\in U^\ell\setminus\{1\},\qquad u_0\in U,\quad u_0^\ell=w,
 \qquad \Phi(u)=\frac{u}{(u+1)^2},\qquad a=\Phi(w).
                                                               \tag{\(\mathsf{AT}_1\)}
\]
Then \(a\in K\) has absolute trace one and exact degree \(m\) over
\(\F_2\).  With
\[
 T_\ell(Z)=\mathcal D_\ell(1,Z),\qquad
 P_{\ell,a}(Z)=Z^\ell+aT_\ell(Z)^2,
\]
one has
\[
 \begin{split}
 G(Z):=P_{\ell,a}(Z+a)
 &=\prod_{v\in\mu_\ell}
   \bigl(Z+\Phi(u_0v)+a\bigr),\\
 G(Z)&\mid L_m(Z):=\sum_{i=0}^{m-1}Z^{2^i}.
 \end{split}                                               \tag{\(\mathsf{AT}_2\)}
\]

More precisely, if
\[
 \mathcal T=\{\Phi(u_0v)+a:v\in\mu_\ell\},
 \qquad W=\operatorname{span}_{\F_2}(\mathcal T),
\]
and \(M_W(Z)=\prod_{w_0\in W}(Z+w_0)\) is the subspace
polynomial, then
\[
 \sum_{t\in\mathcal T}t=0,\qquad
 \lceil\log_2\ell\rceil
 \leq\dim_{\F_2}W\leq\min(m-1,\ell-1),                 \tag{\(\mathsf{AT}_3\)}
\]
and
\[
 G\mid M_W\mid L_m.                                      \tag{\(\mathsf{AT}_4\)}
\]
The trace operator is self-adjoint for
\(\langle x,y\rangle=\operatorname{Tr}_{K/\F_2}(xy)\), with
\[
 (\ker\operatorname{Tr})^\perp=\F_2,                    \tag{\(\mathsf{AT}_5\)}
\]
and the coefficient and derivative constraints are compatible as well:
\[
 [Z^{\ell-1}]G=0,\qquad G'(Z)=(Z+a)^{\ell-1}.          \tag{\(\mathsf{AT}_6\)}
\]

Finally, choose any basis of \(\ker\operatorname{Tr}\) and append
\(a\).  In the induced binary labeling of \(K\), the element \(a\)
is literally the top bit and the least trace-one label.  Hence the
finite-field structure, top-bit trace rule, centered linearized
divisibility, Moore bounds, and adjoint identities are jointly compatible
with failure.  This is not a Conway counterexample: the adapted basis
need not be the multiplicative Fermat basis.  It proves that an exclusion
must use that special multiplicative ancestry, not only additive trace or
a bare choice of binary labeling.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
For \(u\in U\setminus\{1\}\), put \(c=(1+u)^{-1}\).  Since
\(u^q=u^{-1}\), one has
\[
 c^q=c+1,\qquad \Phi(u)=c^2+c.
\]
The Artin--Schreier criterion therefore gives
\(\operatorname{Tr}_{K/\F_2}(\Phi(u))=1\).  In particular \(a\)
has trace one.  If \(a\) lay in a proper subfield
\(\F_{2^d}\subsetneq K\), then \(m/d\) would be an even power of
two and trace transitivity would give
\[
 \operatorname{Tr}_{K/\F_2}(a)
 =(m/d)\operatorname{Tr}_{\F_{2^d}/\F_2}(a)=0,
\]
a contradiction.  Thus \(a\) has degree \(m\).

The Dickson semiconjugacy calculation of
Proposition~\ref{prop:fermat-coset-semiconjugacy} applies to every
\(w=u_0^\ell\) and gives the product in \((\mathsf{AT}_2)\).
All values \(\Phi(u_0v)\) have trace one, so their translates by
\(a\) lie in the trace kernel, whose vanishing polynomial is \(L_m\).
The roots are distinct by the inverse-pair calculation in
\((\mathsf{KC}_1)\): for distinct \(v,v'\), equality could only pair
\(u_0v\) with its
inverse, which after raising to \(\ell\) would force \(w=w^{-1}\),
and the odd-order group \(U\) would then force \(w=1\).  This proves
the divisibility.

The original roots have sum \(a\), while translating \(\ell\) of them
adds \(\ell a=a\); hence \(\sum_{t\in\mathcal T}t=0\).  Since
\(W\) contains \(\ell\) distinct points, its dimension is at least
\(\lceil\log_2\ell\rceil\).  The displayed nontrivial dependence
and \(W\subseteq\ker\operatorname{Tr}\) give the two upper bounds.
Root-set inclusion among separable split polynomials proves
\((\mathsf{AT}_4)\).

For the trace pairing,
\[
 \langle\operatorname{Tr}(x),y\rangle
 =\operatorname{Tr}(x)\operatorname{Tr}(y)
 =\langle x,\operatorname{Tr}(y)\rangle.
\]
The one-dimensional space \(\F_2\) is orthogonal to the trace kernel,
so nondegeneracy gives \((\mathsf{AT}_5)\).  Finally
\(P_{\ell,a}'(Z)=Z^{\ell-1}\), and translation cancels its
next-to-leading coefficient because \(\ell\) is odd.  This proves
\((\mathsf{AT}_6)\).  Extending a basis of the trace kernel by \(a\)
gives the final binary labeling.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[half-field additive saturation of a failure fibre]
\label{prop:fermat-additive-saturation}
Retain Proposition~\ref{prop:fermat-trace-divisor}.  Write
\[
 q=Q^2=2^m,\qquad K=\F_q,\qquad K_0=\F_Q,
\]
and suppose that the Conway--Fermat condition fails at \(\ell\).
Let \(S\subset K\) be the \(\ell\)-element root set of
\(P_{\ell,a}\).  Then, for every \(t\in K_0\),
\[
 P_{\ell,a}(Z+t)\mid H_m(Z).                         \tag{\(\mathsf{AS}_1\)}
\]
These translated polynomials are pairwise distinct; indeed,
\[
 [Z^{\ell-1}]P_{\ell,a}(Z+t)=a+t.                   \tag{\(\mathsf{AS}_2\)}
\]
Define the relative-trace polynomial
\[
 \Theta_{\ell,a}(Y)
 =\prod_{z\in S}\bigl(Y+z^Q+z\bigr)\in K_0[Y].
\]
Then
\[
 \prod_{t\in K_0}P_{\ell,a}(Z+t)
 =\Theta_{\ell,a}(Z^Q+Z).                           \tag{\(\mathsf{AS}_3\)}
\]
If \(\Theta_{\ell,a}\) is separable, then
\[
 \ell\leq\frac Q2.                                  \tag{\(\mathsf{AS}_4\)}
\]
Equivalently, failure at a prime \(\ell>Q/2\) forces distinct roots
\(z,z'\in S\) with
\[
 z^Q+z=(z')^Q+z',
 \qquad\text{or equivalently}\qquad z+z'\in K_0.    \tag{\(\mathsf{AS}_5\)}
\]
Thus separability of this one selected relative-trace fibre would give
a square-root-scale sieve strictly sharper than the unconditional degree
bound \((\mathsf{TD}_3)\).  It is an additional selected assertion, not
a consequence of one-step trace and norm data.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Every \(z\in S\) has absolute trace one by
Proposition~\ref{prop:fermat-trace-divisor}.  For \(t\in K_0\),
\[
 \operatorname{Tr}_{K/\F_2}(t)
 =\operatorname{Tr}_{K_0/\F_2}(t+t)=0.
\]
Hence all roots \(z+t\) of \(P_{\ell,a}(Z+t)\) are again roots of
the trace-one polynomial \(H_m\), proving \((\mathsf{AS}_1)\).
Because \(R_\ell\) has degree \(\ell-1\) and leading coefficient
one, while \(\ell\) is odd, translation gives
\((\mathsf{AS}_2)\).

Interchanging the two products gives
\[
\begin{split}
 \prod_{t\in K_0}P_{\ell,a}(Z+t)
 &=\prod_{z\in S}\prod_{t\in K_0}(Z+t+z)\\
 &=\prod_{z\in S}\bigl(Z^Q+Z+z^Q+z\bigr),
\end{split}
\]
which is \((\mathsf{AS}_3)\).  If \(\Theta_{\ell,a}\) is separable,
its \(\ell\) relative-trace values are distinct.  The \(Q\) translates
of each root then give \(Q\ell\) distinct trace-one elements of \(K\).
There are exactly \(q/2=Q^2/2\) such elements, proving
\((\mathsf{AS}_4)\).  Negating this injectivity condition gives
\((\mathsf{AS}_5)\).
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[relative-trace collision factorization]
\label{prop:fermat-relative-trace-axes}
Retain Proposition~\ref{prop:fermat-additive-saturation}, and put
\[
 U=\ker\!\left(\Norm_{\F_{q^2}/K}\right),\qquad
 U_0=\ker\!\left(\Norm_{K/K_0}\right),\qquad
 \Phi(u)=\frac{u}{(u+1)^2}.
\]
Under the hypothetical failure, choose \(u_0\in U\) with
\(u_0^\ell=w=(c_n+1)/c_n\), so that, as in
Proposition~\ref{prop:fermat-coset-semiconjugacy},
\[
 S=\{\Phi(u_0v):v\in\mu_\ell\}.
\]
For \(x^\ell=w\) and \(v\in\mu_\ell\setminus\{1\}\), set
\[
 A=x^Q,\qquad B=v^Q,\qquad
 D=(1+x)(1+xv),\qquad D_Q=(1+A)(1+AB)
\]
and
\[
 \begin{split}
 N_0&=A(1+B)D+x(1+v)D_Q,\\
 N_1&=N_0+DD_Q.
 \end{split}
\]
All four factors in \(DD_Q\) are nonzero, and the collision equation
factors exactly as
\[
 \Phi(A)+\Phi(AB)+\Phi(x)+\Phi(xv)
 =\frac{N_0N_1}{(DD_Q)^2}.                            \tag{\(\mathsf{AX}_1\)}
\]

There is a more intrinsic form.  Let \(s\in\mu_\ell\) be the unique
square root of \(v\), put \(y=xs\), and set
\[
 \alpha=\Phi(y),\qquad \beta=\Phi(s).
\]
Then \(y^\ell=w\), \(s^\ell=1\), and \(\alpha\ne\beta\).  The two
factors in \((\mathsf{AX}_1)\) give the disjoint alternatives
\[
 \begin{split}
 N_0=0&\iff \alpha/\beta\in K_0^\times,\\
 N_1=0&\iff \alpha/\beta\in U_0.
 \end{split}                                           \tag{\(\mathsf{AX}_2\)}
\]
Consequently the selected polynomial \(\Theta_{\ell,a}\) is
separable if and only if
\[
 \frac{\Phi(y)}{\Phi(s)}
 \notin K_0^\times\cup U_0                            \tag{\(\mathsf{AX}_3\)}
\]
for every \(y^\ell=w\) and every
\(s\in\mu_\ell\setminus\{1\}\).  Thus its separability is exactly
a selected two-axis transversality assertion, not merely a generic
property of a transported multiplicative coset.

Equivalently, put \(\sigma=s+s^{-1}\) and use the Dickson power sum
\(\mathcal D_\ell\) of
Proposition~\ref{prop:fermat-root-ancestry}.  A collision exists if
and only if there are
\[
 s\in\mu_\ell\setminus\{1\},\qquad
 \tau\in K_0^\times\cup U_0
\]
such that
\[
 \mathcal D_\ell(\sigma,1)=0,\qquad
 \mathcal D_\ell(\tau\sigma,1)=a^{-1}.                \tag{\(\mathsf{AX}_4\)}
\]
Thus the Conway value is not discarded: the remaining assertion is
that \(a^{-1}\) avoids the two axis-restrictions of the Dickson map
through every fibotomic zero.  In particular, for
\(z\in K^\times\setminus K_0^\times\) put
\[
 J(z)=\frac{\Norm_{K/K_0}(z)}
            {\operatorname{Tr}_{K/K_0}(z)^2}
      =\Phi(z^{Q-1}),
\]
the selected ancestry \(J(a)=a_{n-2}\) makes every collision satisfy
\[
 J\!\left(\mathcal D_\ell(\tau\sigma,1)\right)=a_{n-2}. \tag{\(\mathsf{AX}_5\)}
\]
This last equality is a necessary shadow of \((\mathsf{AX}_4)\), not
an exclusion by itself.

Indeed, generic separability is false even at square-root scale.
For every fixed \(s\in\mu_\ell\setminus\{1\}\), there are exactly
\(Q/2\) elements \(\lambda\in K_0^\times\) for which
\(\lambda\Phi(s)\) has absolute trace one, and hence exactly \(Q\)
elements \(y\in U\setminus\{1\}\) such that
\[
 \frac{\Phi(y)}{\Phi(s)}\in K_0^\times.              \tag{\(\mathsf{AX}_6\)}
\]
If \(\ell\leq Q\), at least one of these \(y\)'s lies outside
\(\mu_\ell\).  For \(w'=y^\ell\ne1\), the nonselected transported
coset over \(w'\) then has a type-\(N_0\) relative-trace collision.
This construction includes the range \(Q/2<\ell\leq Q\); therefore
the square-root inequality alone cannot exclude the collision of the
actual Conway coset.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
For \(t\in U\setminus\{1\}\), put \(c_t=(1+t)^{-1}\).  Then
\(\Phi(t)=c_t^2+c_t\).  Hence, with
\[
 h=c_x+c_{xv}
   =\frac{x(1+v)}{(1+x)(1+xv)},
\]
one has
\[
 \Phi(x)+\Phi(xv)=h^2+h.                              \tag{\(\mathsf{AX}_7\)}
\]
The assumptions \(x^\ell=w\ne1\) and \(v^\ell=1\) show that neither
\(x\) nor \(xv\) is one; the same holds after \(Q\)-Frobenius, so the
displayed denominators are nonzero.  Moreover \(x,v\in U\), and
inversion fixes \(h\), so \(h^q=h\) and \(h\in K\).

The two relative traces collide precisely when
\[
 0=(h^2+h)^Q+(h^2+h)
   =(h^Q+h)^2+(h^Q+h).
\]
Now \(h^Q+h\in K_0\), and the roots of \(T^2+T\) are \(0,1\).
Thus a collision is exactly \(h^Q=h\) or \(h^Q=h+1\).  Directly,
\[
 h^Q+h=\frac{N_0}{DD_Q},
\]
so multiplying
\((h^Q+h)(h^Q+h+1)\) by \((DD_Q)^2\) proves
\((\mathsf{AX}_1)\) and identifies its two factors.

Because \(\ell\) is odd, squaring permutes \(\mu_\ell\), so \(s\)
is unique.  Substitution of \(x=y/s\) and \(v=s^2\) gives
\[
 h=\frac{y(1+s)^2}{(y+s)(1+ys)}
   =\frac{\Phi(y)}{\Phi(y)+\Phi(s)}
   =\frac{\rho}{\rho+1},\qquad
 \rho=\frac{\alpha}{\beta}.                          \tag{\(\mathsf{AX}_8\)}
\]
Here \(y^\ell=w\) and \(s^\ell=1\).  The standard identity
\[
 \Phi(r)=\Phi(t)\iff(r+t)(1+rt)=0
\]
shows that \(\alpha=\beta\) would force \(y=s^{\pm1}\), contrary to
\(w\ne1\).  Thus every denominator in \((\mathsf{AX}_8)\) is nonzero.
It follows that
\[
 h^Q=h\iff\rho^Q=\rho\iff\rho\in K_0^\times,
\]
whereas
\[
 h^Q=h+1
 \iff \frac{\rho^Q}{\rho^Q+1}=\frac1{\rho+1}
 \iff \rho^{Q+1}=1
 \iff \rho\in U_0.
\]
Since
\(K_0^\times\cap U_0\) has order
\(\gcd(Q-1,Q+1)=1\), and \(\rho\ne1\), these alternatives are
disjoint.  Centering an unordered pair of distinct elements of
\(u_0\mu_\ell\) reverses the construction uniquely up to replacing
\(s\) by \(s^{-1}\).  This proves \((\mathsf{AX}_2)\)--
\((\mathsf{AX}_3)\).

Since \(\Phi(t)^{-1}=t+t^{-1}\), take
\(\tau=\beta/\alpha=\rho^{-1}\).  Both axes are closed under
inversion, and
\[
 y+y^{-1}=\tau(s+s^{-1})=\tau\sigma.
\]
The Dickson power-sum identity and \(y^\ell=w\), \(s^\ell=1\) give
\((\mathsf{AX}_4)\).  Conversely, suppose \((\mathsf{AX}_4)\) holds
and let \(y,y^{-1}\) be the roots of
\[
 X^2+\tau\sigma X+1.
\]
Then
\[
 y^\ell+y^{-\ell}=a^{-1}=w+w^{-1},
\]
so \(y^\ell\in\{w,w^{-1}\}\).  The element \(y\) cannot lie in \(K\),
because then \(y^\ell\in K\), whereas
\(w\in U\setminus\{1\}\) and \(U\cap K^\times=\{1\}\).  As the
quadratic has coefficients in \(K\), it follows that \(y^q=y^{-1}\),
so \(y\in U\); replace \(y\) by \(y^{-1}\) if necessary to arrange
\(y^\ell=w\).  Now
\[
 \frac{\Phi(y)}{\Phi(s)}=\tau^{-1}
 \in K_0^\times\cup U_0,
\]
and \((\mathsf{AX}_2)\) gives a collision.  Finally,
\(J(z^{-1})=J(z)\), while the Conway trace and norm recursion gives
\(J(a)=a_{n-2}\), proving \((\mathsf{AX}_5)\).

For the final assertion fix \(s\ne1\) and write \(\beta=\Phi(s)\).
The element \(\beta\) has absolute trace one.  Therefore
\(b_0=\beta^Q+\beta\) is nonzero: if \(\beta\in K_0\), its absolute
trace from the quadratic extension \(K/K_0\) would vanish.  For
\(\lambda\in K_0\), transitivity of trace gives
\[
 \operatorname{Tr}_{K/\F_2}(\lambda\beta)
 =\operatorname{Tr}_{K_0/\F_2}(\lambda b_0).
\]
This nonzero \(\F_2\)-linear functional takes the value one on exactly
\(Q/2\) elements of \(K_0\), none of them zero.  Every trace-one
element of \(K\) has exactly the inverse pair of lifts under
\(\Phi:U\setminus\{1\}\to K\), so these scalars produce exactly
\(Q\) values of \(y\) satisfying \((\mathsf{AX}_6)\).  At most
\(\ell-1\) of them lie in \(\mu_\ell\).  If \(\ell\leq Q\), choose
one outside, put \(w'=y^\ell\), and center the pair \(y/s,ys\).
It consists of distinct members of the same \(\mu_\ell\)-coset and
has a collision by \((\mathsf{AX}_2)\), completing the construction.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[the reverse-Dickson divisor is a transported Kummer coset]
\label{prop:fermat-coset-semiconjugacy}
Retain Proposition~\ref{prop:fermat-trace-divisor}, put \(N=q+1\), and
write
\[
 U=\ker\!\left(\Norm_{\F_{q^2}/\F_q}\right)=\mu_N.
\]
For odd \(r\), define
\[
 T_r(Z)=\mathcal D_r(1,Z),\qquad
 F_r(Z)=\frac{Z^r}{T_r(Z)^2},\qquad
 \Phi(u)=\frac{u}{(u+1)^2}.
\]
Then \(\Phi\) induces a bijection
\[
 (U\setminus\{1\})/(u\sim u^{-1})
 \xrightarrow{\;\sim\;}
 \{z\in\F_q:\operatorname{Tr}_{\F_q/\F_2}(z)=1\},       \tag{\(\mathsf{KC}_1\)}
\]
and one has the semiconjugacy
\[
 T_r(\Phi(u))=\frac{1+u^r}{(1+u)^r},\qquad
 F_r(\Phi(u))=\Phi(u^r).                               \tag{\(\mathsf{KC}_2\)}
\]
Consequently
\[
 H_m(Z)=T_N(Z)
 =\prod_{\substack{\{u,u^{-1}\}\\u\in U\setminus\{1\}}}
   (Z+\Phi(u)).                                       \tag{\(\mathsf{KC}_3\)}
\]

For the selected Conway point put
\[
 w=\frac{c_n+1}{c_n}\in U,\qquad a=\Phi(w)=a_{n-1}.
\]
Failure at \(\ell\) is equivalent to \(w\in U^\ell\).  If
\(u_0^\ell=w\), then
\[
 P_{\ell,a}(Z)
 =Z^\ell+aT_\ell(Z)^2
 =\prod_{v\in\mu_\ell}\bigl(Z+\Phi(u_0v)\bigr).
                                                               \tag{\(\mathsf{KC}_4\)}
\]
Thus the reverse-Dickson fibre is exactly one multiplicative
\(\mu_\ell\)-coset transported through \(\Phi\).  Furthermore
\[
 T_\ell\mid H_m,\qquad
 \gcd(\mathcal P_{\ell,n},T_\ell)=1,
\]
so failure gives the sharper factor statement
\[
 \mathcal P_{\ell,n}(Z)T_\ell(Z)\mid H_m(Z).           \tag{\(\mathsf{KC}_5\)}
\]
There are exactly
\[
 \frac{N/\ell-1}{2}
\]
distinct trace-one values \(\Phi(w)\) with
\(w\in U^\ell\setminus\{1\}\), each realizing the same transported
coset factorization.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
For \(u\in U\setminus\{1\}\), put \(c=(u+1)^{-1}\).  Since
\(u^q=u^{-1}\),
\[
 c^q=c+1,\qquad c^2+c=\Phi(u).
\]
The Artin--Schreier trace criterion gives
\(\operatorname{Tr}_{\F_q/\F_2}(\Phi(u))=1\).  Direct simplification
also gives
\[
 \Phi(u)=\Phi(v)\iff(u+v)(1+uv)=0,
\]
so the fibres are precisely inverse pairs.  Both sides of
\((\mathsf{KC}_1)\) have \(q/2\) elements.

The roots of \(X^2+X+\Phi(u)\) are
\((1+u)^{-1}\) and \(u(1+u)^{-1}\).  The Dickson power-sum identity
therefore proves the first formula in \((\mathsf{KC}_2)\), and the
second follows on squaring the denominator.  Taking \(r=N\) shows
that the monic degree-\(q/2\) polynomials \(T_N\) and \(H_m\) have
the same roots, proving \((\mathsf{KC}_3)\).

One has \(w=c_n^{q-1}\).  In the coprime decomposition
\(\F_{q^2}^{\times}\simeq\F_q^\times\times U\), the
\(\ell\)-power map is an automorphism on \(\F_q^\times\).  Hence
\(c_n\) is an \(\ell\)-th power exactly when \(w\in U^\ell\), by
Proposition~\ref{prop:fermat-root-ancestry}.
For \(v\in\mu_\ell\), semiconjugacy gives
\[
 F_\ell(\Phi(u_0v))=\Phi((u_0v)^\ell)=\Phi(w)=a.
\]
These \(\ell\) values are distinct.  Indeed, an inverse collision
inside \(u_0\mu_\ell\) would imply \(w^2=1\), hence \(w=1\) because
\(|U|\) is odd; but \(w=1\) is impossible.  The monic degree-\(\ell\)
polynomial \(P_{\ell,a}\) therefore has precisely the roots in
\((\mathsf{KC}_4)\).

The same argument on \(\mu_\ell\setminus\{1\}\) gives
\(T_\ell\mid T_N\).  Also
\[
 P_{\ell,a}\equiv Z^\ell\pmod{T_\ell},\qquad T_\ell(0)=1,
\]
and the same congruence holds for every Frobenius conjugate.  This
proves coprimality and \((\mathsf{KC}_5)\).  Finally the image
\(U^\ell\) has order \(N/\ell\); its nonidentity elements form
\((N/\ell-1)/2\) inverse pairs, and \((\mathsf{KC}_1)\) proves the
count.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[exact fibotomic support of a hypothetical failure]
\label{rem:fermat-coset-support}
Use the fibotomic polynomials \(\mathfrak F_s\) of
Proposition~\ref{prop:fermat-fibotomic}.  Put
\[
 e=v_\ell(N),\qquad
 \delta=\ord(w)=\ell^f\delta_0,\qquad
 (\ell,\delta_0)=1.
\]
Under failure \(0\leq f\leq e-1\).  If \(f=0\), the coset
\(u_0\mu_\ell\) contains exactly one element of order \(\delta\) and
\(\ell-1\) elements of order \(\ell\delta\).  Accordingly
\(\mathcal P_{\ell,n}\) has exactly one degree-\(m\) irreducible factor
in \(\mathfrak F_\delta\) and exactly \(\ell-1\) in
\(\mathfrak F_{\ell\delta}\).  If \(f\geq1\), every element of the
coset has order \(\ell\delta\), and all \(\ell\) factors lie in
\(\mathfrak F_{\ell\delta}\).  This includes the cases
\(v_\ell(N)>1\).

These support constraints do not close the arm.  Every trace-one
element of \(\F_q\) has absolute degree \(m\): if it lay in a proper
subfield \(\F_{2^d}\), then \(m/d\) would be even and its absolute
trace would vanish.  Hence the only resulting capacity inequality is
\[
 \varphi(\delta)\geq2m.
\]
It is automatic, because \(\delta>1\) divides \(2^m+1\), while \(m\)
is a power of two; therefore \(\ord_\delta(2)=2m\).
The full divisor, derivative, Hasse-derivative, Newton-sum, coefficient,
and fibotomic-support packages are thus realized by transported
nonselected cosets.  Excluding the selected one still requires the
recursive Conway ancestry.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[the reverse-Dickson gap is selected but not yet excluded]
\label{rem:fermat-trace-gap}
Put \(e=v_2(\ell-1)\).  For \(e\geq2\), substitution
\(X=W+W^{-1}\) gives
\[
 \mathcal D_\ell(X,1)+X
 =(W^{\ell-1}+1)(W+W^{-\ell}).
\]
At \(W=1\) the two factors vanish to orders \(2^e\) and \(2\), while
\(X\) has order \(2\).  Therefore
\[
 \mathcal D_\ell(X,1)\equiv X
 \pmod{X^{2^{e-1}+1}}.                                 \tag{\(\mathsf{TG}\)}
\]
Every prime divisor of the Fermat number \(F_n=q+1\) has
\(e\geq n+2\).  Hence
\[
 \begin{split}
 P_{\ell,a}(Z)
 &=Z^\ell+aZ^{\ell-1}
   +\text{terms of degree at most }\ell-(2m+1),\\
 \mathcal P_{\ell,n}(Z)
 &=Z^{m(\ell-1)}A_{n-1}(Z)
   +\text{terms of degree at most }m\ell-(2m+1).
 \end{split}
                                                               \tag{\(\mathsf{TG}_1\)}
\]
This long leading gap still does not contradict
\((\mathsf{TD}_1)\).  For \(n>2\), every nontrivial complementary
divisor \(d\mid F_n\) satisfies \(d\equiv1\pmod{4m}\), hence
\(d\geq4m+1\) and \(m\ell<q/4\).  A hypothetical quotient
\(H_m/\mathcal P_{\ell,n}\) is therefore long enough to absorb the
leading gap.  The divisor criterion retains the actual selected
polynomial \(A_{n-1}\); its elementary degree and coefficient
consequences alone do not decide it.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Elliptic and generalized-Jacobian reformulations}

\begin{proposition}[supersingular elliptic form of the Conway transition]
\label{prop:fermat-elliptic}
For $i\geq0$ put
\[
 u_i:=\frac{c_i+1}{c_i}.
\]
Fix $n\geq1$, set $Q=2^{2^{n-1}}$, $v=u_{n-1}$ and $u=u_n$.
Then the exact Conway recursion is
\[
 \frac{u}{(u+1)^2}=\frac{v}{(v+1)^3},                    \tag{\(\mathsf E\)}
\]
or equivalently
\[
 u(v+1)^3=v(u+1)^2,
 \qquad
 u+u^{-1}=v^2+v+1+v^{-1}.
\]
The normalization of this correspondence is the fixed supersingular
elliptic curve over $\F_2$
\[
 \mathcal E:\qquad y^2+y=x^3+x^2.                       \tag{\(\mathsf W\)}
\]
The maps to the $v$- and $u$-lines have degrees two and three,
respectively; in particular the transition has no rational section
recovering $u$ from $v$.

On the original $(u,v)$ correspondence, the rational map
\[
 \mathcal A(u,v)=\left(\frac{u+v}{uv+1},v^{-1}\right)
\]
extends to an automorphism of order four, with
\[
 \mathcal A^2(u,v)=(u^{-1},v).
\]
For the distinguished Conway point $P_n=(u_n,u_{n-1})$ one has
\[
 \Frob_Q(P_n)=\mathcal A(P_n)
 \quad\hbox{or}\quad
 \mathcal A^{-1}(P_n).                                   \tag{\(\mathsf Q\)}
\]
Thus, at \(P_n\), relative Frobenius is one of the two quarter-turns
\(\mathcal A^{\pm1}\) on the elliptic correspondence.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Write $a_i=\prod_{j\leq i}c_j$.  The identities
\[
 a_{n-1}=\frac{u}{(u+1)^2},\qquad
 a_{n-1}=a_{n-2}c_{n-1}
 =\frac{v}{(v+1)^2}\frac1{v+1}
\]
give \((\mathsf E)\); inversion gives the second displayed form.

Put $U=u+1$, $V=v+1$, and blow up the affine singularity
\((U,V)=(0,0)\) by
$X=U/V$, $Y=V$.  After division by $V^2$ the equation becomes
\[
 XY^2+(X^2+1)Y+X^2=0.
\]
Its homogenization
\[
 H=XY^2+(X^2+Z^2)Y+X^2Z
\]
has partial derivatives $(Y^2,X^2+Z^2,X^2)$ and is therefore smooth.
The point $[1:1:0]$ is a flex.  The linear change
\[
 (x,y,z)=(Z,X,X+Y+Z)
\]
takes $H=0$ to
$y^2z+yz^2=x^3+x^2z$, which is \((\mathsf W)\).  This curve has
five \(\F_2\)-points, hence trace \(-2\), and is supersingular.  The original
equation has degrees two in $u$ and three in $v$, proving the degrees
of the two projections.  A rational section of the nontrivial
degree-two finite cover would induce a retraction of its degree-two
function-field extension, impossible for a proper finite field
extension.

Direct substitution in \((\mathsf E)\) gives
\[
 \frac{\mathcal A_u}{(\mathcal A_u+1)^2}
 =v\frac{u}{(u+1)^2}
 =\frac{v^{-1}}{(v^{-1}+1)^3},
\]
so $\mathcal A$ preserves the correspondence.  A second substitution
gives $\mathcal A^2(u,v)=(u^{-1},v)$, proving the order assertion.
Thus this birational self-map and its birational inverse extend over
the smooth projective normalization.

Finally $v^Q=v^{-1}$ and
\[
 \left(\frac{u}{(u+1)^2}\right)^Q
 =\left(\frac{v}{(v+1)^3}\right)^Q
 =v\frac{u}{(u+1)^2}.
\]
The fibres of $z\mapsto z/(z+1)^2$ are the inverse-pairs
$\{z,z^{-1}\}$.  The first coordinate of $\Frob_Q(P_n)$ is therefore
either $\mathcal A_u(P_n)$ or its inverse, while the second coordinate
is $v^{-1}$, proving \((\mathsf Q)\).
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[new-prime character neutrality and the exact Kummer target]
\label{prop:fermat-kummer}
Retain Proposition~\ref{prop:fermat-elliptic} and let
$\ell$ be a prime divisor of $Q^2+1=F_n$.  If
$\chi:E_n^\times=\F_{Q^4}^\times\to\mu_\ell$ has exact order
$\ell$, and
\[
 \alpha=\chi(u),\qquad \beta=\chi(u+1),
\]
then
\[
 \alpha=\beta^2,\qquad \beta^{Q^2}=\beta^{-1}.           \tag{\(\mathsf K\)}
\]
The second identity is tautological because
$Q^2\equiv-1\pmod\ell$; in particular the exact lower transition and
the quarter-turn Frobenius symmetry do not by themselves exclude
$\beta=1$.

The following are equivalent:
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)}]
\item the order $\delta_n$ contains the full $\ell$-primary part of
      $F_n$;
\item $u_n\notin U_n^\ell$, where
      $U_n=\ker\Norm_{E_n/E_{n-1}}$;
\item $c_n\notin(E_n^\times)^\ell$;
\item $X^\ell-c_n$ is irreducible over $E_n$.
\end{enumerate}
Consequently the all-level Conway--Fermat theorem is exactly the
nontriviality, for every $\ell\mid F_n$, of this new Kummer symbol at
the distinguished point of \((\mathsf W)\).  No lower-field
multiplicative character can supply its value.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The lower field has order $Q^2$, and
$\gcd(\ell,Q^2-1)=1$, so $\chi$ is trivial on all of
$E_{n-1}^\times$, in particular on every expression in $v$.  Applying
$\chi$ to \((\mathsf E)\) gives $\alpha=\beta^2$.  Since
$u^{Q^2}=u^{-1}$,
\[
 (u+1)^{Q^2}=u^{-1}+1=\frac{u+1}{u},
\]
which gives the other identity in \((\mathsf K)\).  It reduces to
$\beta^{-1}=\beta^{-1}$ modulo $\ell$.

In the cyclic group $U_n$ of order $F_n$, nonmembership in
$U_n^\ell$ is equivalent to having the full $\ell$-primary order.
Here
\[
 U_n^\ell=U_n\cap(E_n^\times)^\ell.                    \tag{\(\mathsf K_0\)}
\]
Indeed, if \(u=x^\ell\in U_n\), then
\(\Norm_{E_n/E_{n-1}}(x)^\ell=1\); because
\(\gcd(\ell,Q^2-1)=1\), this forces \(\Norm(x)=1\), so \(x\in U_n\).
The reverse inclusion is immediate.
Moreover \((\mathsf E)\) lies in the lower field, hence is an
$\ell$-th power in $E_n$.  In
$E_n^\times/(E_n^\times)^\ell$ it therefore says
\[
 [u]=2[u+1].
\]
Since $\ell$ is odd, $u$ is an $\ell$-th power exactly when $u+1$ is.
But $u+1=c_n^{-1}$, proving the equivalence of the first three
assertions.  Finally $\mu_\ell\subset E_n$, so the prime-degree Kummer
criterion proves the equivalence with irreducibility of
$X^\ell-c_n$.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[four-point saturation of a Fermat failure]
\label{prop:fermat-four-plane}
Fix \(n\geq2\), put

\[
 c=c_n,\qquad d=c_{n-1},\qquad b=a_{n-2},\qquad
 Q=|E_{n-2}|=2^{2^{n-1}}.
\]

Then, for one \(\epsilon\in\F_2\),

\[
 c^Q=c+d+\epsilon,\qquad c^{Q^2}=c+1.              \tag{\(\mathsf{FP}_1\)}
\]

Consequently, if \(\ell\mid F_n\) and \(c\) is an \(\ell\)-th power
in \(E_n\), the complete affine plane

\[
 c+\F_2+\F_2d=\{c,c+1,c+d,c+d+1\}                 \tag{\(\mathsf{FP}_2\)}
\]

consists of \(\ell\)-th powers.  Its product is nevertheless the
old-field element

\[
 \Norm_{E_n/E_{n-2}}(c)=b^3,                        \tag{\(\mathsf{FP}_3\)}
\]

which is automatically an \(\ell\)-th power.  Thus the exact four-point
saturation and its norm are compatible with failure; they do not supply
the missing nonresidue.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The tower equations give

\[
 d^2+d=b,\qquad c^2+c=bd=d^3+d^2,\qquad d^Q=d+1.
\]

For \(s=c^Q+c\), subtraction of the equation for \(c\) from its
\(Q\)-th power gives \(s^2+s=b\).  Hence
\(s\in\{d,d+1\}\), proving the first identity in
\((\mathsf{FP}_1)\); applying \(Q\)-Frobenius once more gives the
second.  Frobenius preserves the subgroup of \(\ell\)-th powers, so
one power value forces all four values in \((\mathsf{FP}_2)\).
Finally,

\[
 c(c+1)=bd,\qquad
 (c+d)(c+d+1)=b(d+1),
\]

and their product is \(b^2d(d+1)=b^3\).  Since
\(\ell\mid Q^2+1\) is coprime to \(|E_{n-2}^\times|=Q-1\), every
old-field element is an \(\ell\)-th power in \(E_n\).
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[CM form and the Kummer-cover obstruction]
\label{prop:fermat-elliptic-kummer}
On \(\mathcal E:y^2+y=x^3+x^2\), let \(O\) be the origin
and \(T=(0,0)\).  Then
\(\mathcal E(\F_2)=\langle T\rangle\cong\mathbb Z/5\mathbb Z\), with
\[
 -T=(0,1),\qquad 2T=(1,1),\qquad -2T=(1,0).
\]
On the normalized Conway correspondence, the function
\(\mathfrak c=1/(u+1)\) is
\[
 \mathfrak c=\frac{x}{x^2+x+y},\qquad
 \operatorname{div}(\mathfrak c)=[-T]+2[O]-3[-2T].     \tag{\(\mathsf{EC}_1\)}
\]
The automorphism \(\mathcal A\) satisfies
\[
 \mathcal A^2(P)=T-P,\qquad
 \mathfrak c(\mathcal A^2P)=\mathfrak c(P)+1.          \tag{\(\mathsf{EC}_2\)}
\]
If \(\pi\) is \(2\)-power Frobenius and \(i=\pi+1\), then
\(i^2=-1\), \(i(T)=2T\), and, after possibly replacing
\(\mathcal A\) by its inverse,
\[
 \mathcal A=\tau_{2T}\circ i.
\]
Both orientations fix \(R_0=-2T\).

For \(n\geq4\), set
\[
 Q=2^{2^{n-1}},\qquad R=\sqrt Q=2^{2^{n-2}},
\]
and let \(P_n\) be the distinguished Conway point.  If
\(X_n=P_n-R_0\), then
\[
 \Frob_Q(X_n)=\pm iX_n,\qquad
 X_n\in\ker([R]\mp i).                                 \tag{\(\mathsf{EC}_3\)}
\]
Each kernel is cyclic of order \(R^2+1=Q+1\).

For every prime \(\ell\mid Q^2+1\), the Kummer cover
\[
 \mathcal C_\ell:\qquad z^\ell=\mathfrak c
\]
is geometrically connected, ramified exactly above
\(-T,O,-2T\), and has genus
\[
 g(\mathcal C_\ell)=\frac{3\ell-1}{2}.                 \tag{\(\mathsf{EC}_4\)}
\]
The automorphism \(\mathcal A\) does not lift to
\(\mathcal C_\ell\).  Hence the quarter-turn on the base supplies no
action on the Kummer fibre above \(P_n\); the surviving Conway
condition is exactly
\[
 \mathfrak c(P_n)=c_n\notin
 \bigl(\F_{Q^4}^{\times}\bigr)^\ell.                   \tag{\(\mathsf{EC}_5\)}
\]
It is the Artin-symbol problem for one distinguished fibre of this
ramified cover, not a consequence of the CM action on \(\mathcal E\).
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
In the blow-up coordinates of
Proposition~\ref{prop:fermat-elliptic}, \(U=u+1\), \(V=v+1\),
\(X=U/V\), \(Y=V\), and
\((x:y:z)=(Z:X:X+Y+Z)\).  On \(z=1\), homogeneous representatives give
\[
 V=\frac{1+x+y}{x},\qquad U=\frac{y(1+x+y)}{x^2}.
\]
Since \(y(1+x+y)=xy+x^3+x^2=x(x^2+x+y)\), this proves the formula for
\(\mathfrak c=1/U\).  With \(d=x^2+x+y\), direct intersection gives
\[
 \operatorname{div}(x)=[T]+[-T]-2[O],\qquad
 \operatorname{div}(d)=[T]+3[-2T]-4[O].
\]
Indeed \(d=0\) gives \(y=x^2+x\), whence the curve equation gives
\(x(x+1)^3=0\), and \(d\) has pole order four at \(O\).  Subtraction
proves \((\mathsf{EC}_1)\).

The function \(v=(y+1)/x\) has pole divisor \([O]+[T]\), so the deck
involution of its degree-two map is \(J(P)=T-P\).
Since \(\mathcal A^2\) fixes \(v\) and is nontrivial, it equals \(J\).
Also \(\mathcal A^2\) sends \(u\) to \(u^{-1}\), and therefore
\[
 \mathfrak c(\mathcal A^2P)
 =\frac1{u(P)^{-1}+1}
 =\frac{u(P)}{u(P)+1}
 =\mathfrak c(P)+1,
\]
proving \((\mathsf{EC}_2)\).

The Frobenius polynomial of \(\mathcal E/\F_2\) is
\(\pi^2+2\pi+2=0\).  Thus \(i=\pi+1\) satisfies \(i^2=-1\), and it
acts on \(\mathcal E(\F_2)\) as multiplication by two.
Write \(\mathcal A=\tau_S\circ\alpha\), with \(\alpha(O)=O\).
As \(\mathcal A\) is defined over \(\F_2\), \(\alpha\) commutes with
\(\pi\).  The centralizer of \(\pi\) in the supersingular endomorphism
algebra is \(\mathbb Q(i)\), whose integral units are
\(\{\pm1,\pm i\}\).  The linear part of \(\mathcal A^2\) is
\([-1]\), so \(\alpha=\pm i\).  Comparing translation parts gives
\[
 \mathcal A=\tau_{2T}\circ i
 \quad\text{or}\quad
 \mathcal A=\tau_{-T}\circ(-i);
\]
these are inverse and both fix \(R_0=-2T\).

For \(n\geq4\), \(2^{n-1}\) is divisible by eight.  Since
\(\pi^8=[16]\),
\[
 \pi^{2^{n-1}}=[2^{2^{n-2}}]=[R].
\]
Translation of \(R_0\) to the origin makes
\(\mathcal A^{\pm1}\) the linear maps \(\pm i\), proving
\((\mathsf{EC}_3)\).  The separable endomorphisms \([R]\mp i\) have
degree \(R^2+1\).  For every odd prime \(p\), the Tate module
\(T_p(\mathcal E)\) is free of rank one over \(\mathbb Z_p[i]\)
(the polynomial \(X^2+1\) is separable away from \(2\)).  Hence the
\(p\)-primary kernel is the additive quotient
\(\mathbb Z_p[i]/(R\mp i)\).  The matrix of \(R\mp i\) over
\(\mathbb Z_p\) has entries with gcd one and determinant \(R^2+1\),
so its first Smith invariant is one.  Combining the odd primary parts
shows that the geometric kernel is cyclic of order \(R^2+1\).

Every prime \(\ell\mid Q^2+1\) is coprime to \(15\).  Thus the
valuations \(1,2,-3\) in \((\mathsf{EC}_1)\) are nonzero modulo
\(\ell\).  Kummer theory makes \(z^\ell=\mathfrak c\) geometrically
connected and totally ramified at exactly those three points.
Riemann--Hurwitz gives
\[
 2g(\mathcal C_\ell)-2=3(\ell-1),
\]
which proves \((\mathsf{EC}_4)\).

It remains to prove non-lifting.  Choose
\(\mathcal A=\tau_{2T}\circ i\), put
\(B_j=\mathcal A^j(O)\), and retain \(R_0=-2T\).  Then
\[
 (B_0,B_1,B_2,B_3)=(O,2T,T,-T).
\]
For \(D_j=\operatorname{div}(\mathfrak c\circ\mathcal A^{-j})\),
pullback gives
\[
 D_j=2[B_j]+[B_{j+3}]-3[R_0].                          \tag{\(\mathsf{EC}_6\)}
\]
If \(\sum_{j=0}^3r_jD_j\equiv0\pmod\ell\), comparison at the four
\(B_j\)'s gives \(2r_j+r_{j+1}=0\) cyclically.  Iteration gives
\(15r_0=0\), and \(\ell\nmid15\) forces every \(r_j=0\).  Hence
\[
 \mathfrak c,\quad\mathfrak c\circ\mathcal A,\quad
 \mathfrak c\circ\mathcal A^2,\quad
 \mathfrak c\circ\mathcal A^3
\]
are linearly independent in
\[
 \overline{\F}_2(\mathcal E)^\times/
 \overline{\F}_2(\mathcal E)^{\times\ell}.
\]
A lift of \(\mathcal A\) would normalize the cyclic deck group, hence
send \(z\) to \(gz^r\) for
\(r\in(\mathbb Z/\ell\mathbb Z)^\times\), and would require
\(\mathfrak c\circ\mathcal A=g^\ell\mathfrak c^r\), contradicting
this independence.

Finally, set
\[
 \chi(t)=t^{(Q^4-1)/\ell},\qquad
 \tau=\chi(\mathfrak c(P_n)).
\]
Since \(\Frob_Q^2(P_n)=\mathcal A^2(P_n)\),
\((\mathsf{EC}_2)\) gives
\(\mathfrak c(P_n)^{Q^2}=\mathfrak c(P_n)+1\).
Because \(Q^2\equiv-1\pmod\ell\), the four CM/Frobenius character
values are only
\[
 \tau,\qquad\tau^{\pm Q},\qquad\tau^{-1},\qquad
 \tau^{\mp Q}.
\]
All norm identities among them are tautological.  This proves neither
\(\tau=1\) nor \(\tau\ne1\): the desired assertion remains
\((\mathsf{EC}_5)\).
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Miller-unit and generalized-Jacobian location]
\label{prop:fermat-generalized-jacobian}
Retain Proposition~\ref{prop:fermat-elliptic-kummer}, put
\(S=-2T\), \(q=Q^2\), and \(K_n=\F_{Q^4}=\F_{q^2}\).  Then

\[
 \mathfrak c^{-1}=f_{3,S}
\]

up to a nonzero scalar, where \(f_{3,S}\) is the Miller tripling
function with divisor

\[
 \operatorname{div}(f_{3,S})=3[S]-[3S]-2[O].          \tag{\(\mathsf{GJ}_1\)}
\]

For every prime \(\ell\mid q+1\),

\[
 \#\mathcal E(K_n)=(q-1)^2,\qquad
 \mathcal E(K_n)[\ell]=0,\qquad
 [\ell]:\mathcal E(K_n)\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal E(K_n). \tag{\(\mathsf{GJ}_2\)}
\]

Let \(B=\{-T,O,-2T\}\) and \(U=\mathcal E\setminus B\).  The Kummer
class of \(\mathfrak c\) on \(U\) has nonzero tame residue vector

\[
 (1,2,-3)\pmod\ell.                                  \tag{\(\mathsf{GJ}_3\)}
\]

It is therefore ramified and does not come from an unramified
\(\mu_\ell\)-class on \(\mathcal E\).  In the generalized Jacobian

\[
 1\longrightarrow\mathbb G_m^2\longrightarrow J_B
 \longrightarrow\mathcal E\longrightarrow0,          \tag{\(\mathsf{GJ}_4\)}
\]

Hilbert 90 and \((\mathsf{GJ}_2)\) give

\[
 J_B(K_n)/\ell J_B(K_n)
 \cong(K_n^\times/K_n^{\times\ell})^2.                \tag{\(\mathsf{GJ}_5\)}
\]

Thus the residue vector has a genuinely ramified toric projection;
the ordinary \(K_n\)-rational elliptic quotient cannot detect that
projection.  Ordinary \(K_n\)-rational Tate--Lichtenbaum and formal-
group data cannot by themselves decide \((\mathsf{EC}_5)\).  This
does not assert that the ramified class evaluates nontrivially at the
distinguished point, nor does it exclude a calculation using
non-rational \(\mathcal E[\ell]\) or the full extension structure of
\(J_B\).  An elliptic completion must evaluate that
generalized-Jacobian Miller-unit residue.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The point \(T\) has order five, so \(3S=3(-2T)=-T\).  Equation
\((\mathsf{EC}_1)\) therefore gives

\[
 \operatorname{div}(\mathfrak c^{-1})
 =3[S]-[3S]-2[O],
\]

which proves \((\mathsf{GJ}_1)\).

For \(n\geq4\), the Frobenius relation used in
Proposition~\ref{prop:fermat-elliptic-kummer} gives

\[
 \pi^{2^{n+1}}=[q].
\]

This is Frobenius on \(K_n=\F_{2^{2^{n+1}}}\), so

\[
 \#\mathcal E(K_n)=\deg([q]-[1])=(q-1)^2.
\]

As \(\ell\mid q+1\) is odd, it is coprime to \(q-1\).  This proves
\((\mathsf{GJ}_2)\); multiplication by \(\ell\) is likewise an
automorphism of the characteristic-two formal group because its
linear coefficient is a unit.

The divisor \((\mathsf{EC}_1)\) gives the residue vector
\((\mathsf{GJ}_3)\), which is nonzero because
\(\ell\nmid6\).  The standard generalized-Jacobian sequence for the
three-point reduced rational modulus is \((\mathsf{GJ}_4)\).  Its
torus is split; Hilbert 90 makes the map on \(K_n\)-points surjective,
and the kernel created on passing modulo \(\ell\) vanishes because
\(\mathcal E(K_n)[\ell]=0\).  This proves \((\mathsf{GJ}_5)\) and the
stated scope of the toric obstruction.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[exact extension character and cohomological exhaustion]
\label{prop:fermat-gj-character}
Retain Proposition~\ref{prop:fermat-generalized-jacobian} and put
\[
 D_1=[-T]-[-2T],\qquad D_2=[O]-[-2T]
\]
in \(\operatorname{Div}^0(B)\).  The character lattice of the split
torus in \(J_B\) is
\[
 X^*(\mathbb G_m^2)=\operatorname{Div}^0(B)
 =\mathbb ZD_1\oplus\mathbb ZD_2,
\]
and the extension is classified, under
\(\mathcal E\cong\operatorname{Pic}^0(\mathcal E)\), by
\[
 \lambda(aD_1+bD_2)=(a+2b)T.                         \tag{\(\mathsf{GJ}_6\)}
\]
Thus its entire extension class is rational five-torsion.  Moreover
\[
 r:=\operatorname{div}(\mathfrak c)=D_1+2D_2,
 \qquad\lambda(r)=5T=O,
\]
so \(r\) defines a character \(\varphi_r:J_B\to\mathbb G_m\).  If
\(j_T:U\to J_B\) is the generalized Abel map normalized by
\(j_T(T)=0\), then
\[
 \varphi_r(j_T(P))=\mathfrak c(P).                    \tag{\(\mathsf{GJ}_7\)}
\]
For every prime \(\ell\mid q+1\), even the non-rational geometric
\(\mathcal E[\ell]\) supplies no further rational Kummer class:
\[
 H^1(K_n,\mu_\ell^2)\xrightarrow{\sim}
 H^1(K_n,J_B[\ell]),
 \qquad
 J_B(K_n)/\ell J_B(K_n)\cong
 (K_n^\times/K_n^{\times\ell})^2.                    \tag{\(\mathsf{GJ}_8\)}
\]
In torus coordinates dual to \((D_1,D_2)\), the character is
\((x,y)\mapsto xy^2\), hence the nonzero linear functional
\((x,y)\mapsto x+2y\) modulo \(\ell\).  Naturality of Kummer theory gives
\[
 \varphi_{r,*}\!\left(\delta_J(j_T(P_n))\right)
   =P_n^*[\mathfrak c]=[\mathfrak c(P_n)]
   \in K_n^\times/K_n^{\times\ell}.                  \tag{\(\mathsf{GJ}_9\)}
\]
Consequently \((\mathsf{EC}_5)\) is exactly the assertion that the
normalized Abel class of \(P_n\) avoids the hyperplane \(x+2y=0\).
Torus translation above any fixed elliptic point realizes every value
of this functional.  Hence the CM point, the full extension class, and
non-rational elliptic \(\ell\)-torsion do not force
\((\mathsf{EC}_5)\); the remaining datum is the explicit toric coordinate
of this one distinguished normalized Abel lift.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
For a reduced three-point rational modulus, the generalized-Jacobian
torus is \((\mathbb G_m^3)/\mathbb G_m\), with character lattice
\(\operatorname{Div}^0(B)\).  The standard extension-class map sends a
boundary divisor to its class in \(\operatorname{Pic}^0(\mathcal E)\).
Here
\[
 (-T)-(-2T)=T,\qquad O-(-2T)=2T,
\]
which proves \((\mathsf{GJ}_6)\).  Equation \((\mathsf{EC}_1)\) gives
\(r=D_1+2D_2\), and its image is \(T+4T=O\).  Characters of \(J_B\)
are precisely the torus characters whose pushout extension class
vanishes, so \(r\) yields \(\varphi_r\).  The generalized-Albanese
universal property gives \((\mathsf{GJ}_7)\) after normalization at
\(T\).  Indeed, \(T\notin B\) and
\(\mathfrak c(T)\in\F_2^\times=\{1\}\).

The \(K_n\)-Frobenius on \(\mathcal E\) is \([q]\), and
\(q\equiv-1\pmod\ell\).  It therefore acts as \([-1]\) on
\(\mathcal E[\ell]\).  Since \(\ell\) is odd,
\[
 \mathcal E[\ell](K_n)=0,
 \qquad
 H^1(K_n,\mathcal E[\ell])
 =\mathcal E[\ell]/(\Frob-1)\mathcal E[\ell]=0.
\]
The cohomology sequence of
\[
 0\longrightarrow\mu_\ell^2\longrightarrow J_B[\ell]
 \longrightarrow\mathcal E[\ell]\longrightarrow0
\]
then proves the first isomorphism in \((\mathsf{GJ}_8)\).  Lang's
theorem and Kummer theory prove the second.  The natural Kummer square
for \(\varphi_r\), together with \((\mathsf{GJ}_7)\), gives
\((\mathsf{GJ}_9)\).  Finally
\(\mathbb G_m^2(K_n)/\ell\to J_B(K_n)/\ell\) is an isomorphism:
surjectivity uses bijectivity of \([\ell]\) on \(\mathcal E(K_n)\),
and injectivity uses \(\mathcal E(K_n)[\ell]=0\).  The character
\((x,y)\mapsto xy^2\) is surjective on the \(\ell\)-quotient, proving
the fixed-fibre statement and the asserted exact boundary.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[explicit Rosenlicht coordinate and its reciprocity boundary]
\label{prop:fermat-rosenlicht}
Retain Proposition~\ref{prop:fermat-gj-character}, write
\(S=-2T\), \(P=P_n\), and put \(X=P-S\).  For
\(\ell\mid q+1\), let \(Z\in\mathcal E(K_n)\) be the unique point with

\[
 [\ell]Z=P-T,
 \qquad W=T+Z.                                      \tag{\(\mathsf{RC}_1\)}
\]

If \(e\ell\equiv1\pmod{5(Q+1)}\), then

\[
 Z=[e]X+[2e]T,
 \qquad W=[e]X+[2e+1]T,                             \tag{\(\mathsf{RC}_2\)}
\]

so the division point remains an explicit translate of the same cyclic
CM kernel as \(X\).  Choose a Miller function \(F=F_{\ell,Z}\) with

\[
 \operatorname{div}(F)
 =\ell[Z]-[[\ell]Z]-(\ell-1)[O].
\]

In the Rosenlicht coordinates dual to
\(D_1=[-T]-[S]\), \(D_2=[O]-[S]\), the normalized Abel Kummer class of
\(P\) is

\[
 \rho_1=\frac{F(2T)}{F(S)},
 \qquad
 \rho_2=\frac{F(2T)}{F(-T)},                       \tag{\(\mathsf{RC}_3\)}
\]

and its Miller-unit character is exactly

\[
 [\mathfrak c(P)]=[\rho_1\rho_2^2]
 \quad\hbox{in }K_n^\times/K_n^{\times\ell}.       \tag{\(\mathsf{RC}_4\)}
\]

There is also a formula avoiding the \(\ell\)-division point.  On
\(\mathcal E:y^2+y=x^3+x^2\), set

\[
 A_5=1+x+x^2+y+xy,
 \qquad B_5=1+x+xy,
 \qquad g_1=\frac{A_5}{B_5},
 \qquad g_2=\frac1{B_5}.
\]

Then

\[
 \operatorname{div}(g_1)=5D_1,
 \qquad \operatorname{div}(g_2)=5D_2,
 \qquad \mathfrak c^5=g_1g_2^2.                    \tag{\(\mathsf{RC}_5\)}
\]

If \(5\bar5\equiv1\pmod\ell\), the full normalized Abel class in
\(J_B(K_n)/\ell J_B(K_n)\) has coordinates

\[
 \bigl([g_1(P)]^{\bar5},[g_2(P)]^{\bar5}\bigr).
                                                               \tag{\(\mathsf{RC}_6\)}
\]

More explicitly, for the consecutive Conway variables
\(u=u_n\), \(v=u_{n-1}\), put

\[
 U=u+1,\qquad V=v+1,\qquad D=V^2+V+U.
\]

Then the normalized point has \(x=V/D\), \(y=U/D\), and

\[
 g_1(P)=
 \frac{D^2+D(U+V)+V^2+UV}{D^2+VD+UV},
 \qquad
 g_2(P)=\frac{D^2}{D^2+VD+UV},                       \tag{\(\mathsf{RC}_7\)}
\]

with \(g_1(P)g_2(P)^2=U^{-5}\).

These formulas compute the missing toric coordinate exactly, but do not
force it to be nonzero modulo \(\ell\).  Indeed, Weil reciprocity gives

\[
 \mathfrak c(P)
 =\mathfrak c(W)^\ell
   \frac{F(2T)^3}{F(S)F(-T)^2}.                      \tag{\(\mathsf{RC}_8\)}
\]

Applying reciprocity instead to
\(G(Y)=\mathfrak c(Y+T)\), whose divisor is
\([S]+2[-T]-3[2T]\), returns precisely the inverse identity

\[
 \frac{F(S)F(-T)^2}{F(2T)^3}
 =\frac{\mathfrak c(W)^\ell}{\mathfrak c(P)}.
                                                               \tag{\(\mathsf{RC}_9\)}
\]

Thus the boundary Miller values and the rational five-torsion splitting
exhaust the Rosenlicht coordinate, but collapsing them by the evident
reciprocity law is exactly equivalent to the original Kummer value
\([\mathfrak c(P)]\); it supplies no independent nonresidue constraint.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Since \(P=X+3T\), one has \(P-T=X+2T\).  Multiplication by \(\ell\)
is invertible both on the cyclic kernel containing \(X\) and on
\(\langle T\rangle\), which proves \((\mathsf{RC}_2)\).

Put \(h(Y)=F(Y-T)\).  Translation of the displayed divisor of \(F\)
gives

\[
 \operatorname{div}(h)=\ell[W]-[P]-(\ell-1)[T].
\]

The Rosenlicht evaluation of this principal relation against \(D_1,D_2\)
is

\[
 \frac{h(S)}{h(-T)}=\frac{F(2T)}{F(S)},
 \qquad
 \frac{h(S)}{h(O)}=\frac{F(2T)}{F(-T)},
\]

which proves \((\mathsf{RC}_3)\).  Weil reciprocity between \(h\) and
\(\mathfrak c\), using

\[
 \operatorname{div}(\mathfrak c)=[-T]+2[O]-3[S],
 \qquad \mathfrak c(T)=1,
\]

gives \((\mathsf{RC}_8)\), and hence
\((\mathsf{RC}_4)\).

Direct intersection with the Weierstrass equation, including the local
orders at the indicated five-torsion points, gives

\[
 \operatorname{div}(A_5)=5[-T]-5[O],
 \qquad
 \operatorname{div}(B_5)=5[S]-5[O].
\]

Thus \(g_1,g_2\) have the divisors asserted in
\((\mathsf{RC}_5)\).  Both sides of
\(\mathfrak c^5=g_1g_2^2\) have the same divisor and value one at
\(T\), proving the exact identity.  Since five is invertible modulo
\(\ell\), this proves \((\mathsf{RC}_6)\).  Substitution of
\(x=V/D\), \(y=U/D\) gives \((\mathsf{RC}_7)\); here
\(\mathfrak c(P)=1/U\), so its last identity is also immediate from
\((\mathsf{RC}_5)\).

Finally, the divisor of \(G\) follows by translating that of
\(\mathfrak c\).  Weil reciprocity between \(F\) and \(G\) is
\((\mathsf{RC}_9)\), proving the exact neutrality statement.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[five-point equivariant generalized-Jacobian exhaustion]
\label{prop:fermat-five-point-exhaustion}
Retain the Conway point \(P=P_n\), the quarter-turn
\(\mathcal A\), and \(S=-2T\).  Put
\[
 B_0=O,\qquad B_1=2T,\qquad B_2=T,\qquad B_3=-T,\qquad
 D=\mathcal E(\F_2)=\{S,B_0,B_1,B_2,B_3\}.
\]
For \(j\in\mathbb Z/4\mathbb Z\), set
\[
 e_j=[B_j]-[S],\qquad
 f_j=\mathfrak c\circ\mathcal A^{-j},\qquad
 r_j=\operatorname{div}(f_j)=2e_j+e_{j+3}.           \tag{\(\mathsf{FE}_1\)}
\]
Then \(D\) is \(\mathcal A\)-invariant, and the four classes \(r_j\)
form a basis of
\(\operatorname{Div}^0(D)\otimes\mathbb F_\ell\) for every prime
\(\ell\mid F_n\), \(n\geq2\).

Let \(J_D\) be the generalized Jacobian of the five-point reduced
modulus, let \(K_n=\F_{Q^4}\), where
\(Q=2^{2^{n-1}}\), and choose any
\(R\in\mathcal E(\F_{16})\setminus D\).  If \(j_R\) denotes the
generalized Abel map normalized at \(R\), then
\[
 \mathfrak c(P)\in K_n^{\times\ell}
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 [j_R(P)]=0\text{ in }J_D(K_n)/\ell J_D(K_n).          \tag{\(\mathsf{FE}_2\)}
\]
Thus even the simultaneous Rosenlicht data of the complete
quarter-turn orbit contain no obstruction beyond the original Kummer
value.

For \(n\geq3\), put \(z=[j_R(P)]\) and let
\(\sigma=\Frob_Q\).  If
\(\sigma(P)=\mathcal A^\epsilon(P)\), with
\(\epsilon\in\{1,-1\}\), then
\[
 Qz=\mathcal A_*^\epsilon z.                          \tag{\(\mathsf{FE}_3\)}
\]
Inside the split rank-four torus quotient this eigenspace is
one-dimensional over \(\mathbb F_\ell\), and evaluation by
\(\varphi_{r_0}\)
is an isomorphism from it to
\(K_n^\times/K_n^{\times\ell}\).  Under this isomorphism \(z\) maps
to \([\mathfrak c(P)]\).  Consequently the full
\(\mathcal A\)-invariant generalized Jacobian, its rational
five-torsion extension class, and the exact Frobenius quarter-turn
relation determine precisely one scalar---the scalar in
\((\mathsf{EC}_5)\)---but do not determine whether that scalar
vanishes.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The automorphism \(\mathcal A\) fixes \(S\) and cycles the other four
\(\F_2\)-points as
\[
 O\longmapsto2T\longmapsto T\longmapsto -T\longmapsto O.
\]
Equation \((\mathsf{EC}_6)\) gives
\((\mathsf{FE}_1)\).  In the basis \((e_0,e_1,e_2,e_3)\), the four
rows \(r_j\) form the circulant matrix \(2I+C\), where \(C\) is a
four-cycle.  Hence
\[
 \det(2I+C)=2^4-1=15.                                 \tag{\(\mathsf{FE}_4\)}
\]
No prime divisor of \(F_n\), \(n\geq2\), divides \(15\), proving the
basis assertion.  Notice also that the union of the supports of the
four \(f_j\) is exactly \(D\).

The Frobenius polynomial of \(\mathcal E/\F_2\) gives
\(\#\mathcal E(\F_{16})=25\), so a point
\(R\in\mathcal E(\F_{16})\setminus D\) exists.  Every \(f_j(R)\) is
a nonzero element of \(\F_{16}\); since
\(\gcd(\ell,15)=1\), it is an \(\ell\)-th power already in
\(\F_{16}\).  The five-point generalized Jacobian fits into
\[
 1\longrightarrow\mathbb G_m^4\longrightarrow J_D
 \longrightarrow\mathcal E\longrightarrow0.
\]
As in \((\mathsf{GJ}_2)\),
\(\#\mathcal E(K_n)=(Q^2-1)^2\), so multiplication by \(\ell\) is
bijective on \(\mathcal E(K_n)\).  Hilbert 90 and the absence of
rational \(\ell\)-torsion therefore give
\[
 J_D(K_n)/\ell J_D(K_n)
 \simeq (K_n^\times/K_n^{\times\ell})^4.              \tag{\(\mathsf{FE}_5\)}
\]
For \(r_j=\operatorname{div}(f_j)\), let
\(\varphi_{r_j}:J_D\to\mathbb G_m\) be the corresponding principal
boundary character.  Generalized Abel evaluation is
\[
 \varphi_{r_j}(j_R(P))=\frac{f_j(P)}{f_j(R)}
 \quad\text{in }K_n^\times/K_n^{\times\ell}.          \tag{\(\mathsf{FE}_6\)}
\]
Writing \(\sigma=\Frob_Q\), if \(\mathfrak c(P)\) is an
\(\ell\)-th power, then
\(\sigma(P)=\mathcal A^{\pm1}(P)\) and Frobenius stability of
\(K_n^{\times\ell}\) make every \(f_j(P)\) an \(\ell\)-th power.
Equations \((\mathsf{FE}_4)\)--\((\mathsf{FE}_6)\) then give
\(j_R(P)=0\) modulo \(\ell\).  The converse follows by evaluating
\(\varphi_{r_0}\), since \(f_0=\mathfrak c\).  This proves
\((\mathsf{FE}_2)\).

Suppose now that \(n\geq3\).  Then \(\sigma\) fixes \(\F_{16}\), and
hence \(R\).  Moreover
\[
 \#J_D(\F_{16})=15^4\cdot25
\]
is prime to \(\ell\).  Therefore the affine base-point correction
\([j_R(\mathcal A^\epsilon R)]\) vanishes modulo \(\ell\), and
\[
 \sigma j_R(P)=j_R(\sigma P)
 =\mathcal A_*^\epsilon j_R(P)+j_R(\mathcal A^\epsilon R)
\]
reduces to \((\mathsf{FE}_3)\).  On the right side of
\((\mathsf{FE}_5)\), \(\sigma\) acts on the scalar Kummer quotient
as multiplication by \(Q\), while \(\mathcal A_*^\epsilon\) acts on
the rank-four cocharacter lattice as a four-cycle.  Since
\(Q^2\equiv-1\pmod\ell\), \(Q\) has exact order four modulo
\(\ell\).  The \(Q\)-eigenspace of that four-cycle is consequently
one-dimensional.

Finally, if \(\varphi_{r_0}\) vanished on a vector in this eigenspace, its four
\(\mathcal A^*\)-translates \(r_j\) would all vanish there.  The basis
assertion would force the vector to be zero.  Thus \(\varphi_{r_0}\) is nonzero,
and hence an isomorphism, on the one-dimensional eigenspace.  Its
value is \([f_0(P)/f_0(R)]=[\mathfrak c(P)]\), completing the proof.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[equivariant Kummer countermodels at the actual ordinal degree]
\label{prop:fermat-equivariant-kummer-countermodel}
Retain the elliptic curve \(\mathcal E\), its order-four automorphism
\(\mathcal A\), the five-point boundary
\(D=\mathcal E(\F_2)\), and the four functions
\[
 f_j=\mathfrak c\circ\mathcal A^{-j},
 \qquad j\in\mathbb Z/4\mathbb Z,
\]
from Proposition~\ref{prop:fermat-five-point-exhaustion}.  Let
\(\ell\nmid15\) be an odd prime, and let \(Y_\ell\) be the
normalization of the Kummer cover
\[
 z_j^\ell=f_j,\qquad j=0,1,2,3.                     \tag{\(\mathsf{EK}_1\)}
\]
Then \(Y_\ell/\mathcal E\) is geometrically connected of degree
\(\ell^4\), is ramified exactly over the five points of \(D\), and
has
\[
 2g(Y_\ell)-2=5\ell^4-5\ell^3.                     \tag{\(\mathsf{EK}_2\)}
\]
The order-four automorphism lifts as
\[
 \widetilde{\mathcal A}(P,z_0,z_1,z_2,z_3)
 =(\mathcal A P,z_3,z_0,z_1,z_2).                  \tag{\(\mathsf{EK}_3\)}
\]

Let \(Q\) be a power of two with \(\ell\mid Q^2+1\).  If
\[
 Q+1-2g(Y_\ell)\sqrt Q>5\ell^3,                    \tag{\(\mathsf{EK}_4\)}
\]
then there exists
\(P\in\mathcal E(\F_{Q^4})\setminus D\) such that
\[
 \Frob_Q(P)=\mathcal A(P),\qquad
 f_j(P)\in\F_{Q^4}^{\times\ell}\quad(0\leq j<4).    \tag{\(\mathsf{EK}_5\)}
\]
In particular \(\mathfrak c(P)\) is an \(\ell\)-th power.

Condition \((\mathsf{EK}_4)\) holds at an actual Conway--Fermat field
size:
\[
 Q=2^{256},\qquad
 \ell=2424833\mid F_9=2^{512}+1.                   \tag{\(\mathsf{EK}_6\)}
\]
Thus the exact field degree, elliptic transition, quarter-turn
Frobenius relation, and complete four-function Kummer orbit admit
structural failure points.  This is not a counterexample to
\(\delta_9=F_9\): the constructed point is not asserted to be the one
selected by the full Conway ancestry.  It proves that an
ancestry-insensitive CM, isogeny, generalized-Jacobian, or reciprocity
argument cannot force the required nonresidue.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The divisor vectors of the four functions are the rows of
\(2I+C\), where \(C\) is a four-cycle.  Their determinant is \(15\),
so they are independent modulo \(\ell\).  Kummer theory over the
geometric function field therefore makes \((\mathsf{EK}_1)\)
connected of degree \(\ell^4\).

The union of the supports of the four divisors is exactly \(D\).
At each of its five points the valuation vector is nonzero modulo
\(\ell\), so inertia has order \(\ell\) and there are
\(\ell^3\) geometric points above it.  Since the base curve has genus
one, Riemann--Hurwitz gives
\[
 2g(Y_\ell)-2
 =5\ell^4\left(1-\frac1\ell\right),
\]
which is \((\mathsf{EK}_2)\).  The identity
\[
 f_j(\mathcal A P)=f_{j-1}(P)
\]
proves that \((\mathsf{EK}_3)\) preserves the four Kummer equations
and has order four.

Twist \(Y_\ell\) over \(\F_Q\) by the cocycle sending arithmetic
\(Q\)-Frobenius to \(\widetilde{\mathcal A}\).  Its
\(\F_Q\)-points correspond to geometric points \(y\) satisfying
\[
 \Frob_Q(y)=\widetilde{\mathcal A}(y).
\]
Hasse--Weil and \((\mathsf{EK}_4)\) give more such points than the at
most \(5\ell^3\) points above the branch boundary.  Choose a
nonbranch point.  Four Frobenius iterations give
\(\Frob_Q^4(y)=y\), so all its coordinates lie in \(\F_{Q^4}\).
Projecting to \(\mathcal E\) and reading the four \(z_j\) proves
\((\mathsf{EK}_5)\).

Finally, \(2424833\) is a prime divisor of \(F_9\) and is smaller than
\(2^{22}\).  Its primality and the divisibility
\(2424833\mid 2^{512}+1\) are independently kernel-checked in the
companion Lean file.  Hence
\[
 g(Y_\ell)<3\ell^4<2^{90},\qquad
 2g(Y_\ell)\sqrt Q<2^{219},\qquad
 5\ell^3<2^{69},
\]
whereas \(Q=2^{256}\).  This proves
\((\mathsf{EK}_4)\) and \((\mathsf{EK}_6)\).
\end{proof}

When $F_n$ is prime, irreducibility and $\delta_n>1$ force
$\delta_n=F_n$, recovering Lenstra's Fermat-prime rule.  When $F_n$ is
composite, irreducibility forces only one nontrivial divisor: it does not
force every prime-power factor.  The condition $\delta_n=F_n$ is exactly
the maximal-coset-order hypothesis studied for Conway's quadratic tower
by Popovych~\cite{Popovych18}.  It is strictly weaker than primitivity of
$c_n$, which already fails at $n=2$ since
$\ord(c_2)=5\cdot17=85<255$; Popovych uses the all-level
hypothesis instead to prove that products such as $c_0c_n$ are primitive.
Wiedemann's related iterated quadratic tower
poses the analogous full-Fermat-order problem~\cite{Wiedemann88}, but
Proposition~\ref{prop:fermat-wiedemann-separation} proves that its
selected norm-one element is not Frobenius-identifiable with the
Conway element after the first levels.  Thus
the apparently easy ``singleton even implies zero'' clause already
contains a longstanding explicit maximal-coset-order problem.
Popovych verified the Conway condition only through $n=11$; the recent
analysis of Cagliero--Herman--Szechtman still retains the quotient order
as an undetermined divisor~\cite{CHS25}.  Their Euler--Lucas argument does
give the unconditional congruence
\[
 \delta_n\equiv1\pmod{2^{n+2}},\qquad
 \delta_n\geq1+2^{n+2}\quad(n\geq2),
\]
but this remains far below \(F_n\) and does not force any prescribed prime
factor of a composite \(F_n\).  In fact this congruence cannot distinguish
the selected order from any proper candidate.  For every prime
\(\ell\mid F_n\), one has \(\ord_\ell(2)=2^{n+1}\).  Since
\(\ell\equiv1\pmod8\), the element two is a square modulo \(\ell\), and
a square root of two has order \(2^{n+2}\); hence
\(\ell\equiv1\pmod{2^{n+2}}\).  It follows that every nontrivial divisor
\(d\mid F_n\) already satisfies

\[
 d\equiv1\pmod{2^{n+2}},\qquad \ord_d(2)=2^{n+1}.
\]

The newer first-zero theorem of Zhu--Wu~\cite[Theorem~4.1]{ZhuWu26}
identifies the binomial and multiplicative orders of an irreducible
quadratic from its first vanishing Lucas coefficient.  Applied here, it
recovers exactly the reverse-Dickson/Lucas criterion already isolated
above; it does not prove that the first zero is \(F_n\).  Their later
primitive-coefficient criterion assumes both that the constant term is
primitive and that \(q+1=2^m\pi\) with \(m>0\) and \(\pi\) an odd prime.
The Conway quadratic has constant term of order \((q-1)/3\) under the
full preceding hypothesis, while \(q+1=F_n\) is odd and may be
composite.  Thus that theorem does not cover the selected Conway
polynomial.

Thus the Euler--Lucas congruence is automatic from
\(1<\delta_n\mid F_n\).  No all-level proof or counterexample is presently
known.

\subsection{Comparison with the cubic arm}

These two embedded arms are instances of the same projective statement.  The
quadratic arm asks that the class of $c_n$ generate
$E_n^\times/E_{n-1}^\times$.  In the cubic arm the natural map
\[
 U_k\longrightarrow \F_{q_k^3}^{\times}/\F_{q_k}^{\times}
\]
is an isomorphism, and the increment from $C_{k-1}$ to $C_k$ asks that
the class of $\gamma_k$
generate this quotient.  Equivalently, the projective class of the
multiplication-by-$\gamma_k$ matrix generates a Singer cycle in
$\operatorname{PGL}_3(q_k)$.  The $0/1/4$ rule therefore contains a
quadratic and a cubic maximal-projective-order conjecture among its four exact
arms, rather than
two unrelated order coincidences.

\subsection{The singleton-even conjecture \texorpdfstring{\((Z)\)}{(Z)}}

The singleton-even part of the zero arm is exactly the assertion
$\delta_n=F_n$ for all $n$.  The results above identify the recursively
selected fibotomic factor and exhaust the trace, norm, elliptic, and
generalized-Jacobian data currently available without evaluating its
Kummer class.  Thus the remaining theorem is not another coarse
invariant: it is the nonvanishing of the explicit distinguished
Miller-unit value in Proposition~\ref{prop:fermat-five-point-exhaustion}.

\section{\texorpdfstring{The exceptional \(2\cdot3^k\) arm}{The exceptional 2 times 3-power arm}}\label{sec:exception}

\subsection{Why component data do not decide the excess}

$\Qset(f(p))$ alone does not determine $m_p$:
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{ccc}
\toprule
$\Qset$ & $m=4$ & $m=1$ \\
\midrule
$\{9\}$   & $p=19$,   $f=18$  & $p=73$,   $f=9$ \\
$\{81\}$  & $p=163$,  $f=162$ & $p=2593$, $f=81$ \\
$\{243\}$ & $p=1459$, $f=486$ & $p=487$,  $f=243$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

The split for $\Qset=\{9\}$ is explained by the order
criterion: $\ord(\kappa_9+1)=3^3\cdot(2^9-1)=27\cdot511=13797$ and
$511=7\cdot73$, so $73\mid\ord(\kappa_9+1)$ but
$19\nmid\ord(\kappa_9+1)$; adding $m=4$ changes the order and introduces
the factor $19$.

The lower bound of the exception column is unconditional:
$m_p\geq4$ for all $p$ with $f(p)=2\cdot3^k$
(Proposition~\ref{prop:exception}).

\emph{Conjecture.} The matching upper bound $m_p\leq4$ on that column;
its exact current-level formulation is $D'_k$ in
Conjecture~\ref{conj:dk}, not the Gaussian-period conjecture $C_k$.

\subsection{The corrected norm and current-factor target}
\label{sec:m4}

The $m=4$ translate $\kappa_{3^k}+4$ involves the nimber $4\in\F_{16}$,
which does not lie in $\F_2(\zeta)$ (as $4\nmid2\cdot3^k$); the
translate therefore lies in the degree-$4\cdot3^k$ compositum
$\F_{2^{4h}}$, where $h=3^k$.

\begin{proposition}[the corrected norm]\label{prop:m4norm}
Let $\sigma=\Frob^{2h}$ generate
$\mathrm{Gal}(\F_{2^{4h}}/\F_{2^{2h}})$. Then $\sigma(4)=5$, and with
$\omega:=\zeta^{h}=\kappa_2$,
\[
  N:=\Norm_{\F_{2^{4h}}/\F_{2^{2h}}}(\kappa_{3^k}+4)
   =(\kappa+4)(\kappa+5)
   =\kappa^2+\kappa+\omega .
\]
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
$\F_{16}\cap\F_{2^{2h}}=\F_4$ because $\gcd(4,2\cdot3^k)=2$, so
$\sigma|_{\F_{16}}$ is the nontrivial element of
$\mathrm{Gal}(\F_{16}/\F_4)$, which swaps the two roots $4,5$ of the
Artin--Schreier minimal polynomial $y^2+y+\omega$ of $4$ over $\F_4$.
(Equivalently: $2h\equiv2\pmod4$ and the Frobenius orbit of $4$ is
$4\to6\to5\to7$, so $\sigma(4)=4^{2^2}=5$.) Then
$(\kappa+4)(\kappa+5)=\kappa^2+\kappa+(4\nimmul5)$ and
$4\nimmul5=4^2+4=\omega$ by the same Artin--Schreier relation.
\end{proof}

The corrected norm collapses the $m=4$ test into the \emph{same} sparse
trinomial model $\F_2[x]/(x^{2h}+x^h+1)$ as the rest of the $3$-power
analysis: no compositum arithmetic is needed.

\begin{corollary}[in-field criterion]\label{cor:m4crit}
For a prime $p$ with $f(p)=2\cdot3^k$ and $v_p(2^{2h}-1)=1$,
write $\overline{N}=N^{2^h}$ and $M_k:=\overline{N}/N=N^{2^h-1}\in U$.
Then
\[
  m_p=4
  \iff
  p\mid\ord(M_k)
  \iff
  M_k^{(2^h+1)/p}\neq1 .
\]
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
$m_p\geq4$ is Proposition~\ref{prop:exception}, so $m_p=4$ iff
$\kappa+4$ has no $p$-th root. By Propositions~\ref{prop:power}
and~\ref{prop:norm} with $E=4h$, $f=2h$ (and
$v_p(2^{4h}-1)=v_p(2^{2h}-1)=1$ by lifting the exponent, since
$p\nmid2$), that holds iff $N^{(2^{2h}-1)/p}\neq1$. Since
$\F_{2^{2h}}^{\times}=L^{\times}\times U$ with coprime orders,
$p\mid2^h+1$, and $(2^{2h}-1)/p=(2^h-1)\cdot\bigl((2^h+1)/p\bigr)$, the
test equals $M_k^{(2^h+1)/p}\neq1$.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[exact current-level form of the exception conjecture]
\label{prop:dk-current-level}
Let $k\geq1$.  Retain the notation above, put
$q=2^h$, $n=3^{k+1}=3h$,
and write
\[
 \zeta:=\kappa_{3^k}\in K:=\F_{2^{2h}},\qquad
 \widetilde K:=\F_{2^{4h}},\qquad \beta:=\zeta+4.
\]
Thus $\zeta$ is a primitive $n$-th root of unity.  Define
\[
 N:=\Norm_{\widetilde K/K}(\beta)
   =\zeta^2+\zeta+\zeta^h,
 \qquad M_k:=N^{q-1},
 \qquad \Psi_k:=\frac{\Phi_{2\cdot3^k}(2)}3,
\]
and
\[
 R_k(Y):=\operatorname{Res}_X
 \left(X^{2h}+X^h+1,\,Y+X^h+X^2+X\right)\in\F_2[Y].
\]
Then $R_k$ is the irreducible minimal polynomial of $N$ over $\F_2$
and has degree $2h$.  The prime divisors of $\Psi_k$ are exactly the
primes $\ell\ne3$ with $\ord_\ell(2)=2\cdot3^k$, with full valuations
\[
 v_\ell(\Psi_k)=v_\ell(2^{2h}-1)=v_\ell(2^h+1).
\]
Moreover the following are equivalent:
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\alph*)}]
\item $m_\ell=4$ for every prime $\ell$ with
      $f(\ell)=2\cdot3^k$;
\item $\Psi_k\mid\ord(M_k)$;
\item $N\notin(K^\times)^\ell$ for every prime $\ell\mid\Psi_k$;
\item $R_k(X^\ell)$ is irreducible over $\F_2$ for every prime
      $\ell\mid\Psi_k$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The order identity $\ord_{3^{k+1}}(2)=2h$ makes
